Coarse homological invariants of metric spaces
Alexander Margolis

Abstract
. Inspired by group cohomology, we define several coarse topological invariants of metric spaces. We define the coarse cohomological dimension of a metric space, and demonstrate that if G G is a countable group, then the coarse cohomological dimension of G G as a metric space coincides with the cohomological dimension of G G as a group whenever the latter is finite. Extending a result of Sauer, it is shown that coarse cohomological dimension is monotone under coarse embeddings, and hence is invariant under coarse equivalence. We characterise unbounded quasi-trees as quasi-geodesic metric spaces of coarse cohomological dimension one. A classical theorem of Hopf and Freudenthal states that if G G is a finitely generated group, then the number of ends of G G is either 0 0 , 1 1 , 2 2 or ∞ \infty . We prove a higher-dimensional analogue of this result, showing that if 𝔽 \mathbb{F} is a field, G G is countable, and H k ​ ( G , 𝔽 ​ G ) = 0 H^{k}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=0 for k < n k<n , then dim H n ​ ( G , 𝔽 ​ G ) = 0 , 1 \dim H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=0,1 or ∞ \infty , significantly extending a result of Farrell from 1975. Moreover, in the case dim H n ​ ( G , 𝔽 ​ G ) = 1 \dim H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=1 , then G G must be a coarse Poincaré duality group. We prove an analogous result for metric spaces.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
20J05, 20J06, 20F65, 51F30

1.
Introduction

In this article, we define several coarse homological invariants of metric spaces, inspired by and analogous to concepts in group cohomology; see Table
1
. We first discuss an application of this machinery.
A foundational result in the theory of ends, proven independently by Freudenthal and Hopf, states that the number of ends of a finitely generated group is equal to either
0,1,2
or
\infty
.
We recall the number of ends of an infinite group
G
can be characterised cohomologically as the dimension of
H^{1}(G,\mathbb{F}_{2}G)
minus one. We can thus reformulate the Freudenthal–Hopf theorem as follows:

Theorem 1.1

(
[
Hop44
,
Fre45
]
)
.

If
G
is a finitely generated group, then exactly one of the following holds:

(1)

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}H^{1}(G,\mathbb{F}_{2}G)=0
.

(2)

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}H^{1}(G,\mathbb{F}_{2}G)=1
, in which case
G
is virtually infinite cyclic.

(3)

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}H^{1}(G,\mathbb{F}_{2}G)=\infty
.

We prove the following higher-dimensional analogue of Theorem
1.1
, significantly extending work of Farrell
[
Far75
]
.

Theorem A

(Theorem
12.13
)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbb{F}
be a field and let
G
be a countable group. If
H^{k}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=0
for
k<n
, then exactly one of the following holds:

(1)

\dim_{\mathbb{F}}H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=0
.

(2)

\dim_{\mathbb{F}}H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=1
, in which case
G
is a coarse Poincaré duality group of dimension
n
over
\mathbb{F}
.

(3)

\dim_{\mathbb{F}}H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=\infty
.

Moreover, if
H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)
has countable dimension and a finite-dimensional
G
-invariant subspace, then
\dim_{\mathbb{F}}H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=1
.

Poincaré duality groups
were introduced by Johnson–Wall and Bieri
[
JW72
,
Bie72
]
, and are an important and well-studied class of groups that possess a form of duality between group homology and cohomology. In particular, fundamental groups of closed aspherical manifolds are Poincaré duality groups.

Coarse Poincaré duality groups of dimension
n
over a ring
R
, henceforth called
coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
groups, were introduced by Kapovich–Kleiner
[
KK05
]
. They are a generalisation of Poincaré duality groups in which the dualising maps between chains and cochains need not be equivariant; see Remark
12.8
. Such groups have the same large-scale topology as
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. A coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
group is a Poincaré duality group if and only if it has finite cohomological dimension (Proposition
12.7
). We note that for
n=1
, a finitely generated group is a coarse
PD_{1}^{\mathbb{F}}
group if and only if it is virtually infinite cyclic.

Informally, Theorem
A
can be seen as a precise articulation of the fact that for a countable group
G
, setting
n\coloneqq\inf\{k\in\mathbb{N}\mid H^{k}(G,\mathbb{F}G)\neq 0\}
and assuming
n<\infty
, either:

•

G
is highly singular —
\dim H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=\infty
; or

•

G
is highly non-singular —
G
has the same large-scale topology as
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

An analogous dichotomy was shown in the case of a hyperbolic group
G
, whose boundary
\partial G
is either a highly non-Euclidean
“fractal” space or a sphere
[
KB02
, Theorem 4.4]
. There is a third more exotic possibility that
H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=0
for all
n
, which occurs when
G
is Thompson’s group
F

[
BG84
]
.

In 1975, Farrell obtained a similar result to Theorem
A
under — in Farrell’s own words — “rather strong finiteness conditions”, specifically that the group
G
is of type
FP_{n}^{\mathbb{F}}
and has finite cohomological dimension over
\mathbb{F}

[
Far75
]
; see also
[
Far74
]
for earlier work in the case
n=2
. In Theorem
A
, we impose no such assumptions. Rather, we can actually deduce that in the
\dim H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=1
case of Theorem
A
,
G
must necessarily be of type
FP_{\infty}^{\mathbb{F}}
, and must have
finite coarse cohomological dimension
, a concept we introduce in this article and will discuss shortly.

We recall the following question of Serre from 1974:

Question 1.2

(
[
Far74
]
)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbb{F}
be a field and let
G
be a finitely presented group. Does every
G
-invariant subspace of
H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)
have dimension 0, 1 or
\infty
?

Theorem
A
builds on work of Farrell
[
Far74
,
Far75
]
to obtain many more instances in which Question
1.2
has a positive answer, specifically when
n=\inf\{k\in\mathbb{N}\mid H^{k}(G,\mathbb{F}G)\neq 0\}<\infty
and
H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)
has countable dimension, the latter condition always holding if
G
is of type
FP_{n}^{\mathbb{F}}
by Proposition
9.8
. Moreover, only countability, not finite presentability, is needed. However, Question
1.2
in full generality is still wide open.

We observe the main conclusion of Theorem
A
— that
\dim H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)
is either
0
,
1
or
\infty
— is a statement purely about group cohomology. Therefore, one might naturally expect that such a statement can be proved using group cohomology, i.e. via an algebraic argument using
\mathbb{F}G
-modules and
G
-equivariant maps between them. However, this is not the approach we take to prove Theorem
A
, and the author strongly believes such an approach is impossible without additional hypotheses.
Instead, we prove Theorem
A
by appealing to one of the key concepts of geometric group theory: we equip a countable group with a metric and then forget about the algebraic structure of a group, focussing solely on the large-scale geometric and topological properties of the group as a metric space.

In order to prove Theorem
A
, we work not in the category of
RG
-modules, but in the category of
R
-modules over a metric space
that we introduce in Section
3
. A large part of this article is therefore spent developing the necessary language and machinery of
homological algebra over metric spaces
. Indeed, this machinery is of independent interest, and was developed in ongoing projects of the author to prove quasi-isometric rigidity of splittings and of commensurated subgroups.
To illustrate the philosophy adopted in the article, we state a variant of Theorem
A
that applies to metric spaces rather than to groups:

Theorem B

(Corollary
12.12
)
.

Let
R
be a PID and let
n\geq 1
. Suppose
X
is a coarsely homogeneous proper metric space that is coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic over
R
. Assume that
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;R)=0
for
k<n
. Then either:

(1)

H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(X;R)=0
.

(2)

H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(X;R)\cong R
, in which case
X
is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
space.

(3)

H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(X;R)
is not finitely generated as an
R
-module.

Theorem
B
is another generalisation of the Hopf–Freudenthal theorem on ends.
Although there is significant overlap between Theorems
A
and
B
, neither one implies the other. Indeed, Theorem
A
makes no assumptions that the group is coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic (see Theorem
2.4
), whilst Theorem
B
holds for metric spaces, and is valid for any PID, not just a field. Additional variations of these results are proved in Section
12
.

We briefly discuss the terms in the statement of Theorem
B
.
A metric space
X
is
coarsely homogeneous
if for all points
x,y\in X
, there is a coarse equivalence
\phi
with uniform control functions such that
\phi(x)=y
. Such an assumption is clearly necessary for Theorem
B
— in the case
n=1
, there exist metric spaces that are not coarsely homogeneous, but possess any finite number of ends, a fact that would contradict the conclusion of Theorem
B
.
The property of being coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic is a metric property that is analogous to a group being of type
FP_{n}
. Indeed, Theorem
2.4
says the two properties coincide when
G
is a countable group equipped with a proper left-invariant metric.

Theorem
B
is formulated using
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{*}(X;R)
, the
coarse cohomology
of a metric space
X
with coefficients in
R
. For a countable group
G
equipped with a proper left-invariant metric,
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{*}(G;R)
coincides with group cohomology
H^{*}(G,RG)
with group ring coefficients.
Roe first defined a version of coarse cohomology
[
Roe93
]
. It is well-known to capture many aspects of the topology at infinity of a space or of a group. Building on work of Kapovich–Kleiner
[
KK05
]
, we develop a more general coarse cohomology theory that is better suited to quantitative and effective arguments on the chain and cochain level.

We define coarse cohomology modules of the form
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{*}(X;\mathbf{M})
, where the coefficient module
\mathbf{M}
is an
R
-module over
X
. Our theory is thus a better analogue of group cohomology, in which cohomology modules are of the form
H^{*}(G,M)
for an
RG
-module
M
.
In this article, many phenomena that occur within the category of
RG
-modules are reimagined within the more general category of
R
-modules over a metric space. To that end, Table
1
gives us a dictionary between these two categories.

G

A metric space
X

An
RG
-module
M

An
R
-module
\mathbf{M}
over
X

A projective
RG
-module
M

A projective
R
-module
\mathbf{M}
over
X

A
G
-equivariant map
M\to N
between
RG
-modules

A finite displacement map
\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
between
R
-modules over
X

H^{n}(G,M)

Coarse cohomology

A finitely generated
RG
-module

A finite-height
R
-module over
X

A projective resolution
C_{\bullet}
of the trivial
RG
-module
R

A projective
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X

The following are equivalent:

•

H^{k}(G,M)=0
for all
RG
-modules
M
and
k>n
.

•

There exists a
C_{\bullet}
as above such that
C_{k}=0
for
k>n
.

When either hold, we say
\cd_{R}(G)\leq n
.

The following are equivalent:

•

H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;\mathbf{M})=0
for every
R
-module
\mathbf{M}
over
X
and every
k>n
.

•

There exists a
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
as above such that
\mathbf{C}_{k}=0
for
k>n
.

When either hold, we say
\ccd_{R}(X)\leq n
.

G
is of type
FP_{n}^{R}
if there exists a
C_{\bullet}
as above such that
C_{k}
is a finitely generated
RG
-module for
k\leq n
.

X
is coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic over
R
if and only if there exists a
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
as above such that
\mathbf{C}_{k}
is of finite height for
k\leq n
.

over

over

PD_{n}^{R}
space

Table 1.
A dictionary relating concepts in group cohomology in the left-hand column, to analogous concepts in the coarse cohomology of metric spaces in the right-hand column.

Table
1
is intended as a rough guide for the casual reader who is already familiar with the group cohomology concepts in the left-hand column, and desires to quickly gain an informal appreciation of the vocabulary of
R
-modules over metric spaces in the right-hand column. However, we do not yet assert any precise relationship between items in the left-hand and right-hand columns, referring the reader to the main text for precise definitions and formal statements of results.

It is important to emphasise that even if one is only interested in the group cohomology of a countable group
G
, there are reasons to work in the category of
R
-modules over
G
. Indeed, there are many instances in which it is unnecessarily restrictive to limit ourselves to
G
-equivariant maps between
G
-modules. Passing to the more flexible framework of non-equivariant maps between
R
-modules over a metric space is crucial in proving results such as Theorems
A
and
B
.

These ideas are further illustrated by the
coarse cohomological dimension
of a metric space, which we introduce in Section
8
. We recall that the classical cohomological dimension
\cd_{R}(G)
of a group
G
with respect to a coefficient ring
R
is one of the most important and well-studied invariants of infinite groups
[
Bro82
,
Bie81
]
. Unfortunately (at least from the viewpoint of the author)
\cd_{R}(G)
is quite sensitive to the precise algebraic structure of the group.

The situation is the most extreme in the case of groups with torsion: any group
G
containing a torsion element satisfies
\cd(G)=\infty
. We consider the illuminating case where
G=D_{\infty}
is the infinite dihedral group and
H\leq G
is an infinite cyclic subgroup of index 2, which satisfies
1=\cd(H)\leq\cd(G)=\infty
. We suppose that

\cdots\to C_{2}\to C_{1}\to C_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}\to 0

is a resolution of the trivial
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
\mathbb{Z}
by free
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules. The condition
\cd(G)\leq 1
is equivalent to the existence of a
G
-equivariant retraction
r:C_{1}\to C_{1}
onto
K\coloneqq\ker(C_{1}\to C_{0})
. Indeed, the existence of such a map
r
allows us to
G
-equivariantly truncate the above resolution to a finite length projective resolution

0\to\ker(r)\to C_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}\to 0

of the trivial
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
\mathbb{Z}
.
Of course, since
G=D_{\infty}
has torsion,
\cd(G)=\infty
, and so no such retraction
r:C_{1}\to C_{1}
exists. However, since
\cd(H)=1
, there is an
H
-equivariant retraction
r:C_{1}\to C_{1}
onto
K\coloneqq\ker(C_{1}\to C_{0})
.

When we pass to a more general category that allows non-equivariant maps, such obstructions to finite dimensionality disappear, and we obtain a robust notion of dimension that is invariant under coarse equivalence. In particular, even though the
H
-equivariant retraction
r:C_{1}\to C_{1}
considered above is not
G
-equivariant, it is nonetheless a finite displacement map between
\mathbb{Z}
-modules over
G
; see Section
3
for definitions of these terms. We can therefore (non-equivariantly) truncate the resolution
C_{\bullet}
to obtain a finite length resolution
0\to\ker(r)\to C_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}\to 0
by projective
R
-modules over
G
, which shows that the coarse cohomological dimension of
G
is equal to 1; see Section
8
.

This approach is very fruitful, and yields a robust notion of dimension for arbitrary metric spaces. We let
\ccd_{R}(X)
denote the
coarse cohomological dimension
of
X
with coefficients in
R
, and in the case
R=\mathbb{Z}
, we denote
\ccd_{\mathbb{Z}}
by
\ccd
. We show
\ccd_{R}(X)
satisfies the following properties:

Theorem C

(Theorems
8.3
and
8.7
)
.

For each commutative ring
R
, the following hold:

(1)

If
X
and
Y
are metric spaces and
f:X\to Y
is a coarse embedding, then
\ccd_{R}(X)\leq\ccd_{R}(Y)
. In particular, if
X
and
Y
are coarsely equivalent, then
\ccd_{R}(X)=\ccd_{R}(Y)
.

(2)

If
G
is a countable group equipped with a proper left-invariant metric and
\cd_{R}(G)<\infty
, then
\cd_{R}(G)=\ccd_{R}(G)
.

Remark 1.3
.

For readers more accustomed to quasi-isometries than to coarse equivalences, we note that coarse equivalences and quasi-isometries coincide for quasi-geodesic metric spaces; see Lemma
2.2
. In particular, two finitely generated groups are coarsely equivalent if and only if they are quasi-isometric.

Applying Theorem
C
to the class of countable groups with finite cohomological dimension, we recover the following result of Sauer:

Corollary D

(
[
Sau06
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring and let
G
and
H
be countable groups. If
f:H\to G
is a coarse embedding and
\cd_{R}(H)
is finite, then
\cd_{R}(H)\leq\cd_{R}(G)
.
In particular, if
G
and
H
are finitely generated quasi-isometric groups and
\cd_{R}(H),\cd_{R}(G)<\infty
, then
\cd_{R}(G)=\cd_{R}(H)
.

However, Theorem
C
is a stronger and more powerful result than Corollary
D
, as it applies to metric spaces rather than just groups. Moreover, while
\cd_{R}(-)
is only a quasi-isometry invariant when restricted to groups of finite cohomological dimensional,
\ccd_{R}(-)
is a genuine quasi-isometry invariant amongst all finitely generated groups, agreeing with
\cd_{R}(-)
when the latter is finite.

It should be remarked that our proof of Corollary
D
takes a rather different approach than that used by Sauer. In
[
Sau06
]
, given a coarse embedding
H\to G
and an
H
-module
M
, an induced
G
-module
\overline{I}(M)
is constructed, which allows one to relate the cohomology of
G
to the cohomology of
H
. See also
[
Li18
]
. Our approach is to bypass group cohomology and
G
-modules entirely, instead relating the coarse cohomological dimensions of
G
and
H
when considered as metric spaces.

A (virtually) torsion-free hyperbolic group
G
has finite (virtual) cohomological dimension, and Bestvina–Mess proved the famous formula
[
BM91
]

\vcd(G)=\dim(\partial G)+1,

where
\dim(\partial G)
is the Lebesgue covering dimension of the Gromov boundary of
G
. Using work of Bestvina, Dranishnikov and Moran on
\mathcal{Z}
-boundaries
[
Bes96
,
Dra06
,
Mor16
]
, we extend the Bestvina–Mess formula to all hyperbolic and
\CAT(0)
groups:

Theorem E

(Corollary
9.16
)
.

Suppose that either:

(1)

G
is a hyperbolic group and
X
is a Cayley graph of
G
; or

(2)

G
is a
\CAT(0)
group acting geometrically on a proper
\CAT(0)
space
X
.

Then
\ccd(G)=\dim(\partial X)+1<\infty
.

Here,
\partial X
is either the Gromov or the visual boundary of
X
. It is a notorious open problem to determine whether all hyperbolic groups have a finite-index torsion-free subgroup. This problem is equivalent to determining whether all hyperbolic groups are residually finite
[
Wis96
,
Ol’00
]
. One consequence of a positive solution to this problem would be that all hyperbolic groups have finite virtual cohomological dimension, and then the original Bestvina–Mess formula would hold. Theorem
E
circumvents this issue by passing to the more robust notion of coarse cohomological dimension, which is finite for all hyperbolic groups, regardless of whether they are virtually torsion-free.

In contrast, there are examples of
\CAT(0)
groups that are not virtually torsion-free. Such groups have infinite (virtual) cohomological dimension, but by Theorem
E
, the Bestvina–Mess formula holds when formulated in terms of coarse cohomological dimension. For example, Wise constructed a group
G
which acts geometrically on a
\CAT(0)
square complex, but has no finite-index torsion-free subgroup
[
Wis96
]
. In fact, this example is both biautomatic and a
C(4)-T(4)
small-cancellation group. I am grateful to Jingyin Huang for alerting the author to this example.

Recall that a
quasi-tree
is a metric space that is quasi-isometric to a simplicial tree. Work of Bestvina–Bromberg–Fujiwara has emphasised the importance of quasi-trees in geometric group theory
[
BBF15
]
. Using Manning’s
bottleneck criterion

[
Man05
]
, we give a coarse topological characterisation of quasi-trees. We restrict ourselves to unbounded quasi-trees, since for each
R
, a metric space
X
is bounded if and only if
\ccd_{R}(X)=0
(Proposition
8.10
).

Theorem F

(Theorem
10.2
)
.

Let
X
be a quasi-geodesic metric space and let
R
be a commutative ring. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\ccd_{R}(X)=1
;

(2)

X
is an unbounded quasi-tree.

Since a finitely generated group that is quasi-isometric to a simplicial tree is virtually free
[
Dun85
]
, Theorem
F
is a generalisation of the following theorem of Stallings (in the case
R=\mathbb{Z}
) and Dunwoody (for general
R
).

Corollary 1.4

(
[
Sta68
,
Dun79
]
)
.

Suppose
R
is a commutative ring and
G
is a finitely generated group. If
\cd_{R}(G)=1
, then
G
is virtually free.

Remark 1.5
.

In fact, work of Swan and Dunwoody shows Corollary
1.4
holds without the hypothesis that
G
is finitely generated
[
Swa69
,
Dun79
]
. However, this stronger statement is not generalised by Theorem
F
. We note also that Theorem
F
does
not
give another proof of the fact that a finitely generated group quasi-isometric to a tree is virtually free; this requires work of Stallings and Dunwoody
[
Sta68
,
Dun85
]
.

There is another large-scale notion of dimension of a metric space
X
, due to Gromov, called the
asymptotic dimension
and denoted
\asdim(X)
. In Proposition
9.12
, we apply work of Dranishnikov
[
Dra09
]
to show that
\ccd(X)\leq\asdim(X)
provided
\ccd(X)<\infty
and
X
is coarsely uniformly acyclic. However, the hypothesis that
X
is coarsely uniformly acyclic cannot be dropped due to the following example of Denis Osin
[
FW07
, Example 1.3]
. For any non-trivial finite group
F
, the wreath product
G=F\wr\mathbb{Z}
is finitely generated, not finitely presented, and satisfies
\asdim(G)=1
. In particular,
G
is not virtually free, so by Theorem
F
, we have
\ccd(G)>1=\asdim(G)
.

Outside the world of discrete countable groups, the tools developed in this article can be used to study various non-discrete topological groups.
It is known that many uncountable groups, such as locally compact compactly generated groups
[
CdlH16
]
and more generally
CB
generated groups
[
Ros22
]
, can be endowed with a metric unique up to quasi-isometry. Thus,
\ccd_{R}(G)
provides a well-defined dimension of such groups.
For instance, if
T_{\infty}
is the regular tree of countably infinite valence, then Rosendal showed that
\Aut(T_{\infty})
is quasi-isometric to
T_{\infty}

[
Ros22
, Example 6.34]
. Therefore, Theorem
F
implies that
\ccd(\Aut(T_{\infty}))=1
. One potential future application of coarse cohomological dimension in particular, and the theory of
R
-modules over metric spaces in general, would be to distinguish
big groups
, such as big mapping class groups and homeomorphism groups of manifolds, up to quasi-isometry.

Comparison to other coarse cohomology theories

We briefly compare and contrast our methods to earlier approaches in the study of coarse algebraic topology of groups and metric spaces. Coarse topology was developed and used by many authors throughout the last 30–40 years
[
BW92
,
Gro93
,
Ger93
,
Roe93
,
Sch95
,
FS96
,
FM98
,
EF97
,
MSW03
,
MSW11
]
. Early work of Gersten
[
Ger93
]
showed that when restricted to groups of type
FP_{\infty}
,
H^{i}(G,\mathbb{Z}G)
is a quasi-isometry invariant, and that when restricted to groups of type
FP
,
\cd(G)
is a quasi-isometry invariant. These ideas were significantly developed by Kapovich–Kleiner in both the main article and appendix of
[
KK05
]
, where they developed the coarse algebraic topology of
coarsely uniformly acyclic metric spaces
.
The work of Kapovich–Kleiner has several applications, including work of Farb–Mosher
[
FM00
,
FM02
]
, Mosher–Sageev–Whyte
[
MSW03
,
MSW11
]
, Papasoglu
[
Pap07
]
and earlier works of the author
[
Mar18
,
Mar21
,
Mar24
]
.
The framework and language used in this article is principally inspired by these works. However, one of the key novelties in our approach is that by using filtrations, we are able to prove results for general metric spaces with no uniform acyclicity hypothesis.

Another approach to coarse algebraic topology is due to Roe
[
Roe93
]
. The coarse cohomology modules
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;R)
defined in this article coincide with Roe’s coarse cohomology (Proposition
7.5
), although we consider more general coarse cohomology modules of the form
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;\mathbf{M})
where
\mathbf{M}
is an
R
-module over
X
.
Although Roe’s theory is well suited to problems in index theory, the main difficulty with Roe’s theory is that it is harder to carry out arguments at the cochain level that are needed, for instance, to prove results such as Theorems
A
and
B
. As Kapovich–Kleiner state, “By passing to the limit … one inevitably loses quantitative information which
is essential in many applications of coarse topology to quasi-isometries
and geometric group theory”. Another key feature of our work, not fully developed by either Roe or Kapovich–Kleiner, is a homological algebraic approach to manipulating
chain complexes of
R
-modules over a metric space
as objects in their own right and not merely as a means to defining homology and cohomology. This perspective is central to this article, especially in the theory of coarse cohomological dimension.

Outline of article

In Section
2
, we review basic concepts in coarse geometry and group cohomology. In Sections
3
and
4
, we define
R
-modules over a metric space and projective
R
-modules over a metric space. Section
5
establishes the basic properties of
\Hom
-sets of maps between
R
-modules over a metric space.

In Section
6
, we finally define the main tool of this paper: projective
R
-resolutions over a metric space. These are analogues of projective resolutions of the trivial
RG
-module
R
. After doing this, it is relatively straightforward to define and prove basic properties about coarse cohomology and homology, coarse cohomological dimension, and the theory of uniformly acyclic spaces, which we do in Sections
7
,
8
and
9
respectively. In Section
10
, we characterise quasi-trees in terms of coarse cohomological dimension, using Manning’s bottleneck criterion
[
Man05
]
.

In Section
11
we develop a notion of cup and cap products for proper metric spaces. Section
12
is the culmination of the article, in which we use virtually all the machinery developed so far to further extend the theory of coarse
PD_{n}
spaces, originally developed by Kapovich–Kleiner
[
KK05
]
. In particular, we prove Theorems
A
and
B
. To do this, we make use of some results from Appendix
A
regarding the cardinality of the cohomology of inverse and derived limits of cochain complexes.

2.
Preliminaries

All rings considered in this article will possess a multiplicative identity, and all homomorphisms between such rings will be assumed to preserve the multiplicative identity. Most rings we consider will be commutative, and will typically be denoted by either
R
or
S
. The only potentially non-commutative rings that we consider are group rings, which will be denoted
RG
,
SH
etc.
We assume
0
is a natural number.

We will frequently work with maps
f:X\to Y
between partially ordered sets. It is always assumed that
\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbb{R}
are equipped with their standard orders, and that
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
is equipped with the product partial order, i.e.
(m,n)\leq(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
if and only if
m\leq m^{\prime}
and
n\leq n^{\prime}
. A map
f:X\to Y
between partially ordered sets is
increasing
if
f(x)\leq f(y)
whenever
x\leq y
. In particular, increasing functions need not be strictly increasing.
The set of all maps
f:X\to Y
between partially ordered sets will itself be equipped with a partial order such that
f\leq f^{\prime}
when
f(x)\leq f^{\prime}(x)
for all
x\in X
. In the case
Y
is totally ordered, any non-empty finite collection of functions
X\to Y
will have a maximum element.

2.1.
Coarse geometry of metric spaces

Let
X
and
Y
be metric spaces. Given subsets
Z,W\subseteq X
and
r\geq 0
, we write

N_{r}(Z)\coloneqq\{x\in X\mid d(x,Z)\leq r\}

for the closed metric
r
-neighbourhood of
Z
, and
d_{\textrm{Haus}}(Z,W)
for the Hausdorff distance between
Z
and
W
.
Given
A\geq 0
, two maps
f,f^{\prime}:X\to Y
are said to be
A
-close
if
d(f(x),f^{\prime}(x))\leq A
for all
x\in X
. We say
f,f^{\prime}:X\to Y
are
close
if they are
A
-close for some
A
. An
A
-
coarse inverse
of
f:X\to Y
is a map
g:Y\to X
such that
g\circ f
and
f\circ g
are
A
-close to
\id_{X}
and
\id_{Y}
respectively. A
coarse inverse
of
f
is an
A
-coarse inverse of
f
for some
A\geq 0
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
(X,d_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y})
be metric spaces, and let
\Upsilon,\Lambda:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be proper increasing functions. We say that a function
f:X\to Y
is:

•

\Upsilon
-bornologous
if for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
,
d_{Y}(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))\leq\Upsilon(d_{X}(x,x^{\prime}))
.

•

A
(\Lambda,\Upsilon)
-coarse embedding
if for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
,

\Lambda\left(d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})\right)\leq d_{Y}(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))\leq\Upsilon\left(d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})\right).

•

A
(\Lambda,\Upsilon)
-coarse equivalence
if
f
is a
(\Lambda,\Upsilon)
-coarse embedding, and has an
\Upsilon(0)
-coarse inverse that is also a
(\Lambda,\Upsilon)
-coarse embedding.

We call
\Upsilon
and
\Lambda
the
control functions
of
f
.
We say that
f
is
bornologous
if it is
\Upsilon
-bornologous for some proper increasing function
\Upsilon
, and we define the notion of a
coarse embedding
and a
coarse equivalence
similarly.

If
\Upsilon:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a proper increasing function, we define the
weak inverse
of
\Upsilon
to be the proper increasing function
\widetilde{\Upsilon}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
given by

\widetilde{\Upsilon}(R)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\sup(\Upsilon^{-1}([0,R]))&\textrm{if}\;\Upsilon(0)\leq R,\\
0&\textrm{if}\;\Upsilon(0)>R.\end{cases}

The weak inverse satisfies the following properties:

•

If
\Upsilon(S)\leq R
, then
S\leq\widetilde{\Upsilon}(R)
.

•

If
R<\Upsilon(S)
, then
\widetilde{\Upsilon}(R)\leq S
.

Using weak inverses, is straightforward to verify a coarse embedding
f:X\to Y
is a coarse equivalence if and only if
Y=N_{A}(f(X))
for some
A
.

A map
f:X\to Y
is
coarse Lipschitz
(resp. a
quasi-isometric embedding
, a
quasi-isometry
) if it is bornologous (resp. a coarse embedding, a coarse equivalence) with affine control functions. A metric space is said to be
coarsely geodesic
(resp.
quasi-geodesic
) if it is coarsely equivalent (resp. quasi-isometric) to a geodesic metric space.

The following lemma is due to Gromov:

Lemma 2.2

(
[
Gro93
, §0.2.D, Lemma]
)
.

Let
X
and
Y
be metric spaces, and let
f:X\to Y
be bornologous.

(1)

If
X
is quasi-geodesic, then
f
is coarse Lipschitz.

(2)

If
X
and
Y
are quasi-geodesic and
f
is a coarse equivalence, then
f
is a quasi-isometry.

Each countable group
G
admits a proper left-invariant metric, and this metric is unique up to coarse equivalence; see for instance
[
NY23
, §1]
. For example, if
G
is finitely generated, the word metric with respect to any finite generating set is such a metric, and is in fact a quasi-geodesic metric. In this article, we always assume every countable group
G
is equipped with such a metric. Moreover, in the case
G
is finitely generated, we assume this metric is the word metric with respect to a finite generating set, hence is quasi-geodesic.

Given a metric space
X
and a parameter
r\geq 0
, the
Rips complex

P_{r}(X)
of
X
with parameter
r
is the simplicial complex with vertex set
X
, and where each finite subset
\{x_{0},\dots,x_{k}\}\subseteq X
spans a simplex of
P_{r}(X)
if
d(x_{i},x_{j})\leq r
for all
0\leq i,j\leq k
. For each subspace
Y\subseteq X
and
s\leq r
, we always identify
P_{s}(Y)
with its image in
P_{r}(X)
under the natural inclusion
P_{s}(Y)\to P_{r}(X)
.

In the following definition, if
Z
is a simplicial complex and
R
is a commutative ring, then
\widetilde{H}_{k}(Z;R)
denotes the
k
th reduced simplicial homology of
Z
with coefficients in
R
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\lambda:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\mu:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be increasing functions such that
\lambda(i)\geq i
and
\mu(i,r)\geq r
for all
i,r\in\mathbb{R}
. Given a commutative ring
R
, we say that a metric space
X
is
(\lambda,\mu)
-coarsely uniformly
n
-acyclic over
R
if for every
k\leq n
,
i,r\geq 0
and
x\in X
, the map

\widetilde{H}_{k}(P_{i}(N_{r}(x));R)\to\widetilde{H}_{k}(P_{\lambda(i)}(N_{\mu(i,r)}(x));R),

induced by inclusion, is zero. A metric space is
coarsely uniformly
n
-acyclic over
R
if there exist
\lambda
and
\mu
as above, and a metric space
X
is
coarsely uniformly acyclic over
R
if it is coarsely uniformly
n
-acyclic over
R
for each
n
.

Coarse uniform
n
-acyclicity over
R
is invariant under coarse equivalence
[
Mar18
, Proposition 2.15]
.

2.2.
Group cohomology

We recall some key concepts from group cohomology. The reader is referred to the books of Bieri and Brown for more details
[
Bie81
,
Bro82
]
. We fix a commutative ring
R
.
Suppose
G
is a group and

\cdots\to P_{n}\to P_{n-1}\to\cdots\to P_{0}\to R\to 0

is a projective resolution of the trivial
RG
-module
R
. If
N
is any
RG
-module, we define the
group cohomology

H^{k}(G,N)
to be the
k
th cohomology
H^{k}(\Hom_{RG}(P_{\bullet},N))
of the cochain complex
\Hom_{RG}(P_{\bullet},N)
obtained by applying the functor
\Hom_{RG}(-,N)
to
P_{\bullet}
.

The
cohomological dimension

\cd_{R}(G)
of
G
over
R
is the least
n
such that there exists a length
n
projective resolution

\cdots\to 0\to P_{n}\to P_{n-1}\to\cdots\to P_{0}\to R\to 0

of the trivial
RG
-module
R
. We set
\cd_{R}(G)=\infty
if no such
n
exists. If
G
acts freely on a contractible
n
-dimensional CW complex, then
\cd_{R}(G)\leq n
for all
R
. We write
\cd_{\mathbb{Z}}(G)
as
\cd(G)
. If
G
has torsion, then
\cd(G)=\infty
. If
G
has a torsion-free finite-index subgroup
G^{\prime}
, then the
virtual cohomological dimension

\vcd_{R}(G)
is defined to be
\cd_{R}(G^{\prime})
. A theorem of Serre ensures that
\vcd_{R}(G)
is independent of the choice of
G^{\prime}
. If no such
G^{\prime}
exists, then
\vcd_{R}(G)=\infty
.

Given a commutative ring
R
, a group
G
is of type
FP_{n}^{R}
if the trivial
RG
-module
R
admits a projective resolution

\cdots\to P_{n}\to P_{n-1}\to\cdots\to P_{0}\to R\to 0

with
P_{i}
a finitely generated
RG
-module for each
i\leq n
. In the case
R=\mathbb{Z}
, we write type
FP_{n}^{\mathbb{Z}}
simply as
FP_{n}
. Being of type
FP_{1}
coincides with being finitely generated, whilst being of type
FP_{2}
generalises the notion of being finitely presented; see
[
BB97
,
Geo08
]
for more details. A group that acts freely on a contractible locally finite CW complex with finitely many
G
-orbits of
k
-cells for
k\leq n
is necessarily of type
FP_{n}^{R}
.

The following can be deduced from work of Alonso
[
Alo94
]
and Kapovich–Kleiner
[
KK05
]
; see also
[
Mar18
, Theorem 3.2]
for an explicit statement.

Theorem 2.4

(
[
Alo94
,
KK05
]
,
[
Mar18
, Theorem 3.2]
)
.

A countable group
G
is of type
FP_{n}^{R}
if and only if it is coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic over
R
.

3.
Modules over metric spaces

We define an
R
-module over a metric space in Definition
3.1
. We discuss and develop the basic theory of
R
-modules over a metric space, including finite displacement maps, maps satisfying the uniform preimage property, pullbacks and pushforwards.

Henceforth, we let
R
be a commutative ring unless explicitly stated otherwise.

A
filtration
of a set
B
is a collection
\{B(i)\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of subsets of
B
satisfying the following properties:

(1)

If
i\leq j
, then
B(i)\subseteq B(j)
.

(2)

B=\cup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}B(i)
.

As all filtrations considered in this article will have indexing set
\mathbb{N}
, we will write
\{B(i)\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
as either
\{B(i)\}_{i}
or as
\{B(i)\}
when convenient.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
X
be a metric space and let
R
be a commutative ring. An
R
-module over
X
consists of the data
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\mathcal{F})
where:

(1)

M
is an
R
-module, called the
underlying
R
-module
.

(2)

B
is an arbitrary indexing set.

(3)

\delta
is a function
B\to M^{*}=\Hom_{R}(M,R)
. We denote the image of
b\in B
under
\delta
as
\delta_{b}
. We call each
\delta_{b}
a
coordinate
.

(4)

p:B\to X
is a function called the
control function
.

(5)

For each
m\in M
,
\delta_{b}(m)=0
for all
b\in B
if and only if
m=0
.

(6)

\mathcal{F}=\{B(i)\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a filtration of
B
such that for each
m\in M
, there exists some
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\delta_{b}(m)=0
for all
b\in B\setminus B(i)
.

For each
m\in M
, we write

\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)\coloneqq\{p(b)\mid b\in B,\delta_{b}(m)\neq 0\},

and for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
we write,

\mathbf{M}(i)\coloneqq\{m\in M\mid\delta_{b}(m)=0\text{ for all }b\in B\setminus B(i)\}.

We note that
\{\mathbf{M}(i)\}_{i}
is a filtration of
\mathbf{M}
by submodules. For every
R
-module
\mathbf{M}
over
X
, the product map
\Pi_{b\in B}\delta_{b}:M\to\Pi_{b\in B}R
is an embedding. This places restrictions on
R
-modules over
X
; for instance, they are always torsion-free (or more accurately, their underlying
R
-modules are torsion-free). We frequently write
\{\delta_{b}\}_{b\in B}
or even
\{\delta_{b}\}
instead of
\delta
when convenient.
An
R
-module
\mathbf{M}
over
X
is frequently identified with its underlying
R
-module
M
, so we will write expressions such as
m\in\mathbf{M}
and
m\in M
interchangeably. We will use bold typeface
\mathbf{M}
,
\mathbf{N}
etc. to denote
R
-modules over metric spaces, and will use regular typeface
M
,
N
etc. to denote
R
-modules that are not equipped with the structure of an
R
-module over a metric space.
When unambiguous, we write
\supp
instead of
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}
.

Before developing the general theory, we first give some important examples of
R
-modules over a metric space.

Example 3.2
.

Let
X
be a metric space and let
M
be the free abelian group with basis the set
B=\{[x,y]\mid x,y\in X\}
of all ordered pairs in
X
. We define
\delta:B\to M^{*}
so that
\{\delta_{b}\}_{b\in B}
is the dual basis to
B
, i.e.

\delta_{[x,y]}([x^{\prime},y^{\prime}])=\begin{cases}1&\textrm{if}\;x=y\;\textrm{and}\;y=y^{\prime},\\
0&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We define the map
p:B\to X
by
[x,y]\mapsto x
.
Finally, we define a filtration
\{B(i)\}
of
B
by

B(i)\coloneqq\{[x,y]\in B\mid d(x,y)\leq i\}

for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

We claim that
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-module over
X
. Indeed, every
m\in M
can be written as a sum

m=\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(m)b

in which almost all (i.e. all but finitely many) terms are zero. Thus either
m=0
, or
\delta_{b}(m)\neq 0
for some
b\in B
, proving (
5
). Moreover, as almost all terms in the above sum are zero, there is some
i
such that
\delta_{b}(m)=0
for all
b\in B\setminus B(i)
, proving (
6
).

A slogan that should guide the reader’s intuition about elements of a general
R
-module
\mathbf{N}
over a metric space
X
is the following:

If
n\in\mathbf{N}(i)
, then
n
should be visualized as lying close to
\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(n)
, up to some error depending on
i
.

We examine this statement for
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{M}
as defined above. Suppose
x,y,z,w
are distinct points in
X
and
m=[x,y]+3[z,w]\in\mathbf{M}
. We have
\delta_{[x,y]}(m)=1
,
\delta_{[z,w]}(m)=3
, and
\delta_{b}(m)=0
for all
b\in B\setminus\{[x,y],[z,w]\}
. Thus
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)=\{x,z\}
. We now note that
m\in\mathbf{M}(i)
if and only if
i\geq\max(d(x,y),d(z,w))
. Thus if
m\in\mathbf{M}(i)
for some
i
, we have
\{x,y,z,w\}\subseteq N_{i}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m))
. This holds more generally: if
m^{\prime}\in\mathbf{M}(j)
and
L\subseteq X
is the set of all elements that appear when we express
m^{\prime}
as a sum of elements of
B
, then
L\subseteq N_{j}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m^{\prime}))
.

Example 3.3
.

Let
G
be a countable group equipped with a proper left-invariant metric. Let
M
be a free
\mathbb{Z}G
-module with countably infinite
\mathbb{Z}G
-basis
C=\{c_{0},c_{1},\ldots\}
.
We let
B\coloneqq GC_{0}
, which is a free
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
M
. Let
\{\delta_{b}\}_{b\in B}
be the dual basis of
B
. We define
p:B\to G
by
p(gc_{i})=g
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
g\in G
, and we set
B(i)=\{gc_{k}\mid g\in G,k\leq i\}
. Then

\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})

is a
\mathbb{Z}
-module over
G
.
For each
m\in\mathbf{M}
, knowing
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)
and that
m\in\mathbf{M}(i)
tells us a lot of information about
m
. For example, if
m\in\mathbf{M}(1)
and
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)=\{g,k\}
, we know that
m
must lie in the
\mathbb{Z}
-span of
\{gc_{0},gc_{1},kc_{0},kc_{1}\}
.

Examples
3.2
and
3.3
are both instances of
free
R
-modules over a metric space
, which we discuss in more detail in Section
4
. These specific examples will be generalised and developed further in Proposition
6.19
and Lemma
4.8
respectively.

We now return to the general theory of
R
-modules over metric spaces.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\mathbf{M}
be an
R
-module over
X
, and let
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbf{M}
with
r_{1},r_{2}\in R
. Then
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(r_{1}m_{1}+r_{2}m_{2})\subseteq\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m_{1})\cup\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m_{2})
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\mathcal{F})
and
x\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(r_{1}m_{1}+r_{2}m_{2})
. Then there exists
b\in B
with
p(b)=x
and
\delta_{b}(r_{1}m_{1}+r_{2}m_{2})\neq 0
. Then one of
\delta_{b}(m_{1})
or
\delta_{b}(m_{2})
is non-zero, and so either
x\in\supp(m_{1})
or
x\in\supp(m_{2})
.
∎

A
homomorphism

\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
between
R
-modules over
X
and
Y
respectively, is simply a homomorphism
\phi:M\to N
between underlying
R
-modules.
However, two homomorphisms
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
and
\psi:\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{N}^{\prime}
are regarded as being equal when
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{N}^{\prime}
and the underlying homomorphism
M\to N
are equal. Formally, we should think of a homomorphism from
\mathbf{M}
to
\mathbf{N}
as a triple
(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\phi)
, where
\phi:M\to N
is a homomorphism between underlying
R
-modules. However, it is notationally cumbersome to distinguish between the triple
(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\phi)
and the map
\phi
.
In particular, we note that if
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
are different
R
-modules over
X
, then
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
and
\psi:\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{N}
should always be thought of as distinct since they have different “domains”, even in the case when
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
have the same underlying
R
-module
M
and the underlying homomorphisms
M\to N
between
R
-modules are equal.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
X
be a metric space and let
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\mathcal{F})
be an
R
-module over
X
. A
geometric submodule

\mathbf{N}
of
\mathbf{M}
, denoted
\mathbf{N}\leq\mathbf{M}
, is an
R
-module over
X
of the form
\mathbf{N}=(N,B,\epsilon,p,\mathcal{F})
where
N
is a submodule of
M
and for each
b\in B
, the coordinate
\epsilon_{b}
of
\mathbf{N}
is the restriction of the coordinate
\delta_{b}
of
\mathbf{M}
.

If
\mathbf{N}\leq\mathbf{M}
, then for each
n\in\mathbf{N}
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(n)=\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(n)
and
\mathbf{N}(i)=\mathbf{M}(i)\cap N
. In particular, there is no ambiguity in writing
\supp(n)
.
It is clear that there is a bijective correspondence between geometric submodules of
\mathbf{M}
and submodules of the underlying
R
-module
M
.
We emphasise that the notation
\mathbf{N}\leq\mathbf{M}
means
\mathbf{N}
is a geometric submodule, which is stronger than merely requiring that the underlying
R
-module of
\mathbf{N}
be a submodule of the underlying
R
-module of
\mathbf{M}
, which we denote as
N\leq M
.

We now define the appropriate notion of morphism between
R
-modules over a metric space:

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
be
R
-modules over
X
and
Y
respectively. Given a map
f:X\to Y
and a homomorphism
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
, we say
\phi
has
finite displacement
over
f
if there exists an increasing function
\Phi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that the following hold for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
:

(1)

\phi(\mathbf{M}(i))\subseteq\mathbf{N}({\Phi(i)})
;

(2)

for all
m\in\mathbf{M}(i)
,
\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(m))\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(f(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)))
.

When the above holds, we say that
\phi
has
displacement
\Phi
over
f
.
In the case where
X=Y
and no
f:X\to X
is explicitly mentioned, we say
\phi
has
finite displacement
when it has finite displacement over the identity map
\id_{X}:X\to X
.

We note that if
f,g:X\to Y
are close, then
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
has finite displacement over
f
if and only if it has finite displacement over
g
.

Definition 3.7
.

A map
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
between two
R
-modules over
X
is a
geometric isomorphism
if it is an isomorphism between the underlying
R
-modules such that both
\phi
and
\phi^{-1}
have finite displacement over
\id_{X}
.

Remark 3.8
.

We do
not
typically identify geometrically isomorphic
R
-modules over
X
. This is because
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)
is not necessarily equal to
\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(n))
when
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
is a geometric isomorphism. For quantitative arguments in this paper, it is vital that
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)
has a definite meaning, and this is lost upon replacing
\mathbf{M}
with its isomorphism class. Nonetheless,
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)
and
\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(m))
are related up to some error depending on the displacement of
\phi
and
\phi^{-1}
.

We will often consider two
R
-modules
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
over metric spaces
X
and
Y
that have the same underlying
R
-module
M
. In this situation, the
canonical identification
is the map
\iota:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
that is the identity on
M
. In the case
X=Y
, we say
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
are
canonically isomorphic
if the canonical identification is a geometric isomorphism, i.e. both
\iota
and
\iota^{-1}
have finite displacement over
\id_{X}
. For transparency, we explicitly state when
R
-modules over a metric space are canonically isomorphic:

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
X
be a metric space and let
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
be
R
-modules over
X
with the same underlying
R
-module. Let
\iota:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
be the canonical identification. Then
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
are canonically isomorphic if and only if there exists an increasing function
\Psi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that the following hold:

(1)

\iota(\mathbf{M}(i))\subseteq\mathbf{M}^{\prime}(\Psi(i))
and
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}(i)\subseteq\iota(\mathbf{M}(\Psi(i)))
for all
i
;

(2)

For all
m\in\mathbf{M}(i)
,
d_{\textrm{Haus}}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m),\supp_{\mathbf{M}^{\prime}}(\iota(m)))\leq\Psi(i)
.

If
\mathbf{N}\leq\mathbf{M}
is a geometric submodule, then the inclusion
\mathbf{N}\to\mathbf{M}
has finite displacement.
If
\phi:\mathbf{N}\to\mathbf{M}
has finite displacement, then we think of
\ker(\phi)\leq\mathbf{N}
and
\im(\phi)\leq\mathbf{M}
as geometric submodules. Moreover, the induced map
\mathbf{N}\to\im(\phi)
through which
\phi
factors also has finite displacement.

Remark 3.10
.

If
\mathbf{N}
and
\mathbf{M}
are
R
-modules over
X
with
\mathbf{N}\leq\mathbf{M}
a geometric submodule, then
\mathbf{M}/\mathbf{N}
is
not
naturally endowed with the structure of an
R
-module over
X
. In particular, in the category
\mathcal{C}
whose objects are
R
-modules over
X
and whose morphisms are finite displacement maps over
\id_{X}
, cokernels do not typically exist. Thus
\mathcal{C}
is not an abelian category, and there is no general nonsense approach to carrying out homological algebraic methods in
\mathcal{C}
.

We now show that the composition of finite displacement maps is frequently also of finite displacement:

Lemma 3.11
.

Suppose
\mathbf{L}
,
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
are
R
-modules over
X
,
Y
and
Z
.
Suppose also that
\phi:\mathbf{L}\to\mathbf{M}
and
\psi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
both have displacement
\Psi
over
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
. We also suppose that
g
is
\Upsilon
-bornologous. Then
\psi\circ\phi
has displacement
\Psi^{\prime}
over
g\circ f
, where
\Psi^{\prime}
depends only on
\Psi
and
\Upsilon
.

Proof.

Suppose
l\in\mathbf{L}(i)
. Then
\psi(\phi(l))\in\mathbf{N}(\Psi^{2}(i))
and

\displaystyle\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\psi\circ\phi(l))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{\Psi^{2}(i)}(g(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(\phi(l))))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{\Psi^{2}(i)}(g(N_{\Psi(i)}(f(\supp_{\mathbf{L}}l))))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{\Psi^{2}(i)+\Upsilon(\Psi(i))}(gf(\supp_{\mathbf{L}}l)).\qed

Definition 3.12
.

Let
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
be a homomorphism between
R
-modules over
X
. We say
\phi
has
uniform preimages
if there is an increasing function
\Omega:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\in\mathbf{N}(i)\cap\im(\phi)
, there is some
m\in\phi^{-1}(n)
such that:

•

m\in\mathbf{M}({\Omega(i,D)})
and

•

\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)\subseteq N_{\Omega(i,D)}(\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(n))
,

where
D\coloneqq\lceil\diam(\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(n))\rceil
.
For
\Omega
as above, we say
\phi
has
\Omega
-uniform preimages
when the above condition is satisfied.

Remark 3.13
.

The uniform preimage property is defined only for maps
\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
between
R
-modules over the same metric space.

We now discuss pullbacks and pushforwards of modules over metric spaces.

Definition 3.14
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\mathcal{F})
be an
R
-module over
X
. If
f:X\to Y
is an arbitrary map, the
pushforward
of
\mathbf{M}
is the
R
-module
f_{*}\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,f\circ p,\mathcal{F})
over
Y
.

Since
\mathbf{M}
and
f_{*}\mathbf{M}
have the same underlying
R
-module, there is a canonical identification
\iota:\mathbf{M}\to f_{*}\mathbf{M}
.
It is clear that for each
m\in\mathbf{M}
,
f(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m))=\supp_{f_{*}\mathbf{M}}(\iota(m))
. This implies the canonical identification
\mathbf{M}\to f_{*}\mathbf{M}
has finite displacement over
f
. Moreover,
(\id_{X})_{*}\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}
and
g_{*}f_{*}\mathbf{M}=(gf)_{*}\mathbf{M}
for any
g:Y\to Z
.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
X
and
Y
be metric spaces and let
f:X\to Y
be a coarse embedding. Suppose
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
are
R
-modules over
X
and
\mathbf{L}
is an
R
-module over
Y
. Let
\iota:\mathbf{N}\to f_{*}\mathbf{N}
be the canonical identification. Suppose
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{L}
has finite displacement over
f
and
\psi:\mathbf{L}\to f_{*}\mathbf{N}
has finite displacement over
\id_{Y}
. Then
\iota^{-1}\circ\psi\circ\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
has finite displacement over
\id_{X}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{*}\mathbf{N}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{-1}\circ\psi\circ\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

Figure 1
.
Each map in the right-hand diagram has finite displacement over the corresponding map in the left-hand diagram.

Proof.

Suppose that
f
is a
(\Lambda,\Upsilon)
-coarse embedding, and that
\phi
and
\psi
have displacement
\Phi
over
f
and
\id_{Y}
respectively. We define an increasing function
\Psi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by
\Psi(i)\coloneqq\max\left(\widetilde{\Lambda}\left(\Phi(i)+\Phi^{2}(i)\right),\Phi^{2}(i)\right)
, where
\widetilde{\Lambda}
is the weak inverse of
\Lambda
as in Section
2
. We show
\iota^{-1}\circ\psi\circ\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
has displacement
\Psi
over
\id_{X}
.

Fix
i\in\mathbb{N}
and set
i_{k}=\Phi^{k}(i)
.
It is clear that

\displaystyle(\iota^{-1}\circ\psi\circ\phi)(\mathbf{M}(i))\subseteq(\iota^{-1}\circ\psi)(\mathbf{L}(i_{1}))\subseteq\iota^{-1}(f_{*}\mathbf{N}(i_{2}))=\mathbf{N}(i_{2})\subseteq\mathbf{N}(\Psi(i)).

Now suppose
m\in\mathbf{M}(i)
. Then since
\phi(m)\in\mathbf{L}(i_{1})
, we have:

\displaystyle f(\supp_{\mathbf{N}}((\iota^{-1}\circ\psi\circ\phi)(m)))

\displaystyle=\supp_{f_{*}\mathbf{N}}((\psi\circ\phi)(m))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{i_{2}}(\supp_{\mathbf{L}}(\phi(m)))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{i_{2}+i_{1}}(f(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m))).

It follows that
\supp_{\mathbf{N}}((\iota^{-1}\circ\psi\circ\phi)(m))\subseteq N_{\Psi(i)}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m))
as required.
∎

Lemma 3.16
.

If
\mathbf{M}
is an
R
-module over
X
and
f,g:X\to Y
are close, then
f_{*}\mathbf{M}
and
g_{*}\mathbf{M}
are canonically isomorphic. In particular, if
f:X\to Y
is a coarse equivalence with coarse inverse
g:Y\to X
, then
g_{*}f_{*}\mathbf{M}
is canonically isomorphic to
\mathbf{M}
.

Proof.

The condition that
f
and
g
are close ensures there is a constant
A
such that
f(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m))
and
g(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m))
have Hausdorff distance at most
A
. By Lemma
3.9
, this implies the canonical identification
f_{*}\mathbf{M}\to g_{*}\mathbf{M}
is a geometric isomorphism.
∎

The notion of a pullback is more subtle than that of a pushforward:

Definition 3.17
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a coarse embedding.
We define a map
\pi:Y\to X
as follows: for each
y\in Y
, pick
\pi(y)\in X
with
d(f(\pi(y)),y)
minimal. In other words,
\pi
is the composition of a closest-point projection
Y\to f(X)
and a section
f(X)\to X
of
f
.
If
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B_{i}\})
is an
R
-module over
Y
, we define the
pullback

R
-module
f^{*}\mathbf{M}
over
X
by

f^{*}\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,\pi\circ p,\{f^{*}B(i)\}),

where
f^{*}B(i)\coloneqq\{b\in B(i)\mid d(f(X),p(b))\leq i\}
.

Although
\pi
is not necessarily unique,
f^{*}\mathbf{M}
is nonetheless well-defined up to canonical isomorphism:

Lemma 3.18
.

There is a function
\Psi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for all
y\in N_{i}(f(X))
, whenever
x,x^{\prime}\in X
are chosen such that
d(f(x),y)=d(f(x^{\prime}),y)
is minimal, then
d(x,x^{\prime})\leq\Psi(i)
. Consequently, up to canonical isomorphism,
f^{*}\mathbf{M}
is independent of the choice of
\pi:Y\to X
.

Proof.

Suppose
f
is a
(\Lambda,\Upsilon)
-coarse embedding and
\widetilde{\Lambda}
is the weak inverse of
\Lambda
.
Since
d(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))\leq 2i
, we have
d(x,x^{\prime})\leq\widetilde{\Lambda}(2i)
.
Thus if
\pi,\pi^{\prime}:Y\to X
are two functions as in Definition
3.17
, then the restrictions of
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
to
N_{i}(f(X))
are
\widetilde{\Lambda}(2i)
-close.
This ensures that for each
b\in f^{*}B(i)
,
d(\pi(p(b)),\pi^{\prime}(p(b)))\leq\widetilde{\Lambda}(2i)
. By Lemma
3.9
, the resulting pullback modules defined using
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are canonically isomorphic.
∎

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a coarse embedding between metric spaces, let
\mathbf{M}
be an
R
-module over
Y
, and let
\iota:f^{*}\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{M}
be the canonical identification. Then:

(1)

\iota(f^{*}\mathbf{M}(i))=\{m\in\mathbf{M}(i)\mid\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)\subseteq N_{i}(f(X))\}
.

(2)

If
m\in f^{*}\mathbf{M}(i)
, then
d_{\textrm{Haus}}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(\iota(m)),f(\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{M}}(m)))\leq i
.

In particular,
\iota:f^{*}\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{M}
has finite displacement over
f
.

Proof.

(
1
) is an immediate consequence of the definitions. For (
2
), we fix
m\in f^{*}\mathbf{M}(i)
. First, suppose that
x\in\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{M}}(m)
. Pick
b\in f^{*}B(i)
with
\pi(p(b))=x
and
\delta_{b}(m)\neq 0
. It follows that
p(b)\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(\iota(m))
. Since
b\in f^{*}B(i)
, we have
p(b)\in N_{i}(f(X))
, and so the definition of
\pi
ensures
d(f(x),p(b))\leq i
. Thus
f(\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{M}}(m))\subseteq N_{i}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(\iota(m)))
.

Conversely, suppose
y\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(\iota(m))
. Then there exists some
b\in B
with
p(b)=y
and
\delta_{b}(m)\neq 0
. Since
m\in f^{*}\mathbf{M}(i)
and
\delta_{b}(m)\neq 0
, we know
b\in f^{*}B(i)
and so
d(p(b),f(X))\leq i
. It follows that
\pi(y)\in\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{M}}(m)
and
d(f(\pi(y)),y)\leq i
, which shows
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(\iota(m))\subseteq N_{i}(f(\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{M}}(m)))
.
∎

Let
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
be
R
-modules over
Y
, and let
f:X\to Y
be a coarse embedding. Every map
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
corresponds to a map
f^{*}\phi:f^{*}\mathbf{M}\to f^{*}\mathbf{N}
by identifying
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
with
f^{*}\mathbf{M}
and
f^{*}\mathbf{N}
respectively via their canonical identifications.

Proposition 3.20
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a coarse embedding between metric spaces, and let
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
be
R
-modules over
Y
.

(1)

If
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
has finite displacement over
\id_{Y}
, then
f^{*}\phi:f^{*}\mathbf{M}\to f^{*}\mathbf{N}
has finite displacement over
\id_{X}
.

(2)

If
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
has uniform preimages, then so does
f^{*}\phi
.

Proof.

Suppose
f
is a
(\Lambda,\Upsilon)
-coarse embedding and
\widetilde{\Lambda}
is the weak inverse of
\Lambda
. We make use of both parts of Lemma
3.19
freely throughout the subsequent proof.

(
1
): Suppose
\phi
has displacement
\Phi
over
\id_{X}
and
m\in f^{*}\mathbf{M}(i)
. Then
\iota(f^{*}\phi(m))=\phi(\iota(m))\subseteq\phi(\mathbf{M}(i))\subseteq\mathbf{N}(\Phi(i))
and

\displaystyle\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(\iota(m)))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(\iota(m)))\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)+i}(f(\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{M}}(m)))\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)+i}(f(X)).

Thus
f^{*}\phi(m)\in f^{*}\mathbf{N}(\Phi(i)+i)
. Moreover,

\displaystyle f(\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{N}}(f^{*}\phi(m)))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)+i}(\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(\iota(m))))\subseteq N_{2\Phi(i)+2i}(f(\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{M}}(m))),

which ensures that
\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{N}}(f^{*}\phi(m))\subseteq N_{\widetilde{\Lambda}(2\Phi(i)+2i)}(\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{M}}(m))
. This shows
f^{*}\phi
has finite displacement.

(
2
): Suppose
\phi
has
\Omega
-uniform preimages. Suppose also that
n\in\im(f^{*}\phi)\cap f^{*}\mathbf{N}(i)
and
D\coloneqq\lceil\diam(\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{N}}(n))\rceil
. Then Lemma
3.19
ensures
\iota(n)\in\im(\phi)\cap\mathbf{N}(i)
and
\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\iota(n))\subseteq N_{i}(f(X))
.
Moreover, if
y,y^{\prime}\in\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\iota(n))
, then there exist
x,x^{\prime}\in\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{N}}(n)
such that
d(f(x),y)\leq i
and
d(f(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})\leq i
. Thus
d(y,y^{\prime})\leq 2i+d(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))\leq\Upsilon(D)+2i
, so that
\diam(\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\iota(n)))\leq\Upsilon(D)+2i
.

Setting
j\coloneqq\Omega(i,\lceil\Upsilon(D)+2i\rceil)
, there exists some
\iota(m)\in\mathbf{M}(j)
with
\phi(\iota(m))=\iota(n)
and

\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(\iota(m))\subseteq N_{j}(\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\iota(n)))\subseteq N_{i+j}(f(X)).

Thus
m\in f^{*}\mathbf{M}(i+j)
,
(f^{*}\phi)(m)=n
and

\displaystyle f(\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{M}}(m))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{i+j}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(\iota(m)))\subseteq N_{i+2j}(\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\iota(n)))\subseteq N_{2i+2j}(f(\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{N}}(n))).

Thus
\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{M}}(m)\subseteq N_{\widetilde{\Lambda}(2i+2j)}(\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{N}}(n))
. This demonstrates that
f^{*}\phi
has uniform preimages.
∎

When dealing with countable families of
R
-modules over metric spaces, it will be convenient to assume the following condition:

Definition 3.21
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\{\mathbf{M}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
, where each

\mathbf{M}_{j}=(M_{j},B_{j},\delta^{j},p_{j},\{B_{j}(i)\}_{i})

is an
R
-module over a metric space
X_{j}
. We say
\mathcal{M}
is
staggered
if for each
i
, the set
\{j\in\mathbb{N}\mid B_{j}(i)\neq\emptyset\}
is finite.

The following remark shows that there is no loss of generality in working with staggered collections:

Remark 3.22
.

For any countable collection
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\{\mathbf{M}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
of
R
-modules over metric spaces, we can always modify the filtration on each
\mathbf{M}_{j}
to ensure that
\mathcal{M}
is staggered. In detail, if
\mathbf{M}_{j}=(M_{j},B_{j},\delta^{j},p_{j},\{B_{j}(i)\}_{i})
, we set

B^{\prime}_{j}(i)=\begin{cases}0&\textrm{if}\;j>i,\\
B_{j}(i)&\textrm{if}\;j\leq i.\end{cases}

We let
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{j}\coloneqq(M_{j},B_{j},\delta^{j},p_{j},\{B_{j}^{\prime}(i)\}_{i})
. We note that
\mathbf{M}_{j}
and
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{j}
are canonically isomorphic. It is clear from the construction that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\{\mathbf{M}_{j}^{\prime}\}
is staggered.

The utility of working with staggered collections is the following:

Proposition 3.23
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\{\mathbf{M}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathcal{N}\coloneqq\{\mathbf{N}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be staggered, where each
\mathbf{M}_{j}
and
\mathbf{N}_{j}
is an
R
-module over
X_{j}
and
Y_{j}
respectively.
Suppose
\{\phi_{j}:\mathbf{M}_{j}\to\mathbf{N}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is a family of maps.

(1)

If each
\phi_{j}
has finite displacement over some
f_{j}:X_{j}\to Y_{j}
, then there exists a
\Psi
such that each
\phi_{j}
has displacement
\Psi
over
f_{j}
.

(2)

Suppose
X_{j}=Y_{j}
for each
j
and each
\phi_{j}
has uniform preimages. Then there exists an
\Omega
such that each
\phi_{j}
has
\Omega
-uniform preimages.

Proof.

Suppose

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{j}=(M_{j},B_{j},\delta^{j},p_{j},\{B_{j}(i)\})\;\text{and}\;\mathbf{N}_{j}=(N_{j},C_{j},\epsilon^{j},q_{j},\{C_{j}(i)\}).

Since
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
are staggered, we can pick an increasing function
\gamma:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
B_{j}(i)=C_{j}(i)=\emptyset
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
j>\gamma(i)
. In particular, this implies
\mathbf{M}_{j}(i)=\mathbf{N}_{j}(i)=0
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
j>\gamma(i)
.

(
1
): Suppose each
\phi_{j}:\mathbf{M}_{j}\to\mathbf{N}_{j}
has displacement
\Psi_{j}
over
f_{j}
. We set
\Psi(i)\coloneqq\max_{j\leq\gamma(i)}\Psi_{j}(i)
and claim that each
\phi_{j}
has displacement
\Psi
over
f_{j}
.
To see this, let
i\in\mathbb{N}
. In the case
\phi_{j}(\mathbf{M}_{j}(i))=0
there is nothing to show, so assume that
\phi_{j}(\mathbf{M}_{j}(i))\neq 0
. This implies that
j\leq\gamma(i)
. It follows that

\phi_{j}(\mathbf{M}_{j}(i))\subseteq\mathbf{N}_{j}(\Psi_{j}(i))\subseteq\mathbf{N}_{j}(\Psi(i)),

and for each
m\in\mathbf{M}_{j}(i)
, we have

\supp_{\mathbf{N}_{j}}(\phi_{j}(m))\subseteq N_{\Psi_{j}(i)}(f_{j}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}_{j}}(m)))\subseteq N_{\Psi(i)}(f_{j}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}_{j}}(m))).

This shows each
\phi_{j}
has displacement
\Psi
as required.

(
2
): Assume each
\phi_{j}
has
\Omega_{j}
-uniform preimages. Set
\Omega(i,D)\coloneqq\max_{j\leq\gamma(i)}\Omega_{j}(i,D)
. We claim each
\phi_{j}
has
\Omega
-uniform preimages.
Suppose
n\in\im(\phi_{j})\cap\mathbf{N}_{j}(i)
with
D=\lceil\diam(\supp_{\mathbf{N}_{j}}(n))\rceil
. If
n=0
there is nothing to show, so we assume
n\neq 0
. Since
\mathbf{N}_{j}(i)\neq 0
, we have
j\leq\gamma(i)
, and so
\Omega_{j}(i,D)\leq\Omega(i,D)\eqqcolon i^{\prime}
. As
\phi_{j}
has
\Omega_{j}
-uniform preimages, it follows there exists
m\in\mathbf{M}_{j}(i^{\prime})
with
\supp_{\mathbf{M}_{j}}(m)\subseteq N_{i^{\prime}}(\supp_{\mathbf{N}_{j}}(n))
and
\phi_{j}(m)=n
. This proves that
\phi_{j}
has
\Omega
-uniform preimages.
∎

Remark 3.24
.

It is clear from the proof of Proposition
3.23
that
\Psi
and
\Omega
depend only on
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{N}
and the functions
\{\Psi_{i}\}_{i}
and
\{\Omega_{i}\}_{i}
respectively, not on the actual maps
\{\phi_{i}\}
.

We can take the direct sum of
R
-modules over a metric space as follows. We use the following notation: if
f:X\to Z
and
g:Y\to Z
are functions, then
f\sqcup g:X\sqcup Y\to Z
is the function agreeing with
f
on
X\subseteq X\sqcup Y
and with
g
on
Y\subseteq X\sqcup Y
. The proof of Proposition
3.25
is routine, so will be omitted.

Proposition 3.25
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
and
\mathbf{N}=(N,C,\epsilon,q,\{C(i)\})
be
R
-modules over
X
. We identify the codomain
\Hom_{R}(M,R)
of
\delta
with a submodule of
\Hom_{R}(M\oplus N,R)\cong\Hom_{R}(M,R)\oplus\Hom_{R}(N,R)
in the obvious way, and similarly for
\epsilon
, so that
\delta\sqcup\epsilon
is a map
B\sqcup C\to\Hom(M\oplus N,R)
.

(1)

The direct sum

\mathbf{M}\oplus\mathbf{N}\coloneqq(M\oplus N,B\sqcup C,\delta\sqcup\epsilon,p\sqcup q,\{B(i)\sqcup C(i)\}_{i})

is an
R
-module over
X
.

(2)

(\mathbf{M}\oplus\mathbf{N})(i)=\mathbf{M}(i)\oplus\mathbf{N}(i)
.

(3)

\supp_{\mathbf{M}\oplus\mathbf{N}}(m\oplus n)=\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)\cup\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(n)
for all
m\in\mathbf{M}
and
n\in\mathbf{N}
.

We now introduce two finiteness properties that an
R
-module
\mathbf{M}
over
X
may possess:

Definition 3.26
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
be an
R
-module over
X
. We say that:

(1)

\mathbf{M}
is
proper
if, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and bounded set
F\subseteq X
, the intersection
p^{-1}(F)\cap B(i)
is finite.

(2)

\mathbf{M}
has
finite height
if there exists an
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}(i)
. The minimal such
i
is the
height
of
\mathbf{M}
.

The following proposition follows readily from the definitions:

Proposition 3.27
.

Let
\mathbf{M}
be an
R
-module over
X
.

(1)

If
\mathbf{N}\leq\mathbf{M}
is a geometric submodule and
\mathbf{M}
is proper (resp. of finite height), then so is
\mathbf{N}
.

(2)

If
f:X\to Y
is a coarse embedding and
\mathbf{M}
is proper (resp. of finite height), then so is
f_{*}\mathbf{M}
.

(3)

If
f:Y\to X
is a coarse embedding and
\mathbf{M}
is proper, then so is
f^{*}\mathbf{M}
.

(4)

If
f:Y\to X
is a coarse equivalence and
\mathbf{M}
is of finite height, then so is
f^{*}\mathbf{M}
. (This is not typically true if
f
is only a coarse embedding.)

(5)

If
\mathbf{N}
is an
R
-module over
X
and both
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
are proper (resp. of finite height), then so is
\mathbf{M}\oplus\mathbf{N}
.

4.
Projective modules over metric spaces

We define projective
R
-modules over a metric space, and we prove in Proposition
4.6
that they satisfy a lifting property. In Lemma
4.8
, we show that projective
RG
-modules induce projective
R
-modules over
G
. In Propositions
4.10
and
4.12
, we show
G
-equivariant maps between these induced modules have finite displacement and uniform preimages. Lemma
4.17
shows that the finite displacement retraction of a projective
R
-module over
X
is also a projective
R
-module over
X
, which plays an important role in the theory of coarse cohomological dimension, and is the main reason why it is necessary to work with projective
R
-modules over
X
rather than free
R
-modules over
X
.

Recall that, given an
R
-module
M
and an arbitrary indexing set
B
, a
projective basis
of
M
is a collection
\{(m_{b},\delta_{b})\}_{b\in B}
satisfying the following. For each
b\in B
,
m_{b}\in M
,
\delta_{b}\in M^{*}=\Hom_{R}(M,R)
, and for all
m\in M
,

\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(m)m_{b}=m,

where
\delta_{b}(m)=0
for all but finitely many
b\in B
. We call each
m_{b}
a
basis element
and each
\delta_{b}
a
coordinate
. The existence of a projective basis of
M
is equivalent to
M
being a projective
R
-module
[
Bro82
, Proposition I.8.2]
. We define a
projective
R
-module
\mathbf{M}
over
X
as follows:

Definition 4.1
.

Let
X
be a metric space and let
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
be an
R
-module over
X
.
We say a collection of elements
\{m_{b}\}_{b\in B}
of
M
is a
projective basis
of
\mathbf{M}
if there exists an increasing function
\Psi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that the following hold:

•

\{(m_{b},\delta_{b})\}_{b\in B}
is a projective basis of
M
;

•

m_{b}\in\mathbf{M}(\Psi(i))
for each
b\in B(i)
;

•

\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m_{b})\subseteq N_{\Psi(i)}(p(b))
for each
b\in B(i)
.

When the above holds, we say that
\{m_{b}\}_{b\in B}
has displacement
\Psi
. We say that
\mathbf{M}
is a
projective
R
-module over
X
if it has a projective basis, and we say that
\mathbf{M}
has
displacement
\Psi
if it has a projective basis of displacement
\Psi
.

We will shortly see in Proposition
4.6
that projective
R
-modules over
X
satisfy a lifting property.
An important special type of projective
R
-module over
X
is the following:

Definition 4.2
.

An
R
-module
\mathbf{M}
over
X
is a
free
R
-module over
X
if it admits a projective basis
\{m_{b}\}_{b\in B}
that is
R
-linearly independent, in which case we say that
\{m_{b}\}_{b\in B}
is a
basis
.

It is important to note that since the coordinates
\{\delta_{b}\}_{b}
are built into the structure of
\mathbf{M}
as an
R
-module over
X
, the choice of a projective basis of
\mathbf{M}
is extremely limited. For instance, in the case
\mathbf{M}
is a free
R
-module over
X
, the basis
\{m_{b}\}
is uniquely determined by the coordinates
\{\delta_{b}\}_{b}
, in which case
\{\delta_{b}\}_{b\in B}
is the dual basis of
\{m_{b}\}_{b\in B}
.

When working with a free
R
-module
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\mathcal{F})
over
X
, we may identify the indexing set
B
and the basis
\{m_{b}\}_{b\in B}
. We can thus say that
B
is a basis of
\mathbf{M}
and write
m=\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(m)b
for each
m\in\mathbf{M}
. This identification is not typically possible when working with a general projective
R
-module over
X
.

In practice, we often start with an
R
-module and specify the data defining the
R
-module structure over
X
and the projective basis at once. It will thus be convenient to write

\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\},\{m_{b}\})

to indicate that
\mathbf{M}
is the
R
-module over
X
given by
(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
, and that
\{m_{b}\}
is a projective basis of
\mathbf{M}
.

Remark 4.3
.

Given the data

\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\},\{m_{b}\})

as above such that
\{(m_{b},\delta_{b})\}
is a projective basis of
M
, conditions (
5
) and (
6
) in Definition
3.1
are automatically satisfied, and so
\mathbf{M}
is an
R
-module over
X
.

The following proposition complements Proposition
3.23
:

Proposition 4.4
.

Suppose
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\{\mathbf{M}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is staggered, where each
\mathbf{M}_{j}
is a projective
R
-module over
X_{j}
.
Then there exists a
\Phi
such that each
\mathbf{M}_{j}
is of displacement
\Phi
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{M}_{j}=(M_{j},B_{j},\delta^{j},p_{j},\{B_{j}(i)\})
.
We can pick an increasing function
\gamma:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
B_{j}(i)=\emptyset
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
j>\gamma(i)
.
Suppose that each
\mathbf{M}_{j}
has displacement
\Phi_{j}
.
Set
\Phi(i)\coloneqq\max_{j\leq\gamma(i)}\Phi_{j}(i)
. We claim each
\mathbf{M}_{j}
has displacement
\Phi
. Indeed, if
b\in B_{j}(i)
, then
j\leq\gamma(i)
. Thus
m_{b}\in\mathbf{M}_{j}(\Phi_{j}(i))\subseteq\mathbf{M}_{j}(\Phi(i))
and
\supp_{\mathbf{M}_{j}}(m_{b})\subseteq N_{\Phi_{j}(i)}(p(b))\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(p(b))
as required.
∎

The following lemma furnishes us with an important criterion for constructing finite displacement maps between
R
-modules over a metric space.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
X
and
Y
be metric spaces and suppose
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
is a projective
R
-module over
X
with projective basis
\{m_{b}\}_{b\in B}
. Suppose also
\mathbf{N}
is an
R
-module over
Y
and
f:X\to Y
is an arbitrary map.
Assume there is an increasing function
\Psi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and a collection
\{q_{b}\}_{b\in B}
of elements of
\mathbf{N}
such that for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
b\in B(i)
:

(1)

q_{b}\in\mathbf{N}(\Psi(i))
; and

(2)

\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(q_{b})\subseteq N_{\Psi(i)}(f(p(b)))
.

Then
m\mapsto\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(m)q_{b}
defines a map
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
of displacement
\Psi
over
f
.

Proof.

Since each
\delta_{b}
is a homomorphism and for each
m\in M
,
\delta_{b}(m)=0
for almost all
b
, we deduce that
\phi
is a well-defined
R
-module homomorphism. If
m\in\mathbf{M}(i)
, then all non-zero summands of
\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(m)q_{b}
are contained in
\mathbf{N}({\Psi(i)})
, hence
\phi(m)\in\mathbf{N}({\Psi(i)})
. It remains to show that if
y\in\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(m))
, then
y\in N_{\Psi(i)}(f(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)))
. Indeed, Lemma
3.4
implies that
y\in\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(q_{b})
for some
b\in B(i)
with
\delta_{b}(m)\neq 0
, so that
p(b)\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)
. Thus
y\in N_{\Psi(i)}(f(p(b)))\subseteq N_{\Psi(i)}(f(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)))
as required.
∎

We now show projective
R
-modules over a metric space satisfy the following lifting property:

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
X
and
Y
be metric spaces and let
f:X\to Y
be an
\Upsilon
-bornologous map.
Let
\mathbf{P}
be a projective
R
-module over
X
of displacement
\Phi
, and let
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
be
R
-modules over
Y
. Suppose
\psi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
has
\Omega
-uniform preimages.
If
\phi:\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{N}
has displacement
\Phi
over
f
and
\im(\phi)\subseteq\im(\psi)
, then there exists a map
\xi:\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{M}
of displacement
\Psi
over
f
such that
\psi\circ\xi=\phi
. Moreover,
\Psi
depends only on
\Upsilon
,
\Phi
and
\Omega
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

Figure 2
.
The lifting property for projective
R
-modules over a metric space.

Proof.

We suppose
\mathbf{P}=(P,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
and that
\{p_{b}\}_{b\in B}
is a projective basis of
\mathbf{P}
with displacement
\Phi
.
Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and set
i_{1}=\Phi(i)
and
i_{2}\coloneqq\Phi(i_{1})+\Upsilon(i_{1})
. Fixing
b\in B(i)
, we see
p_{b}\in\mathbf{P}(i_{1})
and
\supp_{\mathbf{P}}(p_{b})\subseteq N_{i_{1}}(p(b))
. Therefore
\phi(p_{b})\in\mathbf{N}(i_{2})
and

\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(p_{b}))\subseteq N_{\Phi(i_{1})}(f(\supp_{\mathbf{P}}(p_{b})))\subseteq N_{i_{2}}(f(p(b))).

In particular,
\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(p_{b}))
has diameter at most
2i_{2}
. Setting
i_{3}\coloneqq\Omega(i_{2},2i_{2})+i_{2}
, we see there exists some
m_{b}\in\mathbf{M}(i_{3})
with
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m_{b})\subseteq N_{i_{3}}(f(p(b)))
and
\psi(m_{b})=\phi(p_{b})
.

We can thus apply Lemma
4.5
to define a map
\xi:\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{M}
given by
p\mapsto\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(p)m_{b}
. By the choice of
\{m_{b}\}
and Lemma
4.5
,
\xi
is of displacement
\Psi
over
f
, where
\Psi
depends only on
\Upsilon
,
\Phi
and
\Omega
. For each
p\in\mathbf{P}
, we have

\psi(\xi(p))=\psi\left(\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(p)m_{b}\right)=\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(p)\psi(m_{b})=\phi\left(\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(p)p_{b}\right)=\phi(p)

as required.
∎

We now show that projective
RG
-modules induce projective
R
-modules over
G
.
As always, we assume that whenever
G
is a countable group, it is equipped with a proper left-invariant metric.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
G
be a countable group.
A
G
-induced projective
R
-module over
G
is a projective
R
-module
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\},\{m_{b}\})
over
G
satisfying the following:

•

M
is an
RG
-module;

•

G
acts on
B
so that
m_{gb}=gm_{b}
and
\delta_{gb}(m)=\delta_{b}({g^{-1}m})
for all
b\in B
and
g\in G
;

•

p:B\to G
is
G
-equivariant;

•

For each
i
,
B(i)=GB(i)
and
B(i)
consists of finitely many
G
-orbits.

It is clear that if
\mathbf{M}
is a
G
-induced projective
R
-module over
G
, then
\mathbf{M}(i)
is an
RG
-submodule of
\mathbf{M}
, and
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(gm)=g\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)
for all
b\in B
and
m\in\mathbf{M}
. Moreover, the underlying
RG
-module
M
is necessarily countably generated.

Lemma 4.8
.

Every countably generated projective
RG
-module
M
can be endowed with the structure of a
G
-induced projective
R
-module
\mathbf{M}
over
G
.

Proof.

We pick a countable generating set
C
of
M
as an
RG
-module, and let
\{C(i)\}
be a filtration of
C
with each
C(i)
finite. Let
F_{C}
be the free
RG
-module with
RG
-basis
\{e_{c}\mid c\in C\}
, and let
\pi:F_{C}\to M
be the surjection given by
\pi(e_{c})=c
. Since
M
is a projective
RG
-module, there exists a
G
-equivariant section
s:M\to F_{C}
of
\pi
.

Let
B=\{ge_{c}\mid g\in G,c\in C\}\subseteq F_{C}
, which is a basis of
F_{C}
as a free
R
-module, and let
\{\hat{\delta}_{b}\}_{b}
be the dual basis to
B
, i.e.

\delta_{b}(b^{\prime})=\begin{cases}1&\textrm{if}\;b=b^{\prime},\\
0&\textrm{if}\;b\neq b^{\prime}.\end{cases}

For each
b\in B
, define
\delta_{b}:M\to R
by
\hat{\delta}_{b}\circ s
.
For each
m\in M
, we have

s(m)=\sum_{b\in B}\hat{\delta}_{b}(s(m))b=\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(m)b.

Setting
m_{b}\coloneqq\pi(b)
for each
b\in B
, we see that
\mathcal{B}=\{(m_{b},\delta_{b})\}_{b\in B}
is a projective basis of
M
as an
R
-module. Since
s
and
\pi
are
G
-equivariant, we note that
m_{gb}=gm_{b}
and
\delta_{b}(g^{-1}m)=\delta_{gb}(m)
.

Set
B(i)\coloneqq\{ge_{c}\mid g\in G,c\in C(i)\}
, noting that
\mathcal{B}=\{B(i)\}
is a filtration of
B
. We define
p:B\to G
by
p(ge_{c})=g
for each
g\in G
and
c\in C
.
We define
\mathbf{M}
to be
(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
. All that remains to be shown is that
\{m_{b}\}
has displacement
\Phi
for some
\Phi
.
For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, as
C(i)
is finite, we can pick
n_{i}
such that
m_{e_{c}}\in\mathbf{M}(n_{i})
and
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m_{e_{c}})\subseteq N_{n_{i}}(1)
for all
c\in C(i)
. By
G
-equivariance, we see that
m_{b}\in\mathbf{M}(n_{i})
and
\supp(m_{b})\subseteq N_{n_{i}}(p(b))
for all
b\in B(i)
. Defining
\Phi(i)\coloneqq\max\{n_{j}\mid j\leq i\}
, we see that
\{m_{b}\}
is a projective basis of displacement
\Phi
.
∎

Remark 4.9
.

Let
G
be a countable group and let
\{M_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be a collection of countably generated
RG
-modules. The proof of Lemma
4.8
ensures that we can define a staggered family
\{\mathbf{M}_{j}\}
of
G
-induced projective modules over
G
in which each
\mathbf{M}_{j}
has underlying
RG
-module
M_{j}
.

We now discuss some properties of
G
-induced modules:

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
G
be a countable group.

(1)

Suppose
M
is a countably generated projective
RG
-module and that
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
are two
G
-induced projective
R
-modules over
G
with the same underlying
RG
-module
M
. Then
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
are canonically isomorphic.

(2)

Suppose
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
are
G
-induced projective
R
-modules over
G
. Then any
G
-equivariant map
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
has finite displacement over
\id_{G}
.

Proof.

(
1
): This is a special case of (
2
), since the identity map
\id_{M}
is
G
-equivariant with
G
-equivariant inverse.

(
2
): Suppose
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\},\{m_{b}\})
, and that
B(i)=GC(i)
for some finite
C(i)\subseteq B(i)
. Since
C(i)
is finite, we may choose an increasing function
\Psi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, the following hold:

•

\phi(m_{c})\in\mathbf{N}(\Psi(i))
for all
c\in C(i)
;

•

\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(m_{c}))\subseteq N_{\Psi(i)}(p(c))
for all
c\in C(i)
.

Thus for each
b\in B(i)
, we have
b=gc
for some
c\in C(i)
, so that
\phi(m_{b})=g\phi(m_{c})\in\mathbf{N}(\Psi(i))
and
\supp(\phi(m_{b}))\subseteq gN_{\Psi(i)}(p(c))=N_{\Psi(i)}(p(b))
. It follows from Lemma
4.5
that
\phi
has displacement
\Psi
over
\id_{G}
.
∎

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
G
be a countable group and let
\mathbf{M}
be a
G
-induced projective
R
-module over
G
. Then
\mathbf{M}
is proper, and is of finite height if and only if it is finitely generated as an
RG
-module.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
. The fact that
G
is proper follows from the fact that
B(i)
consists of finitely many
G
-orbits,
p
is
G
-equivariant and
G
is equipped with a proper metric.
If
\mathbf{M}
is of finite height, then
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}(i)
for some
i
, so that
\mathbf{M}
is generated by
B(i)
as an
R
-module. Since
B(i)
consists of finitely many
G
-orbits,
\mathbf{M}
is finitely generated as an
RG
-module.
For the converse, we note that
\mathbf{M}=\cup_{i}\mathbf{M}(i)
is a filtration of
\mathbf{M}
by
RG
-modules. Thus if
\mathbf{M}
is finitely generated as an
RG
-module, then
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}(i)
for some
i
sufficiently large.
∎

The uniform preimage property holds automatically for
G
-equivariant maps under a mild assumption on the ring
R
:

Proposition 4.12
.

If
R
is a Noetherian ring,
G
is a countable group and
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
is a
G
-equivariant map between
G
-induced projective
R
-modules over
G
, then
\phi
has uniform preimages.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{N}
is
G
-induced, it is proper by Proposition
4.11
. Thus for each
i,D\in\mathbb{N}
, the set
\Omega_{i,D}\coloneqq\{n\in\mathbf{N}(i)\mid\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(n)\subseteq N_{D}(e)\}
is a finitely generated
R
-module. Since
R
is Noetherian, the submodule
\Omega_{i,D}\cap\im(\phi)
is also finitely generated. Hence, there is a number
\Psi(i,D)
such that for each
n\in\Omega_{i,D}\cap\im(\phi)
