, there is some
m\in\mathbf{M}(\Psi(i,D))
with
\phi(m)=n
and
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)\subseteq N_{\Psi(i,D)}(e)
.

Now suppose
n\in\mathbf{N}(i)\cap\im(\phi)
with
\diam(\supp(n))\leq D
. If
g\in\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(n)
, we see that
g^{-1}n\in\Omega_{i,D}\cap\im(\phi)
. Then there is some
m\in\mathbf{M}(\Psi(i,D))
with
\phi(m)=g^{-1}n
and
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)\subseteq N_{\Psi(i,D)}(e)
. Hence we deduce that
\phi(gm)=n
, that
m\in\mathbf{M}(\Psi(i,D))
, and that
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(gm)\subseteq N_{\Psi(i,D)}(g)\subseteq N_{\Psi(i,D)}(\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(n))
. This demonstrates that
\phi
has uniform preimages.
∎

If
\mathbf{M}
is a projective
R
-module over
X
of height
i_{0}<\infty
and
\{m_{b}\}
is an arbitrary projective basis of
\mathbf{M}
, then it is possible that
m_{b}\neq 0
for some
b\in B\setminus B(i_{0})
. However, we can always pick a projective basis such that this does not occur:

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
be a projective
R
-module over
X
of displacement
\Phi
and height
i_{0}<\infty
. Then there exists a projective basis
\{m_{b}\}
of
\mathbf{M}
of displacement
\Phi
such that
m_{b}=0
for all
b\in B\setminus B(i_{0})
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}(i_{0})
, we see
\delta_{b}=0
for all
b\in B\setminus B(i_{0})
. By hypothesis, there exists a projective basis
\{m^{\prime}_{b}\}
of
\mathbf{M}
of displacement
\Phi
. Setting

m_{b}\coloneqq\begin{cases}m^{\prime}_{b}&\textrm{if}\;b\in B(i_{0}),\\
0&\textrm{if}\;b\in B\setminus B(i_{0}),\end{cases}

we see that
\{m_{b}\}
is the required projective basis of
\mathbf{M}
.
∎

We now show pullbacks, pushforwards and direct sums preserve projective
R
-modules over a metric space:

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
\mathbf{M}
be a projective
R
-module over
X
.

(1)

If
f:X\to Y
is a coarse embedding, then
f_{*}\mathbf{M}
is a projective
R
-module over
Y
.

(2)

If
f:Y\to X
is a coarse embedding, then
f^{*}\mathbf{M}
is a projective
R
-module over
Y
.

(3)

If
\mathbf{N}
is a projective
R
-module over
X
, then so is
\mathbf{M}\oplus\mathbf{N}
.

Proof.

We suppose
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
has a projective basis
\{m_{b}\}_{b\in B}
of displacement
\Phi
, and that
f
is a
(\Lambda,\Upsilon)
-coarse embedding.

(
1
): Let
\iota:\mathbf{M}\to f_{*}\mathbf{M}
be the canonical identification. For each
b\in B(i)
, we have
\iota(m_{b})\in f_{*}\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i))
and
\supp_{f_{*}\mathbf{M}}(\iota(m_{b}))=f(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m_{b}))\subseteq f(N_{\Phi(i)}(p(b)))\subseteq N_{\Upsilon(\Phi(i))}(f(p(b)))
, showing that
\{\iota(m_{b})\}
is a projective basis of
f_{*}\mathbf{M}
.

(
2
): Let
\iota:f^{*}\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{M}
be the canonical identification, and let
n_{b}\coloneqq\iota^{-1}(m_{b})
for each
b\in B
. For each
b\in f^{*}B(i)
, we have
d(p(b),f(Y))\leq i
and so
m_{b}\in\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i))
and
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m_{b})\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(p(b))\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)+i}(f(Y))
. By Lemma
3.19
, this implies
n_{b}\in f^{*}\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i)+i)
and

\displaystyle f(\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{M}}(n_{b}))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)+i}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m_{b}))\subseteq N_{2\Phi(i)+i}(p(b))\subseteq N_{2\Phi(i)+2i}((f\circ\pi\circ p)(b)),

where
\pi
is as in Definition
3.17
. Thus
\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{M}}(n_{b})\subseteq N_{\widetilde{\Lambda}(2\Phi(i)+2i)}(\pi(p(b)))
, showing that
\{n_{b}\}
is a projective basis of
f^{*}\mathbf{M}
.

(
3
) is straightforward.
∎

In the following lemma, we assume the product
X\times Y
of metric spaces is endowed with the
\ell_{1}
-product metric.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
and
\mathbf{N}=(N,C,\epsilon,q,\{C(i)\})
be projective
R
-modules over
X
and
Y
respectively of displacement
\Phi
.

(1)

The tensor product

\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{N}\coloneqq(M\otimes N,B\times C,\delta\otimes\epsilon,p\times q,\{B(i)\times C(i)\}_{i})

is a projective
R
-module over
X\times Y
of displacement
\Phi
, where for each
b\in B
and
c\in C
,
(\delta\otimes\epsilon)_{(b,c)}
is the map
M\otimes N\to R
given by
m\otimes n\mapsto\delta_{b}(m)\epsilon_{c}(n)
for all
m\in M
and
n\in N
.

(2)

\supp_{\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{N}}(m\otimes n)=\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)\times\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(n)
for all
m\in\mathbf{M}
and
n\in\mathbf{N}
.

(3)

\mathbf{M}(i)\otimes\mathbf{N}(i)\subseteq(\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{N})(i)\subseteq\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i))\otimes\mathbf{N}(\Phi(i))
.

(4)

If both
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
are proper (resp. of finite height), then so is
\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{N}
.

(5)

Suppose
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{L}
are projective
R
-modules over
W
and
Z
respectively. If
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{P}
and
\psi:\mathbf{N}\to\mathbf{L}
have finite displacement over bornologous maps
f:X\to W
and
g:Y\to Z
, then
\phi\otimes\psi:\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{N}\to\mathbf{P}\otimes\mathbf{L}
has finite displacement over
f\times g:X\times Y\to W\times Z
.

Proof.

(1): Let
\{m_{b}\}
and
\{n_{c}\}
be projective bases of
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
of displacement
\Phi
. If
z\in M\otimes N
, then
z=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}\otimes n_{i}
with
m_{i}\in M
and
n_{i}\in N
for all
i
. Thus,

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}\otimes n_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(m_{i})m_{b}\right)\otimes\left(\sum_{c\in C}\epsilon_{c}(n_{i})n_{c}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{(b,c)\in B}\delta_{b}(m_{i})\epsilon_{c}(n_{i})m_{b}\otimes n_{c}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{(b,c)\in B}(\delta\otimes\epsilon)_{(b,c)}(m_{i}\otimes n_{i})m_{b}\otimes n_{c}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(b,c)\in B}(\delta\otimes\epsilon)_{(b,c)}(z)m_{b}\otimes n_{c},

which verifies
M\otimes N
has projective basis
\{(m_{b}\otimes n_{c}),(\delta_{b}\otimes\epsilon_{c})\}_{(b,c)\in B\times C}
. Remark
4.3
now implies
\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{N}
is an
R
-module over
X\times Y
.
It remains to show
\{m_{b}\otimes n_{c}\}
has finite displacement, which will follow from (2) and (3).

(2): We note that
(x,y)\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{N}}(m\otimes n)
if and only if there exist
b\in p^{-1}(x)
and
c\in q^{-1}(y)
such that
\delta_{b}(m)\epsilon_{c}(n)\neq 0
. The latter is true if and only if
(x,y)\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)\times\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(n)
.

(3): If
z\in\mathbf{M}(i)\otimes\mathbf{N}(i)
, then it is clear that
(\delta_{b}\otimes\epsilon_{c})(z)=0
whenever
(b,c)\notin B(i)\times C(i)
, hence
z\in(\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{N})(i)
.
If
z\in(\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{N})(i)
, then
(\delta_{b}\otimes\epsilon_{c})(z)=0
whenever
(b,c)\notin B(i)\times C(i)
. Hence
z=\sum_{(b,c)\in B(i)\times C(i)}(\delta\otimes\epsilon)_{(b,c)}(z)m_{b}\otimes n_{c}
. For each
b\in B(i)
and
c\in C(i)
, we have
m_{b}\otimes n_{c}\in\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i))\otimes\mathbf{N}(\Phi(i))
. Thus
z\in\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i))\otimes\mathbf{N}(\Phi(i))
as required.

(4): This follows easily from the definitions.

(5): By increasing
\Phi
if necessary, we may assume that
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{L}
have displacement
\Phi
, and that
\phi
and
\psi
have displacement
\Phi
over
f
and
g
respectively. We pick
\Upsilon
such that
f
and
g
are
\Upsilon
-bornologous. Fix
i
and set
i_{k}=\Phi^{k}(i)
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thus

\displaystyle(\phi\otimes\psi)((\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{N})(i))

\displaystyle\subseteq(\phi\otimes\psi)(\mathbf{M}(i_{1})\otimes\mathbf{N}(i_{1}))\subseteq\phi(\mathbf{M}(i_{1}))\otimes\psi(\mathbf{N}(i_{1}))

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathbf{P}(i_{2})\otimes\mathbf{L}(i_{2})\subseteq(\mathbf{P}\otimes\mathbf{L})(i_{2}).

Moreover, suppose that
v\in(\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{N})(i)
and that
(w,z)\in\supp_{\mathbf{P}\otimes\mathbf{L}}((\phi\otimes\psi)(v))
.
Since
v=\sum_{(b,c)\in B(i)\times C(i)}(\delta_{b}\otimes\epsilon_{c})(v)m_{b}\otimes n_{c}
, Lemma
3.4
implies there exists some
(b,c)\in B(i)\times C(i)
with
(\delta_{b}\otimes\epsilon_{c})(v)\neq 0
and
(w,z)\in\supp_{\mathbf{P}\otimes\mathbf{L}}((\phi\otimes\psi)(m_{b}\otimes n_{c}))
. Therefore

\displaystyle(w,z)

\displaystyle\in\supp_{\mathbf{P}\otimes\mathbf{L}}(\phi(m_{b})\otimes\psi(n_{c}))

\displaystyle=\supp_{\mathbf{P}}(\phi(m_{b}))\times\supp_{\mathbf{L}}(\phi(n_{c}))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{i_{2}}(f(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m_{b})))\times N_{i_{2}}(g(\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(n_{c})))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{i_{2}}(f(N_{i_{1}}(p(b))))\times N_{i_{2}}(f(N_{i_{1}}(q(c))))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{i_{2}+\Upsilon(i_{1})}(f(p(b)))\times N_{i_{2}+\Upsilon(i_{1})}(g(q(c)))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{j}(f(p(b)),g(q(c))),

where
j\coloneqq 2i_{2}+2\Upsilon(i_{1})
. As
(\delta_{b}\otimes\epsilon_{c})(v)\neq 0
, we see that
(p(b),q(c))\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{N}}(v)
, and so
\supp_{\mathbf{P}\otimes\mathbf{L}}((\phi\otimes\psi)(v))\subseteq N_{j}((f\times g)(\supp_{\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{N}}(v)))
. Thus
\phi\otimes\psi
has finite displacement over
f\times g
.
∎

Given a homomorphism
f:R\to S
between commutative rings and an
R
-module
M
, we have
M\otimes_{R}S
is an
S
-module, where
S
is thought of as a left
R
-module via
r\cdot s=f(r)s
. We can thus convert a projective
R
-module over
X
to a projective
S
-module over
X
as follows:

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
f:R\to S
be a ring homomorphism and let
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
be a projective
R
-module over
X
of displacement
\Phi
.

(1)

Then

\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S=(M\otimes_{R}S,B,\epsilon,p,\{B(i)\})

is a projective
S
-module over
X
, where
\epsilon_{b}
is given by
\epsilon_{b}(m\otimes s)=f(\delta_{b}(m))s
.

(2)

\mathbf{M}(i)\otimes_{R}S\subseteq(\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S)(i)\subseteq\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i))\otimes_{R}S
.

(3)

\supp_{\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S}(m\otimes s)\subseteq\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)
for all
m\in\mathbf{M}
and
s\in S
.

(4)

If
\mathbf{N}
is an
R
-module over
Y
and
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
has finite displacement over
f
, then
\phi\otimes\id_{S}:\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S\to\mathbf{N}\otimes_{R}S
has finite displacement over
f
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\{m_{b}\}
is a projective basis of
\mathbf{M}
and that
\{m_{b}\}
has displacement
\Phi
.

(
1
): For each
z\in\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S
, we have
z=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}\otimes s_{i}
, where each
m_{i}\in\mathbf{M}
and
s_{i}\in S
. Therefore

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(m_{i})m_{b}\right)\otimes s_{i}=\sum_{b\in B}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(\delta_{b}(m_{i}))s_{i}(m_{b}\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in B}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{b}(m_{i}\otimes s_{i})(m_{b}\otimes 1)=\sum_{b\in B}\epsilon_{b}(z)(m_{b}\otimes 1),

showing that
\{(m_{b}\otimes 1,\epsilon_{b})\}
is a projective basis of
M\otimes_{R}S
. Remark
4.3
now implies
\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S
is an
S
-module over
X
.
It remains to show
\{m_{b}\otimes 1\}
has finite displacement, which will follow from (
2
) and (
3
).

(
2
): If
m\in\mathbf{M}(i)
and
s\in S
, then
\epsilon_{b}(m\otimes s)=f(\delta_{b}(m))s=0
for all
b\in B\setminus B(i)
. This implies
\epsilon_{b}(\mathbf{M}(i)\otimes_{R}S)=0
for all
b\in B\setminus B(i)
, and so
\mathbf{M}(i)\otimes_{R}S\subseteq(\mathbf{M}\otimes S)(i)
as required.
Now suppose
z\in(\mathbf{M}\otimes S)(i)
. Then
z=\sum_{b\in B(i)}\epsilon_{b}(z)(m_{b}\otimes 1)
. Since for each
b\in B(i)
we know that
m_{b}\in\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i))
, we deduce
z\in\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i))\otimes_{R}S
as required.

(
3
): Suppose
x\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S}(m\otimes s)
. Then there is some
b\in p^{-1}(x)
with
\epsilon_{b}(m\otimes s)=f(\delta_{b}(m))s\neq 0
. Thus
\delta_{b}(m)\neq 0
, and so
x=p(b)\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)
.

(
4
): By increasing
\Phi
, we can assume
\mathbf{N}
has displacement
\Phi
and
\phi
has displacement
\Phi
over
f
. We pick
\Upsilon
so that
f
is
\Upsilon
-bornologous. Fix
i\in\mathbb{N}
and let
i_{k}\coloneqq\Phi^{k}(i)
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

\displaystyle(\phi\otimes\id_{S})((\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S)(i))

\displaystyle\subseteq(\phi\otimes\id_{S})(\mathbf{M}(i_{1})\otimes_{R}S)\subseteq\mathbf{N}(i_{2})\otimes_{R}S\subseteq(\mathbf{N}\otimes_{R}S)(i_{2}).

Suppose that
z\in(\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S)(i)
and that
y\in\supp_{\mathbf{N}\otimes_{R}S}((\phi\otimes\id)(z))
. Since
z=\sum_{b\in B(i)}\epsilon_{b}(z)(m_{b}\otimes 1)
, there is some
b\in B(i)
with
\epsilon_{b}(z)\neq 0
and
y\in\supp_{\mathbf{N}\otimes_{R}S}((\phi\otimes\id)(m_{b}\otimes 1))
. Thus

\displaystyle y\in\supp_{\mathbf{N}\otimes_{R}S}(\phi(m_{b})\otimes 1)

\displaystyle\subseteq\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(m_{b}))\subseteq N_{i_{2}}(f(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m_{b})))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{i_{2}}(f(N_{i_{1}}(p(b))))\subseteq N_{j}(f(p(b))),

where
j\coloneqq i_{2}+\Upsilon(i_{1})
.
Since
\epsilon_{b}(z)\neq 0
, we see
p(b)\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S}(z)
and so
\supp_{\mathbf{N}\otimes_{R}S}((\phi\otimes\id)(z))\subseteq N_{j}(f(\supp_{\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S}(z)))
. This demonstrates that
\phi\otimes\id_{S}
has finite displacement over
f
.
∎

Given an
R
-module
M
and a submodule
N
, a homomorphism
r:M\to M
is said to be a
retraction onto
N
if
\im(r)=N
and
r|_{N}=\id_{N}
.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
\mathbf{M}
be a projective
R
-module over
X
and let
\mathbf{N}\leq\mathbf{M}
be a geometric submodule. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathbf{N}
is a projective
R
-module over
X
.

(2)

There exists a finite displacement retraction
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{M}
onto
\mathbf{N}
.

(3)

There exists a geometric submodule
\mathbf{L}\leq\mathbf{M}
such that
\mathbf{M}
splits as a direct sum
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{N}\oplus\mathbf{L}
with finite displacement projections.

Remark 4.18
.

Condition (
1
) is significantly more restrictive than merely requiring that the underlying
R
-module
N
is projective. The key point is that as
\mathbf{N}\leq\mathbf{M}
, the coordinates of
\mathbf{N}
are obtained by restricting the coordinates of
\mathbf{M}
. The condition that
\mathbf{N}
is a projective
R
-module thus requires that there is a projective basis of
\mathbf{N}
whose coordinates are compatible with the coordinates of
\mathbf{M}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\mathcal{F})
and
\{m_{b}\}_{b\in B}
is a projective basis of
\mathbf{M}
.

(
1
)
\implies
(
2
): Assume
\{n_{b}\}_{b\in B}
is a projective basis of
\mathbf{N}
. We note that by Definition
3.5
, the indexing of
\mathbf{N}
is identical to the indexing set of
\mathbf{M}
. We define a map
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{M}
by
\phi(m)=\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(m)n_{b}
. This map has finite displacement because
\{n_{b}\}
is a projective basis of
\mathbf{N}
, and so Lemma
4.5
applies. Since
\im(\phi)=\mathbf{N}
and for each
n\in\mathbf{N}
, we have

\phi(n)=\phi\left(\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(n)m_{b}\right)=\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(n)n_{b}=n,

we conclude that
\phi
is indeed a finite displacement retraction onto
\mathbf{N}
.

(
2
)
\implies
(
3
): Setting
\mathbf{L}=\ker(\phi)
, we see that
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{N}\oplus\mathbf{L}
and the projections to
\mathbf{N}
and to
\mathbf{L}
are given by
\phi
and
\id_{\mathbf{M}}-\phi
, which clearly have finite displacement.

(
3
)
\implies
(
1
): Let
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
be the finite displacement projection to
\mathbf{N}
. For each
b\in B
, set
n_{b}=\phi(m_{b})
. Then for each
n\in\mathbf{N}
, we have

\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(n)n_{b}=\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(n)\phi(m_{b})=\phi\left(\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(n)m_{b}\right)=\phi(n)=n,

so that
\{(n_{b},\delta_{b})\}_{b\in B}
is a projective basis of the
R
-module
N
. Pick
\Phi
such that both
\{m_{b}\}
and
\phi
have displacement
\Phi
.
Thus for each
b\in B(i)
we have

n_{b}=\phi(m_{b})\in\phi(\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i)))\subseteq\mathbf{N}(\Phi^{2}(i))

and

\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(n_{b})=\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(n_{b})=\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(\phi(m_{b}))\subseteq N_{\Phi^{2}(i)}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m_{b}))\subseteq N_{\Phi^{2}(i)+\Phi(i)}(p(b)),

showing that
\{n_{b}\}_{b\in B}
is a projective basis of
\mathbf{N}
.
∎

5.
Hom-sets and duality

If
Y
is a locally finite simplicial complex and
C_{k}(Y)
is the module of simplicial
k
-chains of
Y
, there are three modules that can be associated to
C_{k}(Y)
:

(1)

the module
C^{k}(Y)
of all
k
-cochains;

(2)

the submodule
C^{k}_{c}(Y)\leq C^{k}(Y)
of compactly supported
k
-cochains;

(3)

the module
C^{\lf}_{k}(Y)
of infinite, locally finite chains, which contains
C_{k}(Y)
as a submodule.

We refer the reader to
[
Geo08
, Part III]
for a helpful discussion of these topics.

In this section, we construct three analogous modules for each proper projective
R
-module
\mathbf{M}
over a metric space
X
, denoted
\mathbf{M}^{*}
,
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
and
\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
respectively. We also study the hom-set
\Homfd(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N})
of finite displacement maps between
R
-modules over metric spaces, which will be used to define coarse cohomology of metric spaces. In general, neither
\mathbf{M}^{*}
nor
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
is an
R
-module over
X
. However, in the case
\mathbf{M}
is a finite-height proper projective
R
-module over
X
, we show in Proposition
5.20
that
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
is also a finite-height proper projective
R
-module over
X
. This is an analogue of the fact that the dual of a finitely generated projective
RG
-module is also a finitely generated projective
RG
-module, and will play a key role in our study of coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
spaces in Section
12
.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
be
R
-modules over a metric space
X
, with underlying
R
-modules
M
and
N
. Let
\Homfd(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N})
denote the submodule of
\Hom_{R}(M,N)
consisting of homomorphisms with finite displacement over
\id_{X}
.

Remark 5.2
.

If
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
are canonically isomorphic in the sense of Definition
3.7
, then we see that
\Homfd(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N})=\Homfd(\mathbf{M}^{\prime},\mathbf{N})
since they are equal as subsets of
\Hom_{R}(M,N)
. Similarly, if
\mathbf{N}
and
\mathbf{N}^{\prime}
are canonically isomorphic, then
\Homfd(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N})=\Homfd(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N}^{\prime})
.
In particular, suppose
f,g:X\to Y
are close,
\mathbf{M}
is an
R
-module over
X
, and
\mathbf{L}
is an
R
-module over
Y
. Then Lemma
3.16
implies that
\Homfd(\mathbf{L},f_{*}\mathbf{M})=\Homfd(\mathbf{L},g_{*}\mathbf{M})
and
\Homfd(f_{*}\mathbf{M},\mathbf{L})=\Homfd(g_{*}\mathbf{M},\mathbf{L})

The following is a reformulation of Lemma
3.15
:

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
X
and
Y
be metric spaces and let
f:X\to Y
be a coarse embedding. Suppose
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
are
R
-modules over
X
, and
\mathbf{L}
is an
R
-module over
Y
. Let
\iota:\mathbf{N}\to f_{*}\mathbf{N}
be the canonical identification. If
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{L}
has finite displacement over
f
, then
\phi
induces a homomorphism
\phi^{*}:\Homfd(\mathbf{L},f_{*}\mathbf{N})\to\Homfd(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N})
given by
\psi\mapsto\iota^{-1}\circ\psi\circ\phi
.
In particular, when
X=Y
and
f=\id_{X}
, then
\phi
induces a homomorphism
\phi^{*}:\Homfd(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{N})\to\Homfd(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N})
given by
\psi\mapsto\psi\circ\phi
.

Another type of hom-set we work with is the following:

Definition 5.4
.

Let
X
be a metric space, let
\mathbf{M}
be an
R
-module over
X
, and let
N
be an
R
-module. A homomorphism
\phi:M\to N
has
locally bounded support
if for each
i
, there exists a bounded subset
F_{i}\subseteq X
such that
\phi(m)=0
for all
m\in\mathbf{M}(i)
with
\supp(m)\subseteq X\setminus F_{i}
. We then define

\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{M},N)\coloneqq\left\{\phi\in\Hom_{R}(M,N)\;\middle|\;\phi\text{ has locally bounded support}\right\}.

In the case
N=R
, we define
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}\coloneqq\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{M},R)
and
\mathbf{M}^{*}\coloneqq\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{M},R)
, and we observe that
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}\leq\mathbf{M}^{*}
.

We now show that hom-sets of the form
\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{M},N)
are special cases of hom-sets of the form
\Homfd(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N})
, ensuring results proved concerning the latter apply to the former.

Definition 5.5
.

An
R
-module
\mathbf{N}
over
X
is said to be
constant
if:

(1)

\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\mathbf{N})=\{x_{0}\}
for some
x_{0}\in X
; and

(2)

\mathbf{N}(i)=\mathbf{N}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Any
R
-module
N
can be endowed with the structure of a constant
R
-module
\mathbf{N}
over any (non-empty) metric space. Applying Lemma
3.9
, we see that all constant
R
-modules over
X
with the same underlying
R
-module are canonically isomorphic. Consequently, Remark
5.2
ensures that if
\mathbf{M}
is an
R
-module over
X
and if
\mathbf{N}
and
\mathbf{N}^{\prime}
are two constant
R
-modules over
X
with the same underlying
R
-module, then
\Homfd(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N})=\Homfd(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N}^{\prime})
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
X
be a metric space, let
\mathbf{M}
be an
R
-module over
X
, and let
N
be an
R
-module. Then
\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{M},N)=\Homfd(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N})
, where
\mathbf{N}
is some (equivalently any) constant
R
-module over
X
with underlying
R
-module
N
.

Proof.

Pick
x_{0}\in X
such that
\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\mathbf{N})=\{x_{0}\}
.

Suppose
\phi:M\to N
has locally bounded support. For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, choose a bounded
F_{i}\subseteq X
such that
\phi(m)=0
for all
m\in\mathbf{M}(i)
with
\supp(m)\subseteq X\setminus F_{i}
. For each
i
, set
\Phi(i)\coloneqq\lceil\sup_{x\in F_{i}}d(x_{0},x)\rceil
. We claim
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
has displacement
\Phi
.
Clearly
\phi(\mathbf{M}(i))\subseteq\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{N}(\Phi(i))
. Suppose
m\in\mathbf{M}(i)
. It remains to show
\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(m))\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m))
. If
\phi(m)=0
we are done, so assume
\phi(m)\neq 0
. Then
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)\cap F_{i}\neq\emptyset
, and so
\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(m))=\{x_{0}\}\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m))
as required.

Conversely, suppose
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
has displacement
\Phi
. Set
F_{i}\coloneqq N_{\Phi(i)}(x_{0})
, which is a bounded subset of
X
. Suppose
m\in\mathbf{M}(i)
and
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)\subseteq X\setminus F_{i}
. Then

\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(m))\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m))\subseteq X\setminus\{x_{0}\}.

If
\phi(m)\neq 0
, then
\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(m))=\{x_{0}\}
, which is a contradiction. Thus
\phi(m)=0
, and so
\phi
has locally bounded support.
∎

It is easy to see that if
\mathbf{L}
is a constant
R
-module over
X
and
f:X\to Y
is arbitrary, then
f_{*}\mathbf{L}
is a constant
R
-module over
Y
. Combined with Proposition
5.7
, this implies:

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
X
and
Y
be metric spaces and let
f:X\to Y
be a coarse embedding. Suppose
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
are
R
-modules over
X
and
Y
respectively, and
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
has finite displacement over
f
. Then for each
R
-module
L
,
\phi
induces the dual map
\phi^{*}:\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{N},L)\to\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{M},L)
via
\alpha\mapsto\alpha\circ\phi
.

For the remainder of this section, we focus on projective
R
-modules over
X
, which have a richer duality theory than arbitrary
R
-modules over
X
. We first establish counterparts of the support function
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}
that apply to elements of
\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{M},N)
:

Definition 5.8
.

Let
X
be a metric space, let
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
be a projective
R
-module over
X
with projective basis
\{m_{b}\}
, and let
N
be an
R
-module. We assume that
\{m_{b}\}
satisfies the conclusion of Lemma
4.13
when
\mathbf{M}
has finite height; by Lemma
4.13
, there is no loss of generality in assuming this.
For each
R
-module homomorphism
\alpha:\mathbf{M}\to N
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the
local support of
\alpha
at height
i
to be

\supp(\alpha,i)=\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(\alpha,i)\coloneqq\{p(b)\in X\mid b\in B(i),\alpha(m_{b})\neq 0\}.

It is important to note that
\supp(\alpha,i)
depends upon the choice of projective basis
\{m_{b}\}
. However, as we will observe in Remark
5.12
,
\supp(\alpha,i)
is independent of
\{m_{b}\}
up to some error depending on the displacement of
\{m_{b}\}
. We assume that some
\{m_{b}\}
as in Definition
5.8
is fixed.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
X
be a metric space, let
\mathbf{M}
be a projective
R
-module over
X
, and let
N
be an
R
-module. Then
\alpha:\mathbf{M}\to N
has locally bounded support if and only if
\supp(\alpha,i)
is bounded for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
In particular, when
\mathbf{M}
is proper,
\phi
has locally bounded support if and only if
\supp(\alpha,i)
is finite for each
i
.

Proof.

Assume that the projective basis
\{m_{b}\}
as in Definition
5.8
has displacement
\Phi
. We first suppose
\alpha
has locally bounded supports. Fix
i\in\mathbb{N}
and pick a bounded set
F\subseteq X
such that
\alpha(m)=0
for all
m\in\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i))
with
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)\subseteq X\setminus F
. Suppose
x\in\supp(\alpha,i)
. Then there is some
b\in B(i)
with
p(b)=x
and
\alpha(m_{b})\neq 0
. Since
m_{b}\in\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i))
and
\alpha(m_{b})\neq 0
, we deduce
\supp(m_{b})\cap F\neq\emptyset
. As
\supp(m_{b})\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(p(b))=N_{\Phi(i)}(x)
, we deduce that
x\in N_{\Phi(i)}(F)
. Thus
\supp(\alpha,i)\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(F)
is bounded.

Conversely, suppose that
\supp(\alpha,i)
is bounded for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Fix
i\in\mathbb{N}
and let
F\coloneqq\supp(\alpha,i)
, which is bounded by assumption. Suppose
m\in\mathbf{M}(i)
with
\supp(m)\subseteq X\setminus F
. It is sufficient to show
\alpha(m)=0
. To see this, we know that
m=\sum_{b\in B(i)}\delta_{b}(m)m_{b}
with almost all terms zero. If
\alpha(m_{b})\neq 0
for some
b\in B(i)
, then
p(b)\in\supp(\alpha,i)\subseteq F
, and so
\delta_{b}(m)=0
because
\supp(m)\subseteq X\setminus F
. Thus for each
b\in B(i)
, we have
\delta_{b}(m)\alpha(m_{b})=0
. This shows that
\alpha(m)=0
as required.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
X
be a metric space, let
\mathbf{M}
be a projective
R
-module over
X
, and let
N
be an
R
-module. If
\sigma\in\mathbf{M}(i)
,
\alpha\in\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{M},N)
and
\alpha(\sigma)\neq 0
, then
\supp(\sigma)\cap\supp(\alpha,i)\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Since
\sigma\in\mathbf{M}(i)
, we have
\sigma=\sum_{b\in B(i)}\delta_{b}(\sigma)m_{b}
. If
\alpha(\sigma)\neq 0
, then there is some
b\in B(i)
with
\delta_{b}(\sigma)\neq 0
and
\alpha(m_{b})\neq 0
. Thus
p(b)\in\supp(\sigma)\cap\supp(\alpha,i)
.
∎

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
X
be a metric space, let
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
be projective
R
-modules over
X
of displacement
\Phi
, and let
L
be an
R
-module. Suppose
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
has displacement
\Phi
over
\id_{X}
. For all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{N},L)
,

\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(\phi^{*}\alpha,i)\subseteq N_{\Phi^{2}(i)+\Phi(i)}(\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\alpha,\Phi^{2}(i))),

where
\phi^{*}
is as in Proposition
5.7
.

Proof.

Suppose
x\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(\phi^{*}(\alpha),i)
. Then there is some
b\in B(i)
with
p(b)=x
and
\phi^{*}(\alpha)(m_{b})=\alpha(\phi(m_{b}))\neq 0
. Since
m_{b}\in\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i))
, we see that
\phi(m_{b})\in\mathbf{N}(\Phi^{2}(i))
and that
\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(m_{b}))\subseteq N_{\Phi^{2}(i)}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m_{b}))\subseteq N_{\Phi^{2}(i)+\Phi(i)}(x)
. Lemma
5.10
thus implies that since
\alpha(\phi(m_{b}))\neq 0
, we have

\emptyset\neq\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\alpha,\Phi^{2}(i))\cap\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(m_{b}))\subseteq\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\alpha,\Phi^{2}(i))\cap N_{\Phi^{2}(i)+\Phi(i)}(x).

It follows that
x\in N_{\Phi^{2}(i)+\Phi(i)}(\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\alpha,\Phi^{2}(i)))
.
∎

Remark 5.12
.

Applying Lemma
5.11
in the situation
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{N}
,
\phi=\id
, and
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
are equipped with two different projective basis, each of displacement
\Phi
, we see that up to an error of at most
\Phi^{2}(i)+\Phi(i)
,
\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(\alpha,i)
is independent of the choice of projective basis
\{m_{b}\}
.

For the remainder of this section, we focus on
\mathbf{M}^{*}=\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{M},R)
and
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}=\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{M},R)
, where
\mathbf{M}
is a projective
R
-module over
X
. We first make the following observation:

Remark 5.13
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
be an
R
-module over
X
. If
b\in B
, then
\delta_{b}(m)\neq 0
only if
p(b)\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)
. Consequently, if
m\in\mathbf{M}
with
\supp(m)\subseteq X\setminus\{p(b)\}
, then
\delta_{b}(m)=0
. This shows
\delta_{b}\in\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
.

While it is not typically the case that either
\mathbf{M}^{*}
or
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
are themselves projective
R
-modules over
X
, we will see the coordinates
\{\delta_{b}\}
of
\mathbf{M}
are a sort of “projective Schauder basis” of
\mathbf{M}^{*}
. To make this precise, we introduce the following convention regarding infinite sums:

Notation 5.14
.

Let
M
be an
R
-module, let
B
be an arbitrary indexing set and
\phi\in M^{*}=\Hom_{R}(M,R)
. Suppose that for each
b\in B
, we have elements
\phi_{b}\in M^{*}
and
r_{b}\in R
. We write the expression

\phi=\sum_{b\in B}r_{b}\phi_{b},

to indicate that for each
m\in M
, we have
\phi(m)=\sum_{b\in B}r_{b}\phi_{b}(m)
and that
r_{b}\phi_{b}(m)=0
for almost all
b\in B
.

In the case
B
is finite, this notation is clearly consistent with the sum
\sum_{b\in B}r_{b}\phi_{b}
of finitely many elements of the
R
-module
M^{*}
.

This yields a useful description of elements of
\mathbf{M}^{*}
:

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
be a projective
R
-module over
X
with projective basis
\{m_{b}\}
.

(1)

For each
\phi\in\mathbf{M}^{*}
,

\phi=\sum_{b\in B}\phi(m_{b})\delta_{b}.

(2)

For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
\phi\in\mathbf{M}^{*}
, we have
\phi-\phi_{i}\in\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i))
, where
\phi_{i}\coloneqq\sum_{b\in B(i)}\phi(m_{b})\delta_{b}
and
\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i))\coloneqq\{\phi\in\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}\mid\phi(\mathbf{M}(i))=0\}
.

Proof.

Let
m\in\mathbf{M}
. Since
\delta_{b}(m)=0
for almost all
b\in B
, we have

\phi(m)=\phi\left(\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(m)m_{b}\right)=\left(\sum_{b\in B}\phi(m_{b})\delta_{b}\right)(m),

as required.
If
\phi\in\mathbf{M}^{*}
and
m\in\mathbf{M}(i)
, then

\displaystyle\phi(m)=\phi\left(\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(m)m_{b}\right)

\displaystyle=\phi\left(\sum_{b\in B(i)}\delta_{b}(m)m_{b}\right)=\left(\sum_{b\in B(i)}\phi(m_{b})\delta_{b}\right)(m)=\phi_{i}(m),

so that
\phi-\phi_{i}\in\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i))
.
∎

In the case
\mathbf{M}
is a proper projective
R
-module over
X
, we can define an important submodule of
\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c},R)
as follows:

Definition 5.16
.

Let
\mathbf{M}
be a proper projective
R
-module over
X
. We set

\mathbf{M}^{\lf}\coloneqq\{\psi\in\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c},R)\mid\psi(\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i)))=0\text{ for some $i$}\}.

We now show that
\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
can be endowed with the structure of an
R
-module over
X
:

Proposition 5.17
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\mathcal{F})
be a proper projective
R
-module over
X
with projective basis
\{m_{b}\}
of displacement
\Phi
.

(1)

The double-dual map
\tau:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
given by
\tau(m)(\phi)=\phi(m)
is injective.

Henceforth, we identify
\mathbf{M}
with its image under
\tau
.

(2)

For each
\psi\in\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
and
i
is sufficiently large such that
\psi(\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i)))=0
, we have

\psi=\sum_{b\in B}\psi(\delta_{b})m_{b}=\sum_{b\in B(i)}\psi(\delta_{b})m_{b}.

(3)

\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
can be endowed with the structure of an
R
-module over
X
given by

\mathbf{M}^{\lf}=(\mathbf{M}^{\lf},B,\hat{\delta},p,\mathcal{F}),

where
\hat{\delta}_{b}(\psi)=\psi(\delta_{b})
for all
b\in B
. Moreover, after identifying
\mathbf{M}
with its image under
\tau
, we have that
\mathbf{M}
is a geometric submodule of
\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
.

(4)

For all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\{\psi\in\mathbf{M}^{\lf}\mid\psi(\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i)))=0\}\subseteq\mathbf{M}^{\lf}(i)\subseteq\{\psi\in\mathbf{M}^{\lf}\mid\psi(\Ann(\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i))))=0\}.

Proof.

(
1
): We first show that for each
m\in\mathbf{M}
, the element
\tau(m)\in\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c},R)
given by
\tau(m)(\phi)=\phi(m)
is actually in
\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
. Indeed, we know that
m\in\mathbf{M}(i)
for some
i\in\mathbb{N}
. If
\phi\in\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i))
, we have
\tau(m)(\phi)=\phi(m)=0
. Thus
\tau(m)(\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i)))=0
, and so
\tau(m)\in\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
. To see
\tau
is injective, we observe that if
m\in\mathbf{M}
such that
\tau(m)=0
, then

m=\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(m)m_{b}=\sum_{b\in B}\tau(m)(\delta_{b})m_{b}=0.

(
2
): Let
\psi\in\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
with
\psi(\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i)))=0
, and suppose
\phi\in\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
. Note that for
b\in B\setminus B(i)
, we have
\delta_{b}\in\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i))
, and so
\psi(\delta_{b})=0
. Since
\mathbf{M}
is proper, it follows from Proposition
5.9
that
\phi(m_{b})=0
for almost all
b\in B(i)
; thus almost all terms of
\phi_{i}\coloneqq\sum_{b\in B(i)}\phi(m_{b})\delta_{b}
are zero. Lemma
5.15
implies that
\phi-\phi_{i}\in\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i))
, and so
\psi(\phi)=\psi(\phi_{i})
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\psi(\phi)=\psi\left(\sum_{b\in B(i)}\phi(m_{b})\delta_{b}\right)=\sum_{b\in B(i)}\phi(m_{b})\psi(\delta_{b})=\left(\sum_{b\in B(i)}\psi(\delta_{b})\tau(m_{b})\right)(\phi).

Since this holds for all
\phi\in\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
, we conclude that
\psi=\sum_{b\in B(i)}\psi(\delta_{b})\tau(m_{b})
. Since we are identifying
\mathbf{M}
with its image under
\tau
, we have
\psi=\sum_{b\in B(i)}\psi(\delta_{b})m_{b}
.

(
3
):
To show
(\mathbf{M}^{\lf},B,\hat{\delta},p,\mathcal{F})
is an
R
-module over
X
, we need to verify (
5
) and (
6
) of Definition
3.1
. Firstly, if
\psi\in\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
with
\hat{\delta}_{b}(\psi)=\psi(\delta_{b})=0
for all
b\in B
, then
\psi=0
by (
2
). Secondly, if
\psi\in\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
, then
\psi(\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i)))=0
for some
i
. Thus for
b\in B\setminus B(i)
,
\delta_{b}\in\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i))
and so
\hat{\delta}_{b}(\psi)=0
. This shows that
\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
is indeed an
R
-module over
X
. To see
\tau(\mathbf{M})
is a geometric submodule of
\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
, we need to show
\hat{\delta}_{b}\circ\tau
agrees with
\delta_{b}
for each
b\in B
. This is clear, since for each
m\in M
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}_{b}(\tau(m))=\tau(m)(\delta_{b})=\delta_{b}(m)

as required.

(
4
): First suppose
\psi(\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i)))=0
. For each
b\in B\setminus B(i)
, we have
\delta_{b}\in\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i))
and so
\hat{\delta}_{b}(\psi)=\psi(\delta_{b})=0
. It follows that
\psi\in\mathbf{M}^{\lf}(i)
.

Now suppose
\psi\in\mathbf{M}^{\lf}(i)
. Let
\phi\in\Ann(\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i)))
. We know from (
2
) that

(5.1)

\displaystyle\psi(\phi)=\left(\sum_{b\in B}\tau(m_{b})\psi(\delta_{b})\right)(\phi)=\sum_{b\in B}\psi(\delta_{b})\phi(m_{b}),

where
\psi(\delta_{b})\phi(m_{b})=0
for almost all
b\in B
. If
b\in B(i)
, then
m_{b}\in\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i))
and so
\phi(m_{b})=0
. If
b\in B\setminus B(i)
, then
\psi(\delta_{b})=\hat{\delta}_{b}(\psi)=0
since
\psi\in\mathbf{M}^{\lf}(i)
. Thus
\psi(\delta_{b})\phi(m_{b})=0
for all
b\in B
, and so (
5.1
) implies
\psi(\phi)=0
. This implies
\psi(\Ann(\mathbf{M}(\Phi(i))))=0
.
∎

Henceforth, whenever
\mathbf{M}
is a proper projective
R
-module over
X
, we endow
\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
with the structure of an
R
-module over
X
as in Proposition
5.17
, and identify
\mathbf{M}
with its image in
\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
under
\tau
.

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
be proper projective
R
-modules over
X
and
Y
respectively. Suppose
f:X\to Y
is a coarse embedding and
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
has finite displacement over
f
.
Then
\phi
extends to a finite displacement map
\phi^{\lf}:\mathbf{M}^{\lf}\to\mathbf{N}^{\lf}
given by
\phi^{\lf}(\psi)(\alpha)=\psi(\phi^{*}\alpha)
for each
\psi\in\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{N}^{*}_{c}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
and
\mathbf{N}=(N,C,\epsilon,q,\{C(i)\})
. Pick
\Phi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
large enough such that
\mathbf{M}
has displacement
\Phi
and
\phi
has displacement
\Phi
over
f
. Let
\{m_{b}\}
be a projective basis of
\mathbf{M}
with displacement
\Phi
. Assume also that
f
is a
(\Lambda,\Upsilon)
-coarse embedding. Fix
i\in\mathbb{N}
and set
i_{k}=\Phi^{k}(i)
.

If
\psi\in\mathbf{M}^{\lf}(i)
, then
\psi(\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i_{1})))=0
by (
4
) of Proposition
5.17
. Since
\phi(\mathbf{M}(i_{1}))\subseteq\mathbf{N}(i_{2})
, it follows that
\phi^{*}(\Ann(\mathbf{N}(i_{2})))\subseteq\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i_{1}))
. Thus

\phi^{\lf}(\psi)(\Ann(\mathbf{N}(i_{2})))\subseteq\psi(\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i_{1})))=0,

and so (
4
) of Proposition
5.17
implies
\phi^{\lf}(\psi)\in\mathbf{N}^{\lf}(i_{2})
.

Now suppose
\psi\in\mathbf{M}^{\lf}(i)
and
y\in\supp_{\mathbf{N}^{\lf}}(\phi^{\lf}(\psi))
. Then there exists some
c\in C
with
q(c)=y
and
\hat{\epsilon}_{c}(\phi^{\lf}(\psi))\neq 0
. It follows that
\psi(\phi^{*}\epsilon_{c})=\phi^{\lf}(\psi)(\epsilon_{c})\neq 0
. As observed above, we have
\psi(\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i_{1})))=0
. By Lemma
5.15

\phi^{*}\epsilon_{c}-\sum_{b\in B(i_{1})}(\phi^{*}\epsilon_{c})(m_{b})\delta_{b}\in\Ann(\mathbf{M}(i_{1}))
, and so

\displaystyle\psi\left(\sum_{b\in B(i_{1})}(\phi^{*}\epsilon_{c})(m_{b})\delta_{b}\right)=\psi(\phi^{*}\epsilon_{c})\neq 0.

Thus there is some
b\in B(i_{1})
with
\epsilon_{c}(\phi(m_{b}))\hat{\delta}_{b}(\psi)\neq 0
. Since
\hat{\delta}_{b}(\psi)\neq 0
, we have
p(b)\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}^{\lf}}(\psi)
. Since
m_{b}\in\mathbf{M}(i_{2})
and
\epsilon_{c}(\phi(m_{b}))\neq 0
, we have

y\in\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(m_{b}))\subseteq N_{i_{3}}(f(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m_{b})))\subseteq N_{i_{3}}(f(N_{i_{2}}(p(b))))\subseteq N_{j}(f(\supp_{\mathbf{M}^{\lf}}(\psi))),

where
j\coloneqq\Upsilon(i_{2})+i_{3}
.
Thus
\supp_{\mathbf{N}^{\lf}}(\phi^{\lf}(\psi))\subseteq N_{j}(f(\supp_{\mathbf{M}^{\lf}}(\psi)))
as required.
∎

Remark 5.19
.

Suppose
\phi
is as above and
\mathbf{M}
has projective basis
\{m_{b}\}
. Then each
\psi\in\mathbf{M}^{\lf}
can be expressed as an infinite sum
\psi=\sum_{b\in B}\psi(\delta_{b})m_{b}
, and it can be readily verified that

\phi^{\lf}\left(\sum_{b\in B}\psi(\delta_{b})m_{b}\right)=\sum_{b\in B}\psi(\delta_{b})\phi(m_{b}),

where all these infinite sums satisfy the criterion in Notation
5.14
. Thus
\phi^{\lf}
can be thought of as the linear extension of
\phi
to infinite sums.

Although
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
does not naturally inherit the structure of an
R
-module over
X
in general, the next proposition demonstrates that if
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
is a proper projective
R
-module of finite height, then so is
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
. In light of Proposition
4.11
, this is an analogue of the fact that the dual of a finitely generated projective
RG
-module is also projective
[
Bro82
, Proposition I.8.3]
.

Proposition 5.20
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
be a proper projective
R
-module over
X
, of displacement
\Phi
and of height
i_{0}<\infty
. Then
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
can be endowed with the structure of a proper projective
R
-module over
X
, of displacement
\Phi^{\prime}=\Phi^{\prime}(\Phi,i_{0})
and of height at most
i_{0}
. Moreover, the following are satisfied:

(1)

\supp_{\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}}(\delta_{b})\subseteq N_{\Phi(i_{0})}(p(b))
for all
b\in B
.

(2)

If
\alpha\in\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
and
m\in\mathbf{M}
satisfy
\alpha(m)\neq 0
, then

\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)\cap N_{\Phi(i_{0})}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}}(\alpha))\neq\emptyset.

(3)

After identifying
\mathbf{M}
with its image under the natural monomorphism
\tau:\mathbf{M}\to\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c},R)
given by
\tau(m)(\alpha)=\alpha(m)
, we have
\mathbf{M}=(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c})^{*}_{c}
.

(4)

Suppose
f:X\to Y
is a
(\Lambda,\Upsilon)
-coarse equivalence and
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
has displacement
\Phi
over
f
, where
\mathbf{N}
is a finite-height proper projective
R
-module over
Y
. Assume that
\mathbf{N}^{*}_{c}
is endowed with the structure of a proper projective finite-height
R
-module over
X
, in an analogous manner as
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
. Then
\phi^{*}:\mathbf{N}^{*}_{c}\to\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
has displacement
\Phi^{\prime}
over an
\Upsilon(0)
-coarse inverse of
f
, where
\Phi^{\prime}
depends only on
\Lambda
,
\Upsilon
,
\Phi
and
\mathbf{M}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{M}
has height
i_{0}
, we deduce that
\delta_{b}=0
for all
b\in B\setminus B(i_{0})
. By Lemma
4.13
, we can pick a projective basis
\{m_{b}\}
of
\mathbf{M}
with displacement
\Phi
such that
m_{b}=0
for all
b\in B\setminus B(i_{0})
.
Let
\tau:\mathbf{M}\to\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c},R)
be given by
\tau(m)(\alpha)=\alpha(m)
. We give
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}=\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{M},R)
the structure of an
R
-module over
X
by

\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}=(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c},B,\{\tau(m_{b})\},p,\{B(i)\}).

We note that
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}=\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}(i_{0})
, showing
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
has height at most
i_{0}
. Moreover,
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
is proper as
\mathbf{M}
is.

We claim
\{\delta_{b}\}
is a projective basis of
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
. Firstly,
\delta_{b}\in\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
by Remark
5.13
.
Let
\alpha\in\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
. By Proposition
5.9
, there are only finitely many
b\in B(i_{0})
such that
\tau(m_{b})(\alpha)=\alpha(m_{b})\neq 0
. Since
m_{b}=0
for
b\in B\setminus B(i_{0})
, there are only finitely many
b\in B
such that
\tau(m_{b})(\alpha)=\alpha(m_{b})\neq 0

Thus for each
m\in\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}(i_{0})
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{b\in B}\tau(m_{b})(\alpha)\delta_{b}(m)=\sum_{b\in B}\alpha(m_{b})\delta_{b}(m)=\alpha\left(\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(m)m_{b}\right)=\alpha(m),

hence
\alpha=\sum_{b\in B}\tau(m_{b})(\alpha)\delta_{b}
, where almost all terms of this sum are zero. This shows that
\{(\delta_{b},\tau(m_{b}))\}_{b\in B}
is a projective basis of
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
as an
R
-module.

To see
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
is a projective
R
-module over
X
, it remains to demonstrate that
\{\delta_{b}\}
has finite displacement. Let
b\in B
. If
x\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}}(\delta_{b})
, then there is some
b^{\prime}\in B
with
\tau(m_{b^{\prime}})(\delta_{b})\neq 0
and
p(b^{\prime})=x
. Thus
\delta_{b}(m_{b^{\prime}})\neq 0
, and so
p(b)\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m_{b^{\prime}})\subseteq N_{\Phi(i_{0})}(x)
. Therefore
\supp_{\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}}(\delta_{b})\subseteq N_{\Phi(i_{0})}(p(b))
, proving (
1
). As
\delta_{b}\in\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}(i_{0})
for all
b\in B
, it follows that
\{\delta_{b}\}
has displacement depending only on
\Phi
and
i_{0}
.

(
2
): Suppose that
\alpha\in\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
and
m\in\mathbf{M}
satisfy
\alpha(m)\neq 0
. We have
\alpha=\sum_{b\in B}\tau(m_{b})(\alpha)\delta_{b}
and
m=\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(m)m_{b}
. Thus there exist
b,b^{\prime}\in B(i_{0})
with
\tau(m_{b})(\alpha)=\alpha(m_{b})\neq 0
,
\delta_{b^{\prime}}(m)\neq 0
and
\delta_{b}(m_{b^{\prime}})\neq 0
. Thus
p(b)\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m_{b^{\prime}})\subseteq N_{\Phi(i_{0})}(p(b^{\prime}))
so that
d(p(b),p(b^{\prime}))\leq\Phi(i_{0})
. Since
p(b)\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}}(\alpha)
and
p(b^{\prime})\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)
, we conclude that
p(b^{\prime})\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m)\cap N_{\Phi(i_{0})}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}}(\alpha))
as required.

(
3
):
Repeating the above construction with
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
in place of
\mathbf{M}
, we have that

(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c})^{*}_{c}=((\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c})^{*}_{c},B,\theta(\delta_{b}),p,\{B(i)\})

is a finite height projective
R
-module over
X
with projective basis
\{\theta(\delta_{b})\}
, where
\theta(\delta_{b})(\nu)=\nu(\delta_{b})
for all
\nu\in(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c})^{*}_{c}
and
b\in B
.
For each
m\in M
, we note

\{b\in B\mid\tau(m)(\delta_{b})\neq 0\}=\{b\in B\mid\delta_{b}(m)\neq 0\}\subseteq p^{-1}(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m))

is finite because
\mathbf{M}
is proper. Thus Proposition
5.9
ensures
\tau(m)\in(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c})^{*}_{c}
. To see
\tau
is injective, we note that if
\tau(m)=0
, then
m=\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(m)m_{b}=\sum_{b\in B}\tau(m)(\delta_{b})m_{b}=0
. If
\nu\in(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c})^{*}_{c}
, we see that

\displaystyle\tau\left(\sum_{b\in B}\nu(\delta_{b})m_{b}\right)=\sum_{b\in B}\nu(\delta_{b})\tau(m_{b})=\sum_{b\in B}\theta(\delta_{b})(\nu)\tau(m_{b})=\nu,

where the last equality follows because
(\tau(m_{b}),\theta(\delta_{b}))
is a projective basis of
(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c})^{*}_{c}
. Thus
\tau
induces an isomorphism
\mathbf{M}\to(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c})^{*}_{c}
between underlying
R
-modules. Since for each
m\in\mathbf{M}
and
b\in B
, we have

\theta(\delta_{b})(\tau(m))=\tau(m)(\delta_{b})=\delta_{b}(m),

we see that after identifying
\mathbf{M}
with
(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c})^{*}_{c}
via
\tau
, we have
\theta(\delta_{b})=\delta_{b}
, and so
\mathbf{M}=(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c})^{*}_{c}
.

(
4
): Now suppose
\mathbf{N}=(N,C,\epsilon,q,\{C(i)\})
is another proper finite height projective
R
-module over
Y
with projective basis
\{n_{c}\}
. As above, we define
\mathbf{N}^{*}_{c}=(\mathbf{N}^{*}_{c},C,\{\tau(n_{c})\},q,\{C(i)\})
with projective basis
\{\epsilon_{c}\}
.
Setting
A=\Upsilon(0)
, there exists a coarse inverse
g:Y\to X
that is a
(\Lambda,\Upsilon)
-coarse equivalence such that
g\circ f
and
f\circ g
are
A
-close to the identity maps.
Let
\alpha\in\mathbf{N}^{*}_{c}
and suppose
x\in\supp_{\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}}(\phi^{*}(\alpha))
. There is some
b\in B(i_{0})
with
p(b)=x
and
0\neq\tau(m_{b})(\phi^{*}(\alpha))=\alpha(\phi(m_{b}))
. Since
\phi(m_{b})=\sum_{c\in C}\epsilon_{c}(\phi(m_{b}))n_{c}
, there is some
c\in C
with
\tau(n_{c})(\alpha)=\alpha(n_{c})\neq 0
and
\epsilon_{c}(\phi(m_{b}))\neq 0
.
Thus
q(c)\in\supp_{\mathbf{N}^{*}_{c}}(\alpha)\cap\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(m_{b}))
. We have

\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(\phi(m_{b}))\subseteq N_{\Phi(i_{0})}(f(\supp_{\mathbf{M}}(m_{b})))\subseteq N_{\Phi(i_{0})+\Upsilon(\Phi(i_{0}))}(f(x))

Setting
r\coloneqq\Upsilon(\Phi(i_{0})+\Upsilon(\Phi(i_{0})))+A
, we have
d(x,g(q(c)))\leq r
. This implies that
\supp_{\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}}(\phi^{*}(\alpha))\subseteq N_{r}(g(\supp_{\mathbf{N}^{*}_{c}}(\alpha)))
. Since
\mathbf{N}^{*}_{c}
and
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
have finite height, this proves that
\phi^{*}:\mathbf{N}^{*}_{c}\to\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
has finite displacement over
g
.
∎

Remark 5.21
.

Henceforth, for
\mathbf{M}
satisfying the hypotheses of Proposition
5.20
, we always assume
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
is endowed with the structure of a projective
R
-module over
X
as in Proposition
5.20
, and we freely identify
\mathbf{M}
and
(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c})^{*}_{c}
when convenient.

Remark 5.22
.

We remark that if
\mathbf{M}
is of height
i_{0}<\infty
and
i\geq i_{0}
, then
\supp(\alpha,i)=\supp_{\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}}(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
.

Proposition 5.23
.

Let
\mathbf{M}
be a finite-height proper projective
R
-module over
X
. If
f:R\to S
is a ring homomorphism, then there is a geometric isomorphism
\Psi_{\mathbf{M}}=\Psi:\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}\otimes_{R}S\to(\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S)^{*}_{c}
given by
\Psi(\alpha\otimes s)(m\otimes s^{\prime})=f(\alpha(m))ss^{\prime}
for all
m\in\mathbf{M}
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
and
s,s^{\prime}\in S
.

Moreover,
\Psi_{\mathbf{M}}
in natural in the following sense. If
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
is a finite displacement between finite-height projective
R
-modules over
\id_{X}
, then the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{N}^{*}_{c}\otimes_{R}S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbf{N}\otimes_{R}S)^{*}_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}\otimes_{R}S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S)^{*}_{c}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\mathbf{N}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}\otimes\id_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi\otimes\id_{S})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\mathbf{M}}}

Figure 3
.
A commutative diagram showing naturality of
\Psi
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,{\delta_{b}},p,\{B(i)\})
and has projective basis
\{m_{b}\}
.
We use the description of projective basis given in Propositions
4.16
and
5.20
to deduce:

•

\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}\otimes_{R}S
has projective basis
\{\delta_{b}\otimes 1\}_{b\in B}
;

•

(\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S)^{*}_{c}
has projective basis
\{\epsilon_{b}\}_{b\in B}
, where
\epsilon_{b}:\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S\to S
is the map
\epsilon_{b}(s\otimes m)=f(\delta_{b}(m))s
.

Note that for each
b\in B
,
s\in S
and
m\in\mathbf{M}
, we have

\Psi(\delta_{b}\otimes 1)(m\otimes s)=f(\delta_{b}(m))s=\epsilon_{b}(m\otimes s)

so that
\Psi(\delta_{b}\otimes 1)=\epsilon_{b}
for all
b\in B
.

We choose
\Phi
sufficiently large such that both these projective bases have displacement
\Phi
, i.e.

(1)

For each
b\in B(i)
,
\delta_{b}\otimes 1\in(\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}\otimes_{R}S)(\Phi(i))
and
\supp(\delta_{b}\otimes 1)\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(p(b))
.

(2)

For each
b\in B(i)
,
\epsilon_{b}\in((\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S)^{*}_{c})(\Phi(i))
and
\supp(\epsilon_{b})\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(p(b))
.

Thus Lemma
4.5
implies that
\Psi
has finite displacement over
\id_{X}
. Moreover, Lemma
4.5
also implies we can define a finite displacement map
\Theta:(\mathbf{M}\otimes_{R}S)^{*}_{c}\to\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}\otimes_{R}S
such that
\Theta(\epsilon_{b})=\delta_{b}\otimes 1
. Since
\Theta\circ\Psi=\id
and
\Psi\circ\Theta=\id
, we deduce
\Psi
is a geometric isomorphism.

If
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{N}
has finite displacement, then for all
s,s^{\prime}\in S
,
m\in\mathbf{M}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{N}^{*}_{c}
, we have

\displaystyle\left((\phi\otimes\id_{S})^{*}\circ\Psi_{\mathbf{N}}\right)(\alpha\otimes s)(m\otimes s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\mathbf{N}}(\alpha\otimes s)(\phi(m)\otimes s^{\prime})=f(\alpha(\phi(m)))ss^{\prime}

\displaystyle=f(\phi^{*}(\alpha)(m))ss^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\mathbf{M}}(\phi^{*}(\alpha)\otimes s)(m\otimes s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\Psi_{\mathbf{M}}\circ(\phi^{*}\otimes\id_{S}))(\alpha\otimes s)(m\otimes s^{\prime}).

Thus
(\phi\otimes\id_{S})^{*}\circ\Psi_{\mathbf{N}}=\Psi_{\mathbf{M}}\circ(\phi^{*}\otimes\id)
as required.
∎

6.
Projective resolutions over a metric space

In this section, we define the main objects of study: projective
R
-resolutions over a metric space. We prove they always exist in Proposition
6.17
, and that they are unique up to finite displacement chain homotopy in Corollary
6.14
. A key result is Proposition
6.12
, which is an analogue of the
Fundamental Lemma of Homological Algebra

[
Bro82
, Lemma I.7.4]
. It asserts that a coarse embedding
f:X\to Y
induces a finite displacement chain map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
between projective
R
-resolutions over
X
and
Y
, and this map is unique up to a finite displacement chain homotopy.

Definition 6.1
.

We say that a non-negative chain complex
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is an
R
-chain complex over
X
if the following hold:

(1)

each
\mathbf{C}_{i}
is an
R
-module over
X
;

(2)

each boundary map
\partial:\mathbf{C}_{i}\to\mathbf{C}_{i-1}
has finite displacement over
\id_{X}
;

(3)

\{\mathbf{C}_{i}\}_{i}
is staggered.

Remark 6.2
.

The requirement that
\{\mathbf{C}_{i}\}_{i}
is staggered is somewhat artificial, and the subsequent arguments can be easily adapted to remove it. However, Remarks
3.22
and
4.9
ensure there is no real loss of generality in assuming this condition. Doing so means we can apply Propositions
3.23
and
4.4
, allowing us to work with a single function
\Phi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
rather than countable families of such functions, thus drastically simplifying subsequent notation by reducing indices needed.

An
R
-chain complex
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is said to be
proper
(resp. of
finite height
) if each
\mathbf{C}_{i}
is proper (resp. of finite height).
An
R
-chain complex
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X
has
uniform preimages
(resp.
\Omega
-uniform preimages
) if each boundary map
\partial:\mathbf{C}_{i}\to\mathbf{C}_{i-1}
has uniform preimages (resp.
\Omega
-uniform preimages). By Proposition
3.23
, if
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
has uniform preimages, then there exists an
\Omega
such that
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
has
\Omega
-uniform preimages.

We say a subcomplex
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}\leq\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a
geometric subcomplex
if each
\mathbf{D}_{k}
is a geometric submodule of
\mathbf{C}_{k}
. A chain map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
between
R
-chain complexes over
X
and
Y
respectively is said to be of
finite displacement
(resp.
displacement
\Phi
) over
f:X\to Y
if each
f_{i}:\mathbf{C}_{i}\to\mathbf{D}_{i}
has finite displacement (resp. displacement
\Phi
) over
f
. If
f_{\#}
has finite displacement over
f
, then Proposition
3.23
guarantees
f_{\#}
has displacement
\Phi
over
f
for some
\Phi
.
Given chain maps
f_{\#},g_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
, a
chain homotopy
from
f_{\#}
to
g_{\#}
, denoted
f_{\#}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{\#}}}{{\simeq}}g_{\#}
is a map
h_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet+1}
such that
g_{\#}-f_{\#}=\partial h_{\#}+h_{\#}\partial
. A chain homotopy
h_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet+1}
has
finite displacement
(resp.
displacement
\Phi
) over
f
if each
h_{i}:\mathbf{C}_{i}\to\mathbf{D}_{i+1}
has finite displacement (resp. displacement
\Phi
) over
f
.

An
augmentation
of an
R
-chain complex
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X
consists of an
R
-module homomorphism
\epsilon:\mathbf{C}_{0}\to R
such that the composition

\mathbf{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbf{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\epsilon}R

is zero.
An
augmented
R
-chain complex
(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\epsilon)
over
X
is an
R
-chain complex
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X
equipped with an augmentation
\epsilon
.
Given chain complexes
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
and augmentations
\epsilon:\mathbf{C}_{0}\to R
and
\epsilon^{\prime}:\mathbf{D}_{0}\to R
, we say a chain map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
is
augmentation-preserving
if
\epsilon^{\prime}\circ f_{0}=\epsilon
. A
reduced
k
-cycle
of
(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\epsilon)
is a
k
-cycle
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}
satisfying
\epsilon(\sigma)=0
in the case
k=0
. We can thus define the
reduced homology

\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet})
to be the
R
-module of reduced
k
-cycles modulo
k
-boundaries. When unambiguous, we will suppress
\epsilon
from the notation, writing
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
instead of
(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\epsilon)
. In the case where we have an augmented chain complex whose augmentation has not been explicitly specified in the notation, we will always use the letter
\epsilon
to refer to this augmentation.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\mathcal{F})
be an
R
-module over
X
. A geometric submodule
\mathbf{N}\leq\mathbf{M}
is
standard
if it is of the form
\mathbf{N}=\cap_{b\in B^{\prime}}\ker(\delta_{b})
for some
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
. We note that each
\mathbf{M}(i)
is a standard submodule.
If
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is an
R
-chain complex over
X
, we say a subcomplex
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
is
standard
if each
\mathbf{D}_{k}\leq\mathbf{C}_{k}
is standard.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be an
R
-chain complex over
X
.
For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
Y\subseteq X
, we define
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i,Y)
to be the maximal standard subcomplex of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
such that for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the following hold:

(1)

\mathbf{C}_{k}(i,Y)\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
.

(2)

If
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}(i,Y)
, then
\supp(\sigma)\subseteq Y
.

We always assume that when
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is equipped with an augmentation
\epsilon:\mathbf{C}_{0}\to R
, each
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i,Y)
is equipped with the augmentation obtained by restricting
\epsilon
. The key property
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i,Y)
satisfies is the following:

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
X
be a metric space and suppose
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is an
R
-metric complex over
X
such that each boundary map has displacement
\Phi
.

(1)

If
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}(i,Y)
, then
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
and
\supp(\sigma)\subseteq Y
.

(2)

If
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
and
\supp(\sigma)\subseteq Y
, then
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}(\Phi^{k}(i),N_{2^{k}\Phi^{k}(i)}(Y))
.

Proof.

(
1
) follows immediately from Definition
6.3
.

(
2
): Suppose
\mathbf{C}_{k}=(C_{k},B_{k},\delta^{k},p_{k},\{B_{k}(i)\})
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
. For each
Y\subseteq X
and
i,k\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\mathbf{D}_{k}(i,Y)\coloneqq\cap\{\ker(\delta_{b})\mid b\in B_{k}\setminus(B_{k}(i)\cap p_{k}^{-1}(Y))\},

and note that
\mathbf{D}_{k}(i,Y)
consists precisely of the set of all
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
with
\supp(\sigma)\subseteq Y
. In particular,
\mathbf{C}_{k}(i,Y)\subseteq\mathbf{D}_{k}(i,Y)
for each
k
.

We will show
\mathbf{D}_{k}(i,Y)\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{k}(\Phi^{k}(i),N_{2^{k}\Phi^{k}(i)}(Y))
for all
k,i\in\mathbb{N}
and
Y\subseteq X
, which is equivalent to (
2
). We proceed by induction on
k
. For the base case, maximality of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i,Y)
implies that
\mathbf{C}_{0}(i,Y)=\mathbf{D}_{0}(i,Y)
.
For the inductive step, we note that

\displaystyle\partial(\mathbf{D}_{k}(i,Y))

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathbf{D}_{k-1}(\Phi(i),N_{\Phi(i)}(Y))\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{k-1}(\Phi^{k}(i),N_{2^{k-1}\Phi^{k}(i)+\Phi(i)}(Y))

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{k-1}(\Phi^{k}(i),N_{2^{k}\Phi^{k}(i)}(Y)),

where the first inclusion follows because
\partial
has displacement
\Phi
, and the second inclusion follows from the inductive hypothesis. Maximality of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(\Phi^{k}(i),N_{2^{k}\Phi^{k}(i)}(Y))
implies that
\mathbf{D}_{k}(i,Y)\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{k}(\Phi^{k}(i),N_{2^{k}\Phi^{k}(i)}(Y))
as required.
∎

Definition 6.5
.

Let
(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\epsilon)
be an augmented
R
-metric complex over
X
. We say
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
weakly uniformly
n
-acyclic
if there is a function
\Omega:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that

(1)

for all
i,r\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\leq n
and
x\in X
, the map

\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(x)))\to\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(\Omega(i,r),N_{\Omega(i,r)}(x)))

induced by inclusion is zero;

(2)

for each
x\in X
, there is an
m_{x}\in\mathbf{C}_{0}(\Omega_{0})
with
\epsilon(m_{x})=1
and
\supp(m_{x})\subseteq N_{\Omega_{0}}(x)
, where
\Omega_{0}\coloneqq\Omega(0,0)
.

When the above hold, we say
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
\Omega
-weakly uniformly
n
-acyclic
. We say
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
weakly uniformly acyclic
(resp.
\Omega
-weakly uniformly acyclic
) if
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
weakly uniformly
n
-acyclic
(resp.
\Omega
-weakly uniformly
n
-acyclic
) for all
n
.

Remark 6.6
.

Weak uniform acyclicity should not be confused with the more restrictive condition of uniform acyclicity considered in Section
9
. We refer to Remark
9.2
for an explanation of the difference between these terms.

We can reformulate Definition
6.5
as follows:

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\epsilon)
be an augmented
R
-metric complex over
X
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\epsilon)
is weakly uniformly acyclic.

(2)

The following hold:

(a)

The augmented complex

\dots\to\mathbf{C}_{2}\to\mathbf{C}_{1}\to\mathbf{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\epsilon}R\to 0

is exact.

(b)

Each boundary map
\partial:\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\mathbf{C}_{n-1}
has uniform preimages.

(c)

There is a constant
D
such that for each
x\in X
, there is an
m_{x}\in\mathbf{C}_{0}(D)
with
\epsilon(m_{x})=1
and
\supp(m_{x})\subseteq N_{D}(x)
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is staggered, Proposition
3.23
ensures that if each boundary map
\partial:\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\mathbf{C}_{n-1}
has uniform preimages, then there is an
\Omega
such that each
\partial
has
\Omega
-uniform preimages. The equivalence of the two conditions now follows easily by unpacking the definitions and applying Lemma
6.4
.
∎

Remark 6.8
.

A more quantitive version of Proposition
6.7
holds:
Suppose
(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\epsilon)
is
\Omega
-weakly acyclic and each
\partial
has displacement
\Phi
. Then each
\partial
has
\Omega^{\prime}
-uniform preimages, where
\Omega^{\prime}=\Omega^{\prime}(\Omega,\Phi)
, and the constant
D
in (
2c
) depends only on
\Omega
and
\Phi
, and vice versa for the (
2
) implies (
1
) direction.

We are now ready to formulate our key definition:

Definition 6.9
.

Let
X
be a metric space. A
projective (resp. free)
R
-resolution over
X
is an augmented
R
-chain complex
(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\epsilon)
over
X
such that:

(1)

Each
\mathbf{C}_{i}
is a projective (resp. free)
R
-module over
X
.

(2)

(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\epsilon)
is weakly uniformly acyclic.

We note that every free
R
-resolution over
X
is a projective
R
-resolution over
X
, but not conversely.

Remark 6.10
.

A more accurate but verbose term for a projective
R
-resolution over
X
might be
a resolution of
R
by projective
R
-modules over
X
. Since we do not consider projective resolutions of
R
-modules other than
R
, there is no harm in using the terminology projective
R
-resolution over
X
.

As usual, when the augmentation does not need to be explicitly stated, we frequently omit
\epsilon
from the notation and simply say call the complex
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
a projective
R
-resolution over
X
.
Given an increasing function
\Phi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
, we say that
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
has
displacement
\Phi
if the following hold:

•

each
\mathbf{C}_{i}
is a projective
R
-module of displacement
\Phi
;

•

each boundary map
\partial:\mathbf{C}_{i}\to\mathbf{C}_{i-1}
has displacement
\Phi
.

It follows from Propositions
3.23
and
4.4
that each
R
-resolution over
X
has displacement
\Phi
for some
\Phi
.

Example 6.11
.

In
[
KK05
]
, Kapovich–Kleiner define a class of spaces called
metric simplicial complexes
. Recall that a metric simplicial complex consists of the pair
(X^{(1)},X)
, where
X
is the geometric realisation of connected locally finite simplicial complex
X
, and where
X^{(1)}
is the 1-skeleton of
X
equipped with the induced path metric where edges have length one. If
(X^{(1)},X)
is uniformly acyclic (defined in
[
KK05
]
), then the simplicial chain complex
C_{\bullet}(X)
with coefficients in
R
can be given the structure of a free
R
-resolution over
X^{(1)}
.
Specifically, if
(X^{(1)},X)
is a metric simplicial complex, then we define an
R
-chain complex over
X^{(1)}
with

\mathbf{C}_{k}(X)=(C_{k}(X),\Sigma_{k},\delta^{k},p_{k},\{\Sigma_{k}(i)\}),

where:

(1)

\Sigma_{k}
is the set of
k
-simplices in
X
and
C_{k}(X)
is the free
R
-module with basis
\Sigma_{k}
;

(2)

\{\delta^{k}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}}
is the dual basis to
\Sigma_{k}
;

(3)

p:\Sigma_{k}\to X^{(1)}
is a function such that
p_{k}(\sigma)\in\sigma
for each simplex
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}
;

(4)

\Sigma_{k}(i)=\begin{cases}\Sigma_{k}&\textrm{if}\;i\geq k,\\
\emptyset&\textrm{if}\;i<k.\end{cases}

The requirement that
\Sigma_{k}(i)=\emptyset
if
i<k
is to ensure that
\{\mathbf{C}_{k}(X)\}_{k}
is staggered.

More generally, Druţu–Kapovich define
metric cell complexes

[
DK18
]
. It can be shown similarly that the cellular chain complex of a bounded geometry uniformly acyclic metric cell complex can be endowed with the structure of free
R
-resolution over its 1-skeleton. Even more generally, if
(X,\mathbf{C}_{\bullet})
is a
uniformly acyclic metric complex
, as defined in the appendix of
[
KK05
]
, then
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a free
R
-resolution over
X
.

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
X
and
Y
be metric spaces and let
f:X\to Y
be
\Upsilon
-bornologous. Suppose
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
are projective
R
-resolutions over
X
and
Y
respectively, that are of displacement
\Phi
and are
\Omega
-weakly uniformly acyclic.

(1)

There exists an augmentation-preserving chain map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
of displacement
\Phi^{\prime}
over
f
, where
\Phi^{\prime}
depends only on
\Phi
,
\Omega
and
\Upsilon
.

(2)

Suppose there exist augmentation-preserving chain maps
f_{\#},g_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
of displacement
\Psi
over
f
. Then there exists a chain homotopy
f_{\#}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{\#}}}{{\simeq}}g_{\#}
of displacement
\Psi^{\prime}
over
f
, where
\Psi^{\prime}
depends only on
\Phi
,
\Psi
,
\Omega
and
\Upsilon
.

We will prove Proposition
6.12
by induction.
The base case is stated separately, as it does not require the assumption that
f
is bornologous:

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
X
and
Y
be metric spaces, and let
f:X\to Y
be an arbitrary map. Suppose
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
,
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
,
\Phi
and
\Omega
are as in Proposition
6.12
.
Then there exists an augmentation-preserving map
f_{0}:\mathbf{C}_{0}\to\mathbf{D}_{0}
of displacement
\Phi^{\prime}
over
f
, where
\Phi^{\prime}
depends only on
\Phi
and
\Omega
.

Proof.

Set
\Omega_{0}\coloneqq\Omega(0,0)
.
For each
y\in Y
, there is some
n_{y}\in\mathbf{D}_{0}(\Omega_{0})
with
\supp_{\mathbf{D}_{0}}(n_{y})\subseteq N_{\Omega_{0}}(y)
and
\epsilon(n_{y})=1
.
We suppose

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{0}=(C_{0},B,\delta,p,\mathcal{F})

has projective basis
\{m_{b}\}
of displacement
\Phi
.
For each
b\in B
, set
q_{b}=\epsilon(m_{b})n_{f(p(b))}
, noting that
q_{b}\in\mathbf{N}(\Omega_{0})
and that
\supp_{\mathbf{N}}(q_{b})\subseteq N_{\Omega_{0}}(f(p(b)))
. By Lemma
4.5
,
m\mapsto\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(m)q_{b}
defines a map
f_{0}:\mathbf{C}_{0}\to\mathbf{D}_{0}
of displacement
\Omega_{0}
over
f
, which is clearly augmentation-preserving by the choice of
\{q_{b}\}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.12
.

(
1
): We define
f_{k}:\mathbf{C}_{k}\to\mathbf{D}_{k}
by induction on
k
, where the base case
k=0
follows from Lemma
6.13
. If
f_{i}:\mathbf{C}_{i}\to\mathbf{D}_{i}
has been defined for
i\leq k
, we define
f_{k+1}:\mathbf{C}_{k+1}\to\mathbf{D}_{k+1}
as follows. Since
f_{k}
has finite displacement over
f
and
\partial:\mathbf{C}_{k+1}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}_{k}
has finite displacement over
\id_{X}
, Lemma
3.11
implies
f_{k}\partial:\mathbf{C}_{k+1}\to\mathbf{D}_{k}
has finite displacement over
f
.
Exactness of
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
implies
\im(f_{k}\partial)\subseteq\im(\mathbf{D}_{k+1}\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbf{D}_{k})
, where in the case
k=0
, we use the fact that
f_{0}
is augmentation-preserving. As
\partial:\mathbf{D}_{k+1}\to\mathbf{D}_{k}
has uniform preimages by Proposition
6.7
, we can apply Proposition
4.6
to define a map
f_{k+1}:\mathbf{C}_{k+1}\to\mathbf{D}_{k+1}
of finite displacement over
f
, with
\partial f_{k+1}=f_{k}\partial
.

It follows inductively from Lemmas
4.5
and
3.11
, Proposition
4.6
, and Remark
6.8
that the displacement of each
f_{k}
depends only on
\Upsilon
,
\Phi
and
\Omega
. Thus Proposition
3.23
and Remark
3.24
imply
f_{\#}
has displacement
\Phi
, depending only on
\Upsilon
,
\Phi
and
\Omega
.

(
2
): Since
\epsilon\circ(f_{0}-g_{0})=\epsilon-\epsilon=0
on
\mathbf{C}_{0}
,
\im(f_{0}-g_{0})\subseteq\im(\mathbf{D}_{1}\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbf{D}_{0})
. We can thus use Proposition
4.6
as above to define a map
h_{0}:\mathbf{C}_{0}\to\mathbf{D}_{1}
of finite displacement over
f
with
\partial h_{0}=f_{0}-g_{0}
. We now have
\partial(f_{1}-g_{1}-h_{0}\partial)=f_{0}\partial-g_{0}\partial-\partial h_{0}\partial=0
. Exactness of
\mathbf{D}_{2}\to\mathbf{D}_{1}\to\mathbf{D}_{0}
means
\im(f_{1}-g_{1}-h_{0}\partial)\subseteq\im(\mathbf{D}_{2}\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbf{D}_{1})
. We can thus apply Proposition
4.6
to define a map
h_{1}:\mathbf{C}_{1}\to\mathbf{D}_{2}
such that
f_{1}-g_{1}=h_{0}\partial+\partial h_{1}
of finite displacement over
f
. We continue in this way, inductively defining a chain homotopy
h_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet+1}
between
f_{\#}
and
g_{\#}
. A similar argument to that used in (
1
) shows that
h_{\#}
has displacement
\Psi^{\prime}
, where
\Psi^{\prime}
depends only on
\Phi
,
\Psi
,
\Omega
and
\Upsilon
.
∎

Proposition
6.12
readily implies that for any metric space
X
, a projective
R
-resolution over
X
is unique up to a finite displacement chain homotopy.

Corollary 6.14
.

Let
X
be a metric space and let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
be projective
R
-resolutions over
X
. Then there exists an augmentation-preserving finite displacement chain homotopy equivalence
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
, i.e. there exist augmentation-preserving finite displacement chain maps
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
and
g_{\#}:\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
, and finite displacement chain homotopies
\id\simeq g_{\#}f_{\#}
and
\id\simeq f_{\#}g_{\#}
.

Proof.

The existence of
f_{\#}
and
g_{\#}
follows by applying Proposition
6.12
(
1
) twice to the identity
\id_{X}
, whilst the existence of chain homotopies follows by applying Proposition
6.12
(
2
) twice.
∎

We now give a criterion that allows one to truncate projective resolutions to obtain another projective resolution:

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a projective
R
-resolution over a metric space
X
. If there exists a finite displacement retraction from
\mathbf{C}_{n}
onto
\im(\mathbf{C}_{n+1}\to\mathbf{C}_{n})
, then
\mathbf{C}_{n}
contains a projective submodule
\mathbf{P}
such that

0\to\mathbf{P}\xrightarrow{\partial|_{\mathbf{P}}}\mathbf{C}_{n-1}\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbf{C}_{n-2}\to\cdots

is a projective
R
-resolution over
X
.

Proof.

Set
\mathbf{N}\coloneqq\im(\mathbf{C}_{n+1}\to\mathbf{C}_{n})
. By Lemma
4.17
the finite displacement retraction
\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\mathbf{N}
implies the existence of a direct sum
\mathbf{C}_{n}=\mathbf{N}\oplus\mathbf{P}
, where both
\mathbf{N}
and
\mathbf{P}
are projective
R
-modules over
X
, and both projections
\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\mathbf{N}
and
\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\mathbf{P}
of finite displacement. It follows that
0\to\mathbf{P}\xrightarrow{\partial|_{\mathbf{P}}}\mathbf{C}_{n-1}\to\mathbf{C}_{n-2}
is exact.

We pick
\Omega
such that
\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\mathbf{C}_{n-1}
has
\Omega
-uniform preimages. If
\sigma\in\im(\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\mathbf{C}_{n-1})
with
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{n-1}(i)
and
\diam(\supp(\sigma))\leq D
, then there exists
\omega\in\mathbf{C}_{n}(j)
with
\partial\omega=\sigma
and
\supp(\omega)\subseteq N_{j}(\supp(\sigma))
, where
j=\Omega(i,D)
. If the projection
\pi:\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\mathbf{P}
has displacement
\Psi
, then
\pi(\omega)\in\mathbf{P}(\Psi(j))
and
\supp(\pi(\omega))\subseteq N_{j+\Psi(j)}(\supp(\sigma))
with
\partial\pi(\omega)=\sigma
. It follows that
\mathbf{P}\xrightarrow{\partial|_{\mathbf{P}}}\mathbf{C}_{n-1}
has uniform preimages. It thus follows from Proposition
6.7
that

0\to\mathbf{P}\xrightarrow{\partial|_{\mathbf{P}}}\mathbf{C}_{n-1}\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbf{C}_{n-2}\to\cdots

is a projective
R
-resolution over
X
.
∎

Given a coarse embedding
f:X\to Y
and an
R
-projective resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
Y
, we can consider the
pullback chain complex

f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
, where the boundary maps are the pullbacks of the boundary maps of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
, as in Proposition
3.20
. There is a
canonical identification

\iota_{\#}:f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
as in Section
3
, which is just the identity map on underlying
R
-modules, and is clearly a chain map. We equip
f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
with an augmentation
\epsilon
so that
\iota_{\#}
is augmentation-preserving.

Proposition 6.16
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a coarse embedding and let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a projective
R
-resolution over
Y
. Then the pullback complex
f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a projective
R
-resolution over
X
.

Proof.

Proposition
3.20
ensures each boundary map of
f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
has finite displacement and uniform preimages. Moreover,

\dots\to f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{2}\to f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{1}\to f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\epsilon}R\to 0

is exact since
f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is isomorphic to
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
on the level of underlying
R
-modules. Each
f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{k}
is a projective
R
-module over
X
by Proposition
4.14
. The definition of the pullback of
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
ensures that if
B(i)=\emptyset
, then
f^{*}B(i)=\emptyset
. This ensures that
f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is staggered.

All that remains is to show that condition (
2c
) of Proposition
6.7
holds, for then we can apply Proposition
6.7
to deduce
f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is weakly uniformly acyclic. To see this, suppose
f
is a
(\Lambda,\Upsilon)
-coarse embedding. Since
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is an
R
-projective resolution over
Y
, there exists
D
such that for each
y\in Y
, there exists
m_{y}\in\mathbf{C}_{0}(D)
with
\epsilon(m_{y})=1
and
\supp_{\mathbf{C}_{0}}(m_{y})\subseteq N_{D}(y)
. Let
\iota:f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{0}\to\mathbf{C}_{0}
be the canonical identification, and let
n_{x}\coloneqq\iota^{-1}(m_{f(x)})
for each
x\in X
. Then for each
x\in X
, we have
\iota(n_{x})=m_{f(x)}\in\mathbf{C}_{0}(D)
and
\supp_{\mathbf{C}_{0}}(m_{f(x)})\subseteq N_{D}(f(x))\subseteq N_{D}(f(X))
. Thus Lemma
3.19
implies
n_{x}\in f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{0}(D)
. Lemma
3.19
also implies
f(\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{0}}(n_{x}))\subseteq N_{D}(\supp_{\mathbf{C}_{0}}(m_{f(x)}))\subseteq N_{2D}(f(x))
, and so
\supp_{f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{0}}(n_{x})\subseteq N_{D^{\prime}}(x)
, where
D^{\prime}\coloneqq\widetilde{\Lambda}(2D)
. Hence condition (
2c
) of Proposition
6.7
holds as required.
∎

We now prove the existence of a projective
R
-resolution over a metric space.

Proposition 6.17
.

If
X
is a metric space, then there exists a free (and hence projective)
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X
. Moreover,
\mathbf{C}_{0}
has finite height and if
X
is a proper metric space, we may assume
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is proper.

We prove Proposition
6.17
via the following general construction:

Definition 6.18
.

Let
X
be a metric space and let
R
be a commutative ring. The
standard
R
-resolution

\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
of
X
is defined as follows:

(1)

For
j\geq 0
,
C_{j}
is the free
R
-module generated by
B_{j}
, the set of all
(j+1)
-tuples
[x_{0},\dots,x_{j}]
of
X
, and
C_{j}=0
for
j<0
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{j}=\{(b,\delta_{b})\}_{b\in B_{j}}
be the corresponding projective basis of
C_{j}
.

(2)

The boundary map
\partial:C_{k}\to C_{k-1}
is given by

{[x_{0},\dots,x_{k}]\mapsto\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}[x_{0},\dots,\widehat{x_{i}},\dots,x_{k}],}

and as usual,
[x_{0},\dots,\widehat{x}_{i},\dots,x_{k}]
denotes the
k
-tuple with
x_{i}
removed.

(3)

We define
p_{j}:B_{j}\to R
by
[x_{0},\dots,x_{j}]\mapsto x_{0}
.

(4)

We define a filtration
\mathcal{F}_{j}=\{B_{j}(i)\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
by

B_{j}(i)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\{[x_{0},\dots,x_{j}]\in B_{j}\mid d(x_{k},x_{l})\leq i\;\text{for all $0\leq k,l\leq j$}\}&\textrm{if}\;j\leq i,\\
\emptyset&\textrm{if}\;j>i.\end{cases}

(5)

Let
\mathbf{C}_{j}=(C_{j},B_{j},\delta,p,\mathcal{F}_{j},\{b\}_{b\in B_{j}})
be the projective
R
-module over
X
.

(6)

We equip
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
with the augmentation
\epsilon:\mathbf{C}_{0}\to R
given by
\epsilon(b)=1
for each
b\in B_{0}
.

Proposition 6.19
.

The standard
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
of a metric space
X
is a free
R
-resolution over
X
.

Proof.

We first verify each
\mathbf{C}_{j}
is actually a free
R
-module over
X
. Indeed, since for each
b\in B_{j}(i)
, we have
m_{b}\in\mathbf{C}_{j}(i)
and
\supp_{\mathbf{C}_{j}}(m_{b})=\{p(b)\}
, we see that
\mathbf{C}_{j}
has displacement
\id_{\mathbb{N}}
over
X
. We also observe that
\{\mathbf{C}_{j}\}_{j}
is staggered. We now note that if
b=[x_{0},\dots,x_{j}]\in B_{j}(i)
, then
\supp_{\mathbf{C}_{j-1}}(\partial b)=\{x_{0},x_{1}\}\subseteq N_{i}(p(b))
. Thus the boundary maps have finite displacement over
\id_{X}
.

For each
x\in X
, we define the map
h^{x}_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
given by
h^{x}_{\#}([x_{0},\dots,x_{j}])=[x,x_{0},\dots,x_{j}]
. This map does
not
have finite displacement unless
X
is bounded. It is easy to verify that
\partial h^{x}_{\#}\sigma+h^{x}_{\#}\partial\sigma=\sigma
for all
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{j}
and
j>0
, and this also holds when
j=0
if we also assume
\sigma\in\ker(\epsilon)
. This verifies that

\cdots\to\mathbf{C}_{2}\to\mathbf{C}_{1}\to\mathbf{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\epsilon}R\to 0

is exact. Moreover, suppose
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{j}(i)
is a reduced cycle with
x\in\supp(\sigma)
and
D=\lceil\diam(\supp(\sigma))\rceil
. If
b=[x_{0},\dots,x_{j}]\in B_{j}(i)
with
\delta_{b}(\sigma)\neq 0
, then
x_{0}\in\supp(\sigma)
and so
d(x,x_{k})\leq D+i
for all
0\leq k\leq j
. It follows that
h_{\#}^{x}(\sigma)\in\mathbf{C}_{j+1}(i^{\prime})
, where
i^{\prime}=\max(j+1,i+D)
,
\partial h_{\#}^{x}(\sigma)=\sigma
and
\supp(h_{\#}^{x}(\sigma))=x\subseteq\supp(\sigma)
. This shows that each boundary map has uniform preimages.

Finally, for each
x\in X
, we have
x\in B_{0}(0)
and
\epsilon(x)=1
. By Proposition
6.7
, we deduce
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a projective
R
-resolution over
X
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.17
.

The first part of Proposition
6.17
follows directly from Proposition
6.19
. Moreover, since
B_{0}(0)=B_{0}
in Definition
6.18
, for every standard resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
,
\mathbf{C}_{0}
has finite height.

Suppose
X
is a proper metric space. Let
Y\subseteq X
be a 1-net in
X
, i.e. a maximal subset
Y\subseteq X
such that
d(y,y^{\prime})\geq 1
for all distinct
y,y^{\prime}\in Y
. Since
X
is proper and is covered by the collection of radius 1 open balls centred at a point of
Y
, it follows that
\{y\in Y\mid d(x,y)\leq r\}
is finite for each
x\in X
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus the standard resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
of
Y
is proper. Letting
f:X\to Y
be a closest point projection, which is a coarse equivalence, it follows from Propositions
3.27
and
6.16
that
f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a proper projective
R
-resolution over
X
.
Moreover, Proposition
3.27
ensures the pullback of
f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{0}
also has finite height.
∎

The above proof yields the following refinement of Proposition
6.17
in the case
X
is proper:

Corollary 6.20
.

If
X
is a proper metric space, then there exists a subspace
Y\subseteq X
such
N_{A}(Y)=X
for some
A\geq 0
, and the standard
R
-resolution of
Y
is a proper free
R
-resolution over
X
.

Remark 6.21
.

If
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is the standard resolution of a metric space
X
, then for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i)
is a subcomplex of finite-height free
R
-modules over
X
, since each
\mathbf{C}_{j}(i)
is the free summand of
\mathbf{C}_{j}
generated by
B_{j}(i)\subseteq B_{j}
. The analogous statement does not necessarily hold for an arbitrary projective
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X
.
Moreover, every submodule of the form
\mathbf{C}_{k}(i,Y)
is a free summand of
\mathbf{C}_{k}
, since it is a standard submodule of
\mathbf{C}_{k}
.

Corollary 6.22
.

Let
G
be a countable group equipped with a proper left-invariant metric. Then the standard
R
-resolution is a proper
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
G
such that each
\mathbf{C}_{k}
is a
G
-induced projective
R
-module over
X
, and all boundary maps are
G
-equivariant.

Proof.

This follows immediately from the proof of Proposition
6.17
, since the standard resolution of
G
is constructed
G
-equivariantly.
∎

A related construction is the following:

Definition 6.23
.

Let
X
be a metric space, and equip
X
with an arbitrary total order
<
. Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be the standard resolution of
X
. The
ordered standard resolution
of
X
is the subcomplex
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
in which each
\mathbf{D}_{k}
is the geometric submodule of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
generated by
(k+1)
-tuples
(x_{0},\dots,x_{k})

with
x_{0}<x_{1}<\cdots<x_{k}
.

An argument similar to the proof of Proposition
6.19
yields the following:

Lemma 6.24
.

Let
X
be a metric space, and let
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
be an ordered standard resolution of
X
. Then
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
is a projective
R
-resolution over
X
.

One advantage of working with the (unordered) standard resolution is that if a group acts freely and isometrically on
X
, then it acts freely on the standard resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
of
X
. However, ordered standard resolutions are useful as they are related to simplicial chain complexes of Rips complexes.
The following is a consequence of Definition
6.23
:

Lemma 6.25
.

Let
X
be a metric space, let
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
be the ordered standard resolution of
X
. For each
j\in\mathbb{N}
, the subcomplex
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}(j)
of
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
coincides in dimensions
k\leq j
with the simplicial chain complex
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(P_{j}(X);R)
of the Rips complex
P_{j}(X)
with coefficients in
R
, i.e.
\mathbf{D}_{k}(j)=\mathbf{C}_{k}(P_{j}(X);R)
for all
k\leq j
, and the boundary maps coincide.
Moreover, for each subspace
Y\subseteq X
and
k,j\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\leq j
, we have

C_{k}(P_{j}(Y);R)\subseteq\mathbf{D}_{k}(j,Y)\subseteq C_{k}(P_{j}(N_{j}(Y));R).

We define the
n
-truncation

[\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}]_{n}
to be the chain complex
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
with

\mathbf{D}_{k}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{C}_{k}&\textrm{if}\;k\leq n,\\
0&\textrm{if}\;k>n.\end{cases}

We say
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
n
-dimensional
if
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}=[\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}]_{n}
. A
partial projective (resp. free)
R
-resolution of dimension
n
over
X
is an augmented
n
-dimensional
R
-chain complex
