(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\epsilon)
over
X
such that:

(1)

Each
\mathbf{C}_{i}
is a projective (resp. free)
R
-module over
X
;

(2)

(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\epsilon)
is weakly uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic.

We now show that any partial projective resolution can be extended:

Proposition 6.26
.

Let
X
be a metric space and let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a partial projective (resp. free)
R
-resolution of dimensional
n
over
X
. There exists a projective (resp. free)
R
-resolution
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
over
X
with
[\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}]_{n}=[\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}]_{n}
.

Proof.

We construct
\mathbf{D}_{k}
and the boundary map
\partial:\mathbf{D}_{k}\to\mathbf{D}_{k-1}
inductively on
k
, setting
\mathbf{D}_{k}=\mathbf{C}_{k}
for
k\leq n
.
For the inductive step, we assume that for some
k\geq n
we have a partial projective resolution
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
over
X
of dimension
k
.

Let
\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}
be an arbitrary free
R
-resolution over
X
, which exists by Proposition
6.17
. Applying Lemma
6.13
and then Proposition
4.6
inductively, we see there exist finite displacement chain maps
f_{\#}:[\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}]_{k}\to\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}
and
g_{\#}:[\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}]_{k}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
, and a finite displacement chain homotopy
h_{\#}:[\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}]_{k-1}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet+1}
satisfying
\partial h_{\#}+h_{\#}\partial=g_{\#}f_{\#}-\id
on
[\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}]_{k-1}
.

We consider the following chain complex:

\cdots\to 0\to\mathbf{D}_{k}\oplus\mathbf{E}_{k+1}\xrightarrow{\partial_{k+1}}\mathbf{D}_{k}\to\mathbf{D}_{k-1}\to\dots\to\mathbf{D}_{0}\to 0,

where
\partial_{k+1}
is given by
(d,e)\mapsto d+h_{\#}\partial d+g_{\#}\partial e-g_{\#}f_{\#}d
. It follows from Proposition
4.14
that
\mathbf{D}_{k}\oplus\mathbf{E}_{k+1}
is a projective
R
-module over
X
, and is a free
R
-module over
X
when
\mathbf{D}_{k}
is. Since
f_{\#}
,
g_{\#}
and
h_{\#}
have finite displacement, so does
\partial_{k+1}
. We also have
\partial\partial_{k+1}(d,e)=-h_{\#}\partial\partial d=0
for all
(d,e)\in\mathbf{D}_{k}\oplus\mathbf{E}_{k+1}
. By Proposition
6.7
, all that remains is to show
\partial_{k+1}
has uniform preimages, and that
\mathbf{D}_{k}\oplus\mathbf{E}_{k+1}\to\mathbf{D}_{k}\to\mathbf{D}_{k-1}
is exact.

To see this, suppose
f_{\#}
,
g_{\#}
and
h_{\#}
have displacement
\Phi
, and that
\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}
has
\Omega
-uniform preimages. Suppose
\sigma\in\mathbf{D}_{k}(i)
is a reduced
k
-cycle with
\supp(\sigma)\subseteq N_{r}(x)
. Setting
i_{1}\coloneqq\Phi(i)
and
r_{1}\coloneqq r+\Phi(i)
, we see
f_{\#}\sigma\in\mathbf{E}_{k}(i_{1})
with
\supp(f_{\#}\sigma)\subseteq N_{r_{1}}(x)
. Setting
i_{2}\coloneqq\Omega(i_{1},2r_{1})
and
r_{2}\coloneqq r_{1}+i_{2}
, we see that there is some
\omega\in\mathbf{E}_{k+1}(i_{2})
with
\partial\omega=f_{\#}\sigma
and
\supp(\omega)\subseteq N_{r_{2}}(x)
. We have
\partial_{k+1}(\sigma,\omega)=\sigma+h_{\#}\partial\sigma+g_{\#}\partial\omega-g_{\#}f_{\#}\sigma=\sigma
with
(\sigma,\omega)\in(\mathbf{D}_{k}\oplus\mathbf{E}_{k+1})(i_{2})
and
\supp_{\mathbf{D}_{k}\oplus\mathbf{E}_{k+1}}(\sigma,\omega)\subseteq N_{r_{2}}(x)
by Proposition
3.25
, and we are done.
∎

Recall that if
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
are chain complexes, then
\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}=\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
is a chain complex where

\mathbf{E}_{k}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{k}\mathbf{C}_{i}\otimes\mathbf{D}_{k-i}

and the boundary map
\partial
is given by

\partial(\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})=\partial^{I}\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2}+(-1)^{i}\sigma_{1}\otimes\partial^{II}\sigma_{2}

for all
\sigma_{1}\in\mathbf{C}_{i}
and
\sigma_{2}\in\mathbf{D}_{k-i}
, where
\partial^{I}
and
\partial^{II}
are the boundary maps of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
respectively. If
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
are equipped with augmentations
\epsilon_{\mathbf{C}}:\mathbf{C}_{0}\to R
and
\epsilon_{\mathbf{D}}:\mathbf{D}_{0}\to R
, then
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is equipped with the augmentation
\epsilon_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\epsilon_{\mathbf{D}}:\mathbf{C}_{0}\otimes\mathbf{D}_{0}\to R
given by
(\epsilon_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\epsilon_{\mathbf{D}})(\sigma\otimes\tau)=\epsilon_{\mathbf{C}}(\sigma)\epsilon_{\mathbf{D}}(\tau)
.

The following proposition is the starting point for the theory of cup products, cap products, and coarse Poincaré duality that will be developed in Sections
11
and
12
.

Proposition 6.27
.

Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
be projective
R
-resolutions over
X
and
Y
. Then
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\otimes_{R}\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
is a projective
R
-resolution over
X\times Y
.

Proof.

Set
\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}=\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\otimes_{R}\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
. Each
\mathbf{E}_{k}
is given the structure of an
R
-module over
X\times Y
via Propositions
3.25
and
4.15
. It follows from Propositions
4.14
and
4.15
that each
\mathbf{E}_{k}
is a projective
R
-module over
X\times Y
, that all boundary maps have finite displacement and that
\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}
is staggered. All that remains is to show that
\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}
is weakly uniformly acyclic. Pick
\Omega
such that both
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
are
\Omega
-weakly uniformly acyclic.

Let
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{\bullet}
be standard
R
-resolutions over
X
and
Y
respectively. By Corollary
6.14
, there exist augmentation-preserving finite displacement chain homotopy equivalences
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{\bullet}
and
g_{\#}:\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{\bullet}
. Hence
(f_{\#}\otimes g_{\#}):\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{\bullet}
is also an augmentation-preserving finite displacement chain homotopy equivalence. Since being weakly uniformly acyclic is invariant under augmentation-preserving finite displacement chain homotopy equivalences, we can assume without loss of generality that both
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
are standard
R
-resolutions.

By Remark
6.21
, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
Z\subseteq X
, each subcomplex of the form
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i,Z)
is a complex of free
R
-modules, and similarly for
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
.
Set
\Omega_{0}\coloneqq\Omega(0,0)
. Weak uniformly acyclicity of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
ensure that for each
(x,y)\in X\times Y
, there exist
m_{x}\in\mathbf{C}_{0}(\Omega_{0})
and
n_{y}\in\mathbf{D}_{0}(\Omega_{0})
with
(m_{x}\otimes n_{y})\in\mathbf{E}_{0}(\Omega_{0})
,
(\epsilon_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\epsilon_{\mathbf{D}})(m_{x}\otimes n_{y})=1
and
\supp(m_{x}\otimes n_{y})\subseteq N_{\Omega_{0}}(x)\times N_{\Omega_{0}}(y)
.

We first argue in the case
R
is a PID.
The Künneth short exact sequence
[
Rot09
, Theorem 10.81]
implies that for all
i,k,r\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in X
and
y\in Y
, we have a natural short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\to\bigoplus_{p+q=n}\widetilde{H}_{p}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(x)))\otimes_{R}\widetilde{H}_{q}(\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(y)))\to\widetilde{H}_{n}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(x))\otimes_{R}\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(y)))

\displaystyle\to\bigoplus_{p+q=n-1}\Tor_{1}^{R}(\widetilde{H}_{p}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(x))),\widetilde{H}_{q}(\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(y))))\to 0.

Here, we use the fact that all subcomplexes of the form
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(x))
are complexes of free (hence flat)
R
-modules, so the Künneth theorem does apply.
Naturality of this short sequence, coupled with
\Omega
-weak uniform acyclicity of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
, ensures that the maps

(6.1)

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{n}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(x))\otimes_{R}\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(y)))\to\widetilde{H}_{n}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(j,N_{j}(x))\otimes_{R}\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}(j,N_{j}(y))),

induced by inclusion, are zero, where
j=\Omega(i,r)
. This implies that
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\otimes_{R}\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
is weakly uniformly acyclic.

For a general commutative ring
R
we argue similarly, using the Künneth spectral sequence
[
Rot09
, Theorem 10.90]
instead of the Künneth short exact sequence.
Recall this spectral sequence is a first quadrant homological spectral sequence

\displaystyle E^{2}_{p,q}(P_{\bullet},Q_{\bullet})=\bigoplus_{s+t=1}\Tor_{p}^{R}(H_{s}(P_{\bullet}),H_{t}(Q_{\bullet}))\Rightarrow_{p}H_{n}(P_{\bullet}\otimes_{R}Q_{\bullet})

for chain complexes
P_{\bullet}
and
Q_{\bullet}
of flat
R
-modules, and this spectral sequence is natural in
P_{\bullet}
and
Q_{\bullet}
.
Now weak acyclicity of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
implies that the morphism of spectral sequences

E^{2}_{p,q}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(x)),\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(y)))\to E^{2}_{p,q}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(j,N_{j}(x)),\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}(j,N_{j}(y))),

induced by inclusion, is zero, where
j=\Omega(i,r)
. The induced analogous morphism between
E^{\infty}
terms of these spectral sequences is also zero, which implies the maps (
6.1
), induced by inclusion, are zero.
∎

We can use a similar argument to prove a change of rings formula for projective resolutions over a metric space. If
f:R\to S
is a ring homomorphism and
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a chain complex of
R
-modules, let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\otimes_{R}S
denote the chain complex of
S
-modules with boundary maps given by
\partial\otimes\id_{S}:\mathbf{C}_{k}\otimes_{R}S\to\mathbf{C}_{k-1}\otimes_{R}S
.

Proposition 6.28
.

Let
f:R\to S
be a homomorphism between commutative rings, and let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a projective
R
-resolution over
X
. Then
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\otimes_{R}S
is a projective
S
-resolution over
X
.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
4.16
that
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\otimes_{R}S
is an
S
-chain complex over
X
of projective
S
-modules over
X
. All that remains is to show
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\otimes_{R}S
is weakly uniformly acyclic. This argument is virtually identical to the proof of Proposition
6.27
: we first restrict to the case
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is the standard resolution, and then apply the Künneth spectral sequence, thinking of
S
as a chain complex of
R
-modules concentrated in dimension 0.
∎

7.
Coarse cohomology and homology

For every metric space
X
and
R
-module
\mathbf{M}
over
X
, we define the
coarse cohomology

H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;\mathbf{M})
and prove in Theorem
7.2
it is functorial with respect to coarse embeddings. We relate
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;\mathbf{M})
to Roe’s coarse cohomology in Proposition
7.5
. For a proper metric space
X
and commutative ring
R
, we define coarse homology
H^{\textrm{coarse}}_{k}(X;R)
. In Theorem
7.10
, we prove a version of the Künneth and Universal Coefficient Theorems relating coarse cohomology and homology, under the hypotheses of countable generation.

For the remainder of article, we introduce the following
sign convention
regarding the dual complex of a chain or cochain complex. If
C_{\bullet}
is a chain complex with boundary map
\partial:C_{k}\to C_{k-1}
for each
k
, then the cochain complex
\Hom_{R}(C_{\bullet},R)
has coboundary map
\delta:\Hom_{R}(C_{k},R)\to\Hom_{R}(C_{k+1},R)
is given by
\delta\alpha=(-1)^{k+1}\alpha\circ\partial
for each
\alpha\in\Hom_{R}(C_{k},R)
. We adopt a similar convention regarding the chain complex
\Hom_{R}(C^{\bullet},R)
that is the dual of a cochain complex
C^{\bullet}

We can now define cohomology modules
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;\mathbf{N})
of a metric space.
If
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a projective
R
-resolution over
X
and
\mathbf{N}
is an
R
-module over
X
, it follows from Proposition
5.3
that since the boundary maps have finite displacement over
\id_{X}
, there is an induced cochain complex
\Homfd(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\mathbf{N})
. We can now define coarse cohomology modules:

Definition 7.1
.

Let
X
be a metric space and let
\mathbf{N}
be an
R
-module over
X
. We define
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;\mathbf{N})\coloneqq H^{k}(\Homfd(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\mathbf{N}))
, where
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a projective
R
-resolution over
X
.

It follows from Corollary
6.14
and Proposition
5.3
that
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;\mathbf{N})
is well-defined, independent of the choice of projective
R
-resolution over
X
. More generally, we have the following:

Theorem 7.2
.

For any coarse embedding
f:X\to Y
and
R
-module
\mathbf{N}
over
X
, there is an induced map
f^{*}:H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(Y;f_{*}\mathbf{N})\to H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;\mathbf{N})
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, satisfying the following:

(1)

If
g:Y\to Z
is a coarse embedding, then
(gf)^{*}=f^{*}g^{*}
.

(2)

If
Y=X
and
f=\id_{X}
, then
f^{*}
is the identity.

(3)

If
g:X\to Y
is close to
f
, then
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(Y;f_{*}\mathbf{N})=H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(Y;g_{*}\mathbf{N})
and
f^{*}=g^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
be projective
R
-resolutions over
X
and
Y
respectively. By Proposition
6.12
,
f
induces an augmentation-preserving chain map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
of finite displacement over
f
. By Proposition
5.3
,
f_{\#}
induces cochain maps
\Homfd(\mathbf{D}_{\bullet},f_{*}\mathbf{N})\to\Homfd(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\mathbf{N})
, which in turn induces the required maps
f^{*}:H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(Y;f_{*}\mathbf{N})\to H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;\mathbf{N})
in cohomology. Proposition
6.12
also ensures that such a map
f_{\#}
is unique up to a finite displacement chain homotopy. It follows
f^{*}
does not depend on the choice of chain map
f_{\#}
.

(
1
): Let
\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}
be a projective
R
-resolution over
Z
and let
g_{\#}:\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}
be a map of finite displacement over
g
. Since the composition
g_{\#}f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}
has finite displacement over
gf
by Lemma
3.11
, it follows that
(gf)^{*}=f^{*}g^{*}
on the level of cohomology.

(
2
): This is clear, since the identity map
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
has finite displacement over
\id_{X}
.

(
3
): Although
f_{*}\mathbf{N}
and
g_{*}\mathbf{N}
are not necessarily equal, Remark
5.2
implies
\Homfd(\mathbf{D}_{\bullet},f_{*}\mathbf{N})=\Homfd(\mathbf{D}_{\bullet},g_{*}\mathbf{N})
. It follows
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(Y;f_{*}\mathbf{N})=H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(Y;g_{*}\mathbf{N})
. Since any map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
has finite displacement over
f
if and only if it has finite displacement over
g
, it immediately follows that
f^{*}=g^{*}
.
∎

Definition 7.3
.

If
X
is a metric space and
N
is an
R
-module, we define
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;N)=H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;\mathbf{N})
, where
\mathbf{N}
is a constant
R
-module over
X
with underlying
R
-module
N
.

For any coarse embedding
f:X\to Y
, we observe that
f_{*}\mathbf{N}
is a constant
R
-module over
Y
with underlying
R
-module
N
. It follows that
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(Y;f_{*}\mathbf{N})=H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(Y;N)
. We can thus restate Theorem
7.2
in this setting:

Corollary 7.4
.

For any coarse embedding
f:X\to Y
and
R
-module
N
, there is an induced map
f^{*}:H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(Y;N)\to H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;N)
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, satisfying (
1
) — (
3
) of Theorem
7.2
.

We now relate cohomology discussed above with Roe’s coarse cohomology
[
Roe93
,
Roe03
]
.
First, we recall some key definitions from
[
Roe03
]
, translated from the setting of abstract coarse spaces to metric spaces. If
X
is a metric space and
\Omega\subseteq X^{k+1}
, then:

•

\Omega
is
controlled
if there exists a constant
A
such that if
[x_{0},\dots,x_{k}]\in\Omega
, then
d(x_{i},x_{j})\leq A
for all
0\leq i,j\leq k
;

•

\Omega
is
bounded
if there is a bounded subset
F\subseteq X
such that if
[x_{0},\dots,x_{k}]\in\Omega
, then
x_{j}\in F
for each
0\leq j\leq k
;

•

\Omega
is
cocontrolled
if, for every controlled
\Psi\subseteq X^{k+1}
,
\Omega\cap\Psi
is bounded.

The
coarse complex of
X
with coefficients in
G
is then defined to be the cochain complex
CX^{\bullet}(X,G)
where
CX^{k}(X,G)
consists of functions
\phi:X^{k+1}\to G
with cocontrolled support, equipped with the coboundary map:

\delta\phi({[x_{0},\dots,x_{k+1}]})=(-1)^{k+1}\sum_{i=0}^{k+1}(-1)^{i}\phi([x_{0},\dots,\widehat{x_{i}},\dots,x_{k+1}]).

Roe defines the
coarse cohomology of
X
with coefficients in
G
to be

HX^{k}(X,G)\coloneqq H^{k}(CX^{\bullet}(X,G)).

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
X
be a metric space and let
G
be an abelian group. Then

H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;G)\cong HX^{k}(X;G)

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be the standard
\mathbb{Z}
-resolution over
X
, and fix
k
. Recall from Definition
6.18
that
\mathbf{C}_{k}
is a free abelian group with basis
B_{k}=X^{k+1}
. Moreover, if
i\geq k
, then
\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)\leq\mathbf{C}_{k}
is the free abelian group generated by
B_{k}(i)\subseteq B_{k}
, where
B_{k}(i)
consists of all
[x_{0},\dots,x_{k}]
with
d(x_{j},x_{l})\leq i
for all
0\leq j,l\leq k
. In particular, we note that each
B_{k}(i)\subseteq X^{k+1}
is controlled, and that every controlled set is contained in
\Omega\subseteq X^{k+1}
is contained in
B_{k}(i)
for
i
sufficiently large. Recall also that if
\sigma=[x_{0},\dots,x_{k}]\in B_{k}
, then
\supp_{\mathbf{C}_{k}}(\sigma)=\{x_{0}\}
.

Let
\alpha\in\Hom(\mathbf{C}_{k},G)
and
S(\alpha)\coloneqq\{\sigma\in X^{k+1}\mid\alpha(\sigma)\neq 0\}
. We claim
\alpha\in\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{C}_{k},G)
if and only if
S(\alpha)
is cocontrolled. Indeed, suppose
\alpha\in\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{C}_{k},G)
and
\Psi\subseteq X^{k+1}
is controlled. Then
\Psi\subseteq B_{k}(i)
for some
i
. By Definition
5.4
, there is a bounded subset
F
such that if
\sigma=[x_{0},\dots,x_{k}]\in B_{k}(i)\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
with
x_{0}\in X\setminus F
, then
\alpha(\sigma)=0
. Consequently, if
[x_{0},\dots,x_{k}]\in S(\alpha)\cap\Psi
, then
x_{j}\in N_{i}(F)
for each
0\leq j\leq k
, showing that
S(\alpha)
is cocontrolled.

Conversely, suppose
S(\alpha)
is cocontrolled and
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
S(\alpha)\cap B_{k}(i)
is bounded. Thus there is a bounded subset
F\subseteq X
such that if
[x_{0},\dots,x_{k}]\in S(\alpha)\cap B_{k}(i)
, then
x_{0}\in F
. This implies if
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
with
\supp(\sigma)\subseteq X\setminus F
, then
\alpha(\sigma)=0
. By Definition
5.4
, this ensures
\alpha\in\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{C}_{k},G)
.

The universal property of free abelian groups says that the functor
F:\texttt{Set}\to\texttt{Ab}
, taking
B
to the free abelian group
F(B)
with basis
B
, is left-adjoint to the forgetful functor
U:\texttt{Ab}\to\texttt{Set}
. Moreover, for an abelian group
G
, the natural isomorphism
\Hom_{\texttt{Set}}(B,U(G))\cong\Hom_{\texttt{Ab}}(F(B),G)
is an isomorphism of abelian groups. We have thus shown that this natural isomorphism yields an isomorphism
CX^{\bullet}(X;G)\cong\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},G)
of cochain complexes.
∎

For the remainder of this section, we restrict to the case that
X
is a proper metric space, and hence assume that
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a proper projective
R
-resolution over
X
.

We define
\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}=\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},R)
, equipped with the boundary map
\delta\alpha=(-1)^{k+1}\alpha\circ\partial
for each
\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{k}
. The subcomplex
\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},R)
of
\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}
is denoted
\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}
.
We define the
R
-chain complex
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf}
as follows. Each
\mathbf{C}_{k}^{\lf}
is defined as in Definition
5.16
and Proposition
5.17
, and we identify
\mathbf{C}_{k}
with a geometric submodule of
\mathbf{C}_{k}^{\lf}
as in Proposition
5.17
. The boundary map
\partial:\mathbf{C}_{k}^{\lf}\to\mathbf{C}_{k-1}^{\lf}
is induced by the boundary map
\partial:\mathbf{C}_{k}\to\mathbf{C}_{k-1}
as in Lemma
5.18
. This boundary map is obtained by dualising the coboundary map
\delta:\mathbf{C}^{k-1}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
and applying the sign convention.

Definition 7.6
.

If
X
is a proper metric space, we define
H^{\textrm{coarse}}_{k}(X;R)\coloneqq H_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf})
, where
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a proper projective
R
-resolution over
X
.

Proposition 7.7
.

For any coarse embedding
f:X\to Y
between proper metric spaces, there is an induced map
f_{k}:H^{\textrm{coarse}}_{k}(X;R)\to H^{\textrm{coarse}}_{k}(Y;R)
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, satisfying the following:

(1)

If
g:Y\to Z
is a coarse embedding, then
(gf)_{*}=g_{*}f_{*}
.

(2)

If
Y=X
and
f=\id_{X}
, then
f_{*}
is the identity map.

(3)

If
g:X\to Y
is close to
f
, then
f_{*}=g_{*}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
be projective
R
-resolutions over
X
and
Y
respectively. By Proposition
6.12
,
f
induces an augmentation-preserving map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
of finite displacement over
f
. By Lemma
5.18
,
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
extends to
f_{\#}^{\lf}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}^{\lf}
, and this induces maps
f_{*}:H_{*}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf})\to H_{*}(\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}^{\lf})
as required. Since
f_{\#}
is obtained by dualising the map
f^{\#}:\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{D}_{\bullet},R)\to\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},R)
, we can argue as in the proof of Theorem
7.2
to deduce the required properties.
∎

If
X
is a proper metric space and
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a proper projective
R
-resolution over
X
, then for each
k
, there is a pairing
H_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf})\times H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\to R
given by
[\tau]\times[\alpha]=\tau(\alpha)
.

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
X
be a proper metric space. There is a well-defined pairing

H^{\textrm{coarse}}_{*}(X;R)\times H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{*}(X;R)\to R

given by the natural pairing
H_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf})\times H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\to R
for some proper projective
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X
.

Proof.

We need to show the pairing doesn’t depend on the choice of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
. Indeed, suppose
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
are two proper projective
R
-resolutions over
X
. By Proposition
6.12
, there are finite displacement (over
\id_{X}
) chain maps
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
and
g_{\#}:\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
, unique up to finite displacement chain homotopy. Moreover, there is a finite displacement chain homotopy
\id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{\#}}}{{\simeq}}g_{\#}f_{\#}
. Then
f_{\#},g_{\#}
and
h_{\#}
induce maps
f^{\#}
,
g^{\#}
,
f_{\#}^{\lf}
,
g_{\#}^{\lf}
, and
h_{\#}^{\lf}
as in Proposition
5.7
and Lemma
5.18
, and these induce isomorphisms
f^{*}=(g^{*})^{-1}
and
f_{*}^{\lf}=(g_{*}^{\lf})^{-1}
on homology and cohomology.

Thus if
\tau\in\mathbf{C}_{n}^{\lf}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}
, we have

\displaystyle(f_{\#}^{\lf}\tau)(g^{\#}\alpha)=(g_{\#}^{\lf}f_{\#}^{\lf}\tau)(\alpha)=(\tau+h_{\#}\partial\tau+\partial h_{\#}\tau)(\alpha)=\tau(\alpha).

This implies the pairings
H_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf})\times H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\to R
and
H_{k}(\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}^{\lf})\times H^{k}(\mathbf{D}^{\bullet}_{c})\to R
coincide when we identify
H_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf})
with
H_{k}(\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}^{\lf})
via
f_{*}^{\lf}
, and
H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
with
H^{k}(\mathbf{D}^{\bullet}_{c})
via
g^{*}
.
∎

We now discuss versions of the Künneth and Universal Coefficient Theorem for coarse cohomology. These theorems do not hold in full generality, essentially because neither
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf}
nor
\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}
is a chain complex of projective
R
-modules.
We require the following lemma, which uses the language of inverse and direct limits. We refer the reader to Appendix
A
for background on these topics.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
X
be a proper metric space, and let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a proper projective
R
-resolution of
X
, that is also the standard
R
-resolution of some subspace
Y\subseteq X
, which exists by Corollary
6.20
.
Remark
6.21
implies that for
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i)
is a geometric subcomplex of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
of finite-height projective
R
-modules over
X
.
For each
i
, let
\mathbf{C}^{*}_{c}(i)
be the cochain complex
\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i),R)
.
Then:

(1)

\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}=\varprojlim\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(i)
, where the bonding maps
f_{i}^{j}:\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(j)\to\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(i)
for
i\leq j
are restriction maps. Moreover, the inverse system
\{\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(i)\}_{i}
satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition, and each
\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(i)
is a chain complex of countably generated proper projective
R
-modules over
X
.

(2)

\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf}=\varinjlim\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(i),R)
, where the bonding maps

g_{i}^{j}:\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(i),R)\to\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(j),R)

for
i\leq j
are dual to
f_{i}^{j}
.

Proof.

(
1
): Proposition
5.20
and Remark
6.21
imply that as each
\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
is a finite-height proper projective
R
-module over
X
, so is each
\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}(i)
. In particular, each
\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}(i)
is a countably generated
R
-module.
Remark
6.21
also says each
\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
is a direct summand of
\mathbf{C}_{k}
. Therefore, for each
i\leq j
, it follows that
\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
is a direct summand of
\mathbf{C}_{k}(j)
. This implies that each restriction map
f_{i}^{j}
is surjective. Thus
\{\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(i)\}_{i}
is an inverse system with surjective bonding maps, hence satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition.

Now we have

\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},R)=\Hom_{R}(\varinjlim\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i),R)\cong\varprojlim\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i),R),

and the projection
\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},R)\to\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i),R)
is the restriction map. Definition
5.4
implies that
\phi\in\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},R)
has locally bounded support if and only if the restriction of
\phi
to each geometric submodule of the form
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i)
has locally bounded support. This implies
\varprojlim\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(i)=\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}
.

(
2
): We remark that as
\mathbf{C}_{k}^{\lf}
is an
R
-module over
X
, it has a filtration
\{\mathbf{C}_{k}^{\lf}(i)\}
, and so
\varinjlim\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf}(i)=\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf}
. As the geometric submodule
\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)\leq\mathbf{C}_{k}
has finite height, we have
\Ann(\mathbf{C}_{k}(j))=0
for
j
sufficiently large. This ensures that
\mathbf{C}_{k}^{\lf}(i)=\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}(i),R)
for all
k
and
i
, yielding the desired equality.
∎

Combined with results from Appendix
A
, we deduce:

Theorem 7.10
.

Let
X
be a proper metric space. Assume that
R
is either a field or a countable PID, and that
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;R)
is a countably generated
R
-module for
k\leq n+1
. Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a proper projective
R
-resolution over
X
.

(1)

Let
f:R\to S
be a ring homomorphism and let
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}\coloneqq\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\otimes_{R}S
, which is a proper projective
S
-resolution over
X
by Proposition
6.28
. For each
k<n
, we have a natural short exact sequence

0\to H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\otimes_{R}S\xrightarrow{\phi}H^{k}(\mathbf{D}_{c}^{\bullet})\to\Tor_{1}^{R}(H^{k+1}(\mathbf{C}_{c}^{\bullet}),S)\to 0

and an injection
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\otimes_{R}S\xrightarrow{\phi}H^{n}(\mathbf{D}_{c}^{\bullet})
.
Moreover, the injections
\phi
are given by
[\alpha]\otimes s\mapsto[\alpha\otimes s]
.

(2)

We have surjections
H_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf})\xrightarrow{}\Hom_{R}(H^{k}(\mathbf{C}_{c}^{\bullet}),R)
for
k\leq n
, and also for
k=n+1
in the case
R
is a field. These surjections
\psi
are given by
\psi([\phi])([\alpha])=\phi(\alpha)
.

Proof.

By Corollary
6.14
, we can assume without loss of generality that
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
satisfies the hypothesis of Lemma
7.9
.
The result is now an immediate consequence of Lemma
7.9
combined with Corollary
A.9
and Theorem
A.12
.
∎

8.
Coarse cohomological dimension of a metric space

Given a metric space
X
and a commutative ring
R
, we define the
coarse cohomology

\ccd_{R}(X)
of
X
with respect to
R
, and give several equivalent formulations of
\ccd_{R}(X)
in Proposition
8.2
. We prove
\ccd_{R}(X)
is monotone under coarse embeddings in Theorem
8.3
. We show in Theorem
8.7
that
\ccd_{R}(G)
coincides with
\cd_{R}(G)
when
G
is a countable group and
\cd_{R}(G)<\infty
.

Recall a chain complex
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
n
-dimensional
if
\mathbf{C}_{i}=0
for all
i>n
.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
X
be a metric space. We define the
coarse cohomological dimension

\ccd_{R}(X)
of
X
to be the least
n
such that there exists an
n
-dimensional projective
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X
. If no such
n
exists, then
\ccd_{R}(X)=\infty
. We write
\ccd_{\mathbb{Z}}(X)
as
\ccd(X)
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
X
be a metric space and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

If
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is any projective
R
-resolution over
X
, then there exists a projective submodule
\mathbf{P}\leq\mathbf{C}_{n}
such that

\cdots\to 0\to\mathbf{P}\xrightarrow{\partial|_{\mathbf{P}}}\mathbf{C}_{n-1}\to\mathbf{C}_{n-2}\to\mathbf{C}_{n-3}\to\cdots

is a projective
R
-resolution over
X
.

(2)

\ccd_{R}(X)\leq n
.

(3)

H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X,\mathbf{N})=0
for all
k>n
and all
R
-modules
\mathbf{N}
over
X
.

(4)

H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n+1}(X,\mathbf{N})=0
for all
R
-modules
\mathbf{N}
over
X
.

(5)

If
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a projective
R
-resolution over
X
, then there exists a finite displacement retraction from
\mathbf{C}_{n}
to
\im(\mathbf{C}_{n+1}\to\mathbf{C}_{n})
.

Proof.

(\ref{item:propccdchars_2})\implies(\ref{item:propccdchars_1})
is obvious.

(\ref{item:propccdchars_1})\implies(\ref{item:propccdchars_3.0})
: Suppose
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a projective
R
-resolution over
X
with
\mathbf{C}_{k}=0
for
k>n
. Then for any
R
-module
\mathbf{N}
over
X
, the homology of
\Homfd(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\mathbf{N})
vanishes in dimensions greater than
n
.

(\ref{item:propccdchars_3.0})\implies(\ref{item:propccdchars_3})
is obvious.

(\ref{item:propccdchars_3})\implies(\ref{item:propccdchars_4})
: We define
\mathbf{N}\coloneqq\im(\mathbf{C}_{n+1}\to\mathbf{C}_{n})
. By hypothesis, we have
H^{n+1}(\Homfd(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\mathbf{N}))=0
.
We consider map
\phi:\mathbf{C}_{n+1}\to\mathbf{N}
given by
\phi(m)=\partial m
for all
m\in\mathbf{C}_{n+1}
.
It is clear that
\phi(\im(\mathbf{C}_{n+2}\to\mathbf{C}_{n+1}))=0
, so that
\delta\phi=0
and thus
\phi
is a cocycle. Hence there is a finite displacement map
\psi:\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\mathbf{N}
with
\delta\psi=(-1)^{n+1}\phi
. For each
n\in\mathbf{N}
, we have
n=\partial m
for some
m\in\mathbf{C}_{n+1}
, so that
\psi(n)=\psi(\partial m)=(-1)^{n+1}(\delta\psi)(m)=\phi(m)=\partial m=n
. Thus
\psi
is a finite displacement retraction onto
\mathbf{N}
.

(\ref{item:propccdchars_4})\implies(\ref{item:propccdchars_2})
is the content of Lemma
6.15
.
∎

In the following theorem, the relation
\leq
is defined on
\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
in the obvious way.

Theorem 8.3
.

If
R
is a commutative ring,
X
and
Y
are metric spaces, and
f:X\to Y
is a coarse embedding, then
\ccd_{R}(X)\leq\ccd_{R}(Y)
.

Proof.

There is nothing to show if
\ccd_{R}(Y)=\infty
, so assume
n=\ccd_{R}(Y)<\infty
and let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be an
R
-projective resolution over
Y
with
\mathbf{C}_{k}=0
for
k>n
. By Proposition
6.16
,
f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is an
R
-projective resolution over
X
. As
f^{*}\mathbf{C}_{k}=0
for all
k>n
, it follows that
\ccd_{R}(X)\leq n
as required.
∎

Theorem
8.3
readily implies:

Corollary 8.4
.

If
X
and
Y
are coarsely equivalent, then
\ccd_{R}(X)=\ccd_{R}(Y)
.

We can also compare coarse cohomological dimension between different rings:

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
R
and
S
be commutative rings such that there is a homomorphism
f:R\to S
. If
X
is any metric space, then
\ccd_{S}(X)\leq\ccd_{R}(X)
. In particular,
\ccd_{S}(X)\leq\ccd(X)
for every commutative ring
S
.

Proof.

If
\ccd_{R}(X)=\infty
, there is nothing to show, so assume
\ccd_{R}(X)=n<\infty
. Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a projective
R
-resolution over
X
with
\mathbf{C}_{k}=0
for
k>n
. Then
\mathbf{C}_{k}\otimes_{R}S
is a projective
S
-resolution over
X
by Proposition
6.28
, and satisfies
\mathbf{C}_{k}\otimes_{R}S=0
for
k>n
. Thus
\ccd_{S}(X)\leq n
by Proposition
8.2
. For every commutative ring
S
, there is a homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}\to S
, showing
\ccd_{S}(X)\leq\ccd_{\mathbb{Z}}(X)=\ccd(X)
as required.
∎

The following criterion yields a large class of groups of finite coarse cohomological dimension:

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
G
be a countable group acting properly and cocompactly on a contractible simplicial complex
X
of dimension
n
. Then
\ccd(G)\leq n
.

Proof.

Let
(X^{(1)},X)
be the associated metric simplicial complex as in Example
6.11
. Since
X
is contractible and
G
acts cocompactly on
X
,
X
is uniformly acyclic, and so the simplicial chain complex
C_{\bullet}(X)
can be given the structure of a projective
R
-resolution over
X^{(1)}
as in Example
6.11
. As
X
is
n
-dimensional,
\ccd(X)\leq n
. Since
G
acts properly and cocompactly on
X^{(1)}
, it is quasi-isometric to
X^{(1)}
, and so Corollary
8.4
implies
\ccd(G)=\ccd(X)\leq n
.
∎

A priori,
\ccd_{R}(X)
may be hard to compute for general metric spaces. Fortunately, when
G
is a countable group,
\ccd_{R}(G)
agrees with the (virtual) cohomological dimension of
G
when finite:

Theorem 8.7
.

Let
G
be a countable group. If
\cd_{R}(G)<\infty
, then
\ccd_{R}(G)=\cd_{R}(G)
.

Before proving Theorem
8.7
, we need to prove some results relating group cohomology to coarse cohomology.
Let
R
be a commutative ring, let
G
be a group and let
N
be an
R
-module.
Then
RG\otimes_{R}N
admits both a left
G
-action given by
g\cdot(k\otimes n)=gk\otimes n
, and a right
G
-action given by
(k\otimes n)\cdot g=kg\otimes n
, for all
g,k\in G
and
n\in N
.
If
M
is a left
RG
-module and
N
is an
R
-module, then
\Hom_{RG}(M,RG\otimes_{R}N)
consists of the collection of all
G
-equivariant
R
-module homomorphisms with respect to the left
G
-action on
M
and on
RG\otimes_{R}N
. Thus
\Hom_{RG}(M,RG\otimes_{R}N)
admits a right
G
-action given by
(\phi\cdot g)(m)=\phi(m)\cdot g
.

If
\mathbf{M}
is a
G
-induced projective
R
-module over
G
and
N
is an
R
-module, then
\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{M},N)
admits a right
G
-action given by
(\alpha\cdot g)(m)=\alpha(gm)
for all
g\in G
,
\alpha\in\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{M},N)
and
m\in\mathbf{M}
. We now have the following:

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
G
be a countable group and
let
\mathbf{M}
be a
G
-induced projective
R
-module over
G
with underlying
R
-module
M
. Let
N
be an
R
-module. Then there is an isomorphism
\Lambda_{M}=\Lambda:\Hom_{RG}(M,RG\otimes_{R}N)\cong\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{M},N)
as right
RG
-modules. Moreover,
\Lambda
is natural in the following sense: If
\mathbf{L}
is a
G
-induced projective
R
-module over
G
and
\xi:\mathbf{L}\to\mathbf{M}
is an
RG
-module homomorphism, then the diagram in Figure
4
commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom_{RG}(M,RG\otimes_{R}N)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom_{RG}(L,RG\otimes_{R}N)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{M},N)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{L},N)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\mapsto\phi\circ\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\mapsto\psi\circ\xi}

Figure 4
.
Naturality of the isomorphism
\Lambda_{M}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{M}=(M,B,\delta,p,\{B(i)\},\{m_{b}\})
and
\phi\in\Hom_{RG}(M,RG\otimes_{R}N)
. Then there exists a family
\{\phi_{g}:M\to N\}_{g\in G}
of
R
-module homomorphisms such that
\phi(m)=\sum_{g\in G}g\otimes\phi_{g}(m)
for all
m\in M
. For each
m\in M
, there are only finitely many
g\in G
such that
\phi_{g}(m)\neq 0
.

For each
h\in G
and
m\in M
, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{g\in G}g\otimes\phi_{g}(hm)=\phi(hm)=h\phi(m)=\sum_{g\in G}hg\otimes\phi_{g}(m)=\sum_{g\in G}g\otimes\phi_{h^{-1}g}(m).

This implies that
\phi_{g}(m)=\phi_{1}(g^{-1}m)
for all
g\in G
and
m\in M
. We claim that for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

Z_{i}\coloneqq\{b\in B(i)\mid\phi_{1}(m_{b})\neq 0\}

is finite, which by Proposition
5.9
, will imply that
\phi_{1}\in\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{M},N)
. To see this, we fix
i
and recall that
G
acts freely on
B(i)
with finitely many orbits. Let
\{b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\}
be a set of
G
-transversals of
B(i)
.
As noted above, the set
R_{i}\coloneqq\{g\in G\mid\phi_{g}(m_{b_{j}})\neq 0\text{ for some $1\leq j\leq n$}\}
is finite. Thus if
\phi_{1}(m_{b})\neq 0
for some
b=gb_{j}\in B(i)
, then
0\neq\phi_{1}(m_{b})=\phi_{1}(gm_{b_{j}})=\phi_{g^{-1}}(m_{b_{j}})
, which implies
g^{-1}\in R_{i}
. Hence there are only finitely many such
g
, and so
Z_{i}
is finite.

We thus define the map
\Lambda:\Hom_{RG}(M,RG\otimes_{R}N)\to\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{M},N)
by
\Lambda(\phi)=\phi_{1}
. It is easy to see that
\Lambda
is an
R
-module homomorphism.
Now for
h\in G
, we have
(\phi\cdot h)(m)=\sum_{g\in G}gh\otimes\phi_{g}(m)=\sum_{g\in G}g\otimes\phi_{gh^{-1}}(m)
, and so
\Lambda(\phi\cdot h)=\phi_{h^{-1}}
. Since
\phi_{h^{-1}}(m)=\phi_{1}(hm)=(\phi_{1}\cdot h)(m)
, we see
\Lambda(\phi\cdot h)=\phi_{1}\cdot h
, and so
\Lambda
is a homomorphism of right
RG
-modules.
Moreover, if
\Lambda(\phi)=0
, then
\phi_{1}=0
and so, since
\phi_{g}(m)=\phi_{1}(g^{-1}m)=0
for all
m\in\mathbf{M}
and
g\in G
, we see that
\phi=0
. Thus
\Lambda
is injective.

We now show
\Lambda
is surjective. For
\psi\in\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{M},N)

we define
\phi:\mathbf{M}\to RG\otimes N
by
\phi(m)=\sum_{g\in G}g\otimes\psi(g^{-1}m)
, noting that the condition
\psi\in\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{M},N)
ensures that all but finitely many terms of the sum defining
\phi(m)
are zero. Applying the preceding arguments in reverse, it can be shown that
\phi\in\Hom_{RG}(M,RG\otimes_{R}N)
and
\Lambda(\phi)=\psi
.

Finally, to see naturality of
\Lambda
, suppose we have a homomorphism
\phi\in\Hom_{RG}(\mathbf{M},RG\otimes_{R}N)
and a
G
-equivariant map
\xi:\mathbf{L}\to\mathbf{M}
. We note that for each
l\in L
, we have

\displaystyle(\xi^{*}\Lambda_{\mathbf{M}}(\phi))(l)

\displaystyle=(\Lambda_{\mathbf{M}}(\phi))(\xi(l))=\phi_{1}(\xi(l))=(\phi\circ\xi)_{1}(l)

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{\mathbf{L}}(\phi\circ\xi)(l)=\Lambda_{\mathbf{L}}(\xi^{*}(\phi))(l)

as required.
∎

Let
G
be a countable group and let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a projective
R
-resolution over
G
as in Corollary
6.22
, in which each
\mathbf{C}_{k}
is a
G
-induced projective
R
-module over
G
and the boundary maps are
G
-equivariant. By Lemma
8.8
, for each
R
-module
N
, we have an isomorphism
\Hom_{RG}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},RG\otimes_{R}N)\cong\Hom_{c}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},N)
of cochain complexes that is
G
-equivariant with respect to the right
G
-actions. The cohomology of these cochain complexes thus inherit the structure of a right
G
-module, and we deduce the following:

Corollary 8.9
.

Let
G
be a countable group and let
N
be an
R
-module. Then
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(G;N)\cong H^{k}(G,RG\otimes_{R}N)
as right
RG
-modules for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof of Theorem
8.7
.

Let
n=\cd_{R}(G)<\infty
. By
[
Bie81
, Proposition 5.1]
, we have
H^{n}(G,F)\neq 0
for some free
RG
-module
F
. We now note that
F=RG\otimes_{R}N
for some free
R
-module
N
, so Corollary
8.9
implies that
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(G;N)\cong H^{n}(G,F)\neq 0
. It follows from Proposition
8.2
that
\ccd_{R}(G)\geq\cd_{R}(G)=n
.
We now show
\ccd_{R}(G)\leq\cd_{R}(G)
. In the case
R
is Noetherian, any projective resolution of the trivial
RG
-module
R
yields a projective
R
-resolution over
G
by Lemma
4.8
and Propositions
4.10
and
4.12
. This implies the inequality
\ccd_{R}(G)\leq\cd_{R}(G)
when applied to a length
n
projective resolution of the trivial
RG
-module
R
. For more general
R
, Proposition
4.12
cannot be applied, so we require another argument.

We pick
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
as in the discussion preceding Corollary
8.9
. We claim there is a
G
-equivariant retraction
\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\mathbf{C}_{n}
onto
\im(\mathbf{C}_{n+1}\to\mathbf{C}_{n})
. By Proposition
4.10
, such a map will have finite displacement, so Proposition
8.2
will imply
\ccd_{R}(G)\leq n=\cd_{R}(G)
. It remains to prove the claim. We first assume
n>1
.
By
[
Bie81
, Proposition 4.1b)]
,
\ker(\mathbf{C}_{n-1}\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbf{C}_{n-2})=\im(\mathbf{C}_{n}\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbf{C}_{n-1})
is projective as an
RG
-module.
This implies that the short exact sequence

1\to\ker(\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\mathbf{C}_{n-1})\to\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\im(\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\mathbf{C}_{n-1})\to 1

splits
G
-equivariantly, so there is a
G
-equivariant retraction
\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\mathbf{C}_{n}
onto
\ker(\mathbf{C}_{n}\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbf{C}_{n-1})=\im(\mathbf{C}_{n+1}\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbf{C}_{n})
, proving the claim when
n>1
.
For
n=0
(resp.
n=1
) the argument is essentially identical, except that
[
Bie81
, Proposition 4.1b)]
tells us that the trivial
R
-module
R
(resp.
\ker(\mathbf{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\epsilon}R)=\im(\mathbf{C}_{1}\to\mathbf{C}_{0})
) is a projective
R
-module. In both cases, we obtain a
G
-equivariant retraction
\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\mathbf{C}_{n}
onto
\im(\mathbf{C}_{n+1}\to\mathbf{C}_{n})
as required.
∎

We now classify spaces of coarse cohomological dimension zero:

Proposition 8.10
.

If
R
is a commutative ring and
X
is a metric space, then
\ccd_{R}(X)=0
if and only if
X
is bounded.

Proof.

If
X
is bounded, then it is coarsely equivalent to a point
Y=\{y\}
. Let
C_{0}=Rb\cong R
be the free
R
-module with basis
b
. Let
\mathbf{C}_{0}=(C_{0},\{b\},\delta,p,\{B(i)\})
, with
\delta_{b}(rb)=r
,
p(b)=y
and
B(i)=\{b\}
for all
i
. Thus
\mathbf{C}_{0}
is a free
R
-module over
Y
. Letting
\mathbf{C}_{i}=0
for
i\neq 0
, and defining the augmentation
\epsilon:\mathbf{C}_{0}\cong R
by
\epsilon(b)=1
, we have
(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\epsilon)
is a projective
R
-resolution over
Y
, so
\ccd_{R}(Y)=0
. It follows from Corollary
8.4
that
\ccd_{R}(X)=\ccd_{R}(Y)=0
.

Conversely, suppose
\ccd_{R}(X)=0
. Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be the standard resolution of
X
. By Proposition
8.2
, there is a finite displacement retraction from
\mathbf{C}_{0}
onto
\mathbf{N}\coloneqq\im(\mathbf{C}_{1}\to\mathbf{C}_{0})
. By Lemma
4.17
,
\mathbf{N}
has a complement
\mathbf{M}
such that the projection
\phi:\mathbf{C}_{0}=\mathbf{N}\oplus\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{M}
has finite displacement
\Phi
. Moreover, as
\epsilon
vanishes on
\mathbf{N}
,
\phi
is augmentation-preserving and
\epsilon
restricts to an isomorphism
\mathbf{M}\cong\mathbf{C}_{0}/\mathbf{N}\cong R
.
Thus there is a unique
m_{0}\in\mathbf{M}
such that
\epsilon(m_{0})=1
.

Set
L\coloneqq\supp_{\mathbf{C}_{0}}(m_{0})
, which is bounded.
Recall from Definition
6.18
that
\mathbf{C}_{0}
has a basis
B_{0}=B_{0}(0)=\{[x]\}_{x\in X}
with
\epsilon([x])=1
and
\supp_{\mathbf{C}_{0}}([x])=\{x\}
for all
x\in X
. Set
m_{x}=(x)
for each
x
. Since
\phi([x])\in\mathbf{M}
and
\epsilon(\phi([x]))=\epsilon([x])=1
, it follows
\phi([x])=m_{0}
for all
x\in X
. Then
L=\supp_{\mathbf{C}_{0}}(\phi([x]))\subseteq N_{\Phi(0)}(\supp_{\mathbf{C}_{0}}([x]))=N_{\Phi(0)}(x)
for all
x\in X
. It follows that
X\subseteq N_{\Phi(0)}(L)
, and so
X
is bounded as required.
∎

9.
Uniform acyclicity and finite type resolutions

In this section we study spaces that are coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic over
R
. In Proposition
9.3
, we show
X
is coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic over
R
if and only if there exists a projective
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X
such that
\mathbf{C}_{i}
is of finite height for
i\leq n
. The latter characterisation is a metric space analogue of being of type
FP_{n}^{R}
. In Theorem
9.11
we show that for spaces of coarse finite type,
\ccd_{R}(X)
is the largest
n
for which
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(X;R)\neq 0
, generalising an analogous result in group cohomology. We apply this result to compare the coarse cohomological and asymptotic dimensions (Proposition
9.12
), and to compute the coarse cohomological dimension of all hyperbolic and
\CAT(0)
groups in terms of their boundaries (Corollary
9.16
).

We characterise coarse uniform acyclicity of
X
in terms of projective resolutions over
X
. To do this, we require the following:

Definition 9.1
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring and let
X
be a metric space.
Suppose
\lambda:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
and
\mu:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
are increasing functions such that
\lambda(i)\geq i
and
\mu(i,r)\geq r
for all
i,r\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a projective
R
-resolution over
X
augmented
R
-metric complex over
X
.
We say that
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
(\lambda,\mu)
-uniformly
n
-acyclic
if for all
i,r\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\leq n
and
x\in X
, the map

\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(x)))\to\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(\lambda(i),N_{\mu(i,r)}(x))),

induced by inclusion, is zero. We say
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
uniformly
n
-acyclic
if it is
(\lambda,\mu)
-uniformly
n
-acyclic for some
(\lambda,\mu)
, and that
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
uniformly acyclic
if it is uniformly
n
-acyclic for all
n
.

Remark 9.2
.

To avoid confusion between Definitions
6.5
and
9.1
, we briefly explain the differences between weak uniform acyclicity and uniform acyclicity.
If
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a projective
R
-resolution over
X
, then weak uniform acyclicity of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
, which is always assumed by Definition
6.9
, ensures there exist functions
\lambda,\mu:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for all
i,r\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\leq n
and
x\in X
, the map

\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(x)))\to\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(\lambda(i,r),N_{\mu(i,r)}(x))),

induced by inclusion, is zero. The crucial part of being uniformly
n
-acyclic in Definition
9.1
is that there exists
\lambda
as above that does not depend on
r
.

Proposition 9.3
.

Let
X
be a metric space, let
R
be a commutative ring and let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

X
is coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic over
R
.

(2)

There exists a projective
R
-resolution over
X
that is uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic.

(3)

Every projective
R
-resolution over
X
is uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic.

(4)

There exists a free
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X
such that
\mathbf{C}_{k}
has finite height for each
k\leq n
.

(5)

There exists a projective
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X
such that
\mathbf{C}_{k}
has finite height for each
k\leq n
.

Moreover, if
X
is proper and is coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic over
R
, then there exists a proper free
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X
such that
\mathbf{C}_{k}
has finite height for each
k\leq n
.

Proof.

(
1
)
\implies
(
2
): Let
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
be the ordered standard
R
-resolution over
X
. Suppose that
X
is uniformly
(\lambda,\mu)
-coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic over
R
. Without loss of generality, we may assume
\lambda
and
\mu
restrict to functions
\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
respectively. We claim that for all
k<n
and
i,r\in\mathbb{N}
, the map

(9.1)

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(x)))\to\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}(\lambda(i),N_{\mu(i,r+i)}(x))),

induced by inclusion, is zero. In the case
k>i
, we have
\mathbf{D}_{k}(i,N_{r}(x))\subseteq\mathbf{D}_{k}(i)=0
and there is nothing to show, so assume
k\leq i
. Then Lemma
6.25
ensures that (
9.1
) factors through the map

\widetilde{H}_{k}(C_{\bullet}(P_{i}(N_{r+i}(x));R))\to\widetilde{H}_{k}(C_{\bullet}(P_{\lambda(i)}(N_{\mu(i,r+i)}(x));R)),

which is zero by Definition
2.3
.

(
2
)
\implies
(
3
): Let
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be projective
R
-resolutions over
X
, and assume that
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
(\lambda,\mu)
-uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic. By Proposition
6.12
, there exist augmentation-preserving finite displacement chain maps
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
and
g_{\#}:\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and a finite displacement chain homotopy
\id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{\#}}}{{\simeq}}f_{\#}g_{\#}
. Using Lemma
6.4
, there is a function
\Phi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for all
x\in X
and
i,r,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\leq n
,

(9.2)

\displaystyle f_{\#}(\mathbf{C}_{k}(i,N_{r}(x)))\subseteq\mathbf{D}_{k}(\Phi(i),N_{r+\Phi(i)}(x)),\quad g_{\#}(\mathbf{D}_{k}(i,N_{r}(x)))\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{k}(\Phi(i),N_{r+\Phi(i)}(x))

and

\displaystyle h_{\#}(\mathbf{D}_{k}(i,N_{r}(x)))\subseteq\mathbf{D}_{k+1}(\Phi(i),N_{r+\Phi(i)}(x)).

This implies that for all
x\in X
and
i,r,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k<n
, the map

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(x)))\to\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}(\Phi(\lambda(\Phi(i))),N_{\Phi(\lambda(\Phi(i)))+\mu(\Phi(i),r+\Phi(i))}(x))),

induced by inclusion, factors through

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(\Phi(i),N_{r+\Phi(i)}(x)))\to\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(\lambda(\Phi(i)),N_{\mu(\Phi(i),r+\Phi(i))}(x))),

which is zero because
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
(\lambda,\mu)
-uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic.

(
3
)
\implies
(
1
): Let
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
be the ordered standard
R
-resolution over
X
. By assumption,
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
is
(\lambda,\mu)
-uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic. Lemma
6.25
ensures that for all
x\in X
and
i,r,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k<n
, the map

\widetilde{H}_{k}(C_{\bullet}(P_{i}(N_{r}(x));R))\to\widetilde{H}_{k}(C_{\bullet}(P_{\lambda(i)}(N_{\lambda(i)+\mu(i,r)}(x));R)),

induced by inclusion, factors through the map

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(x)))\to\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}(\lambda(i),N_{\mu(i,r)}(x))),

hence is zero. Thus
X
is coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic over
R
.

(
3
)
\implies
(
4
): We prove this by induction on
n
. For the base case, we note that for any metric space
X
, if
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is the standard resolution of
X
, then
\mathbf{C}_{0}
has finite height.
For the inductive hypotheses, we assume the standard resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
of
X
is
(\lambda,\mu)
-uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic and that
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
is some free
R
-resolution over
X
such that
\mathbf{D}_{k}
has finite height for
k<n
. By Proposition
6.12
, there exist augmentation-preserving finite displacement chain maps
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
,
g_{\#}:\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
, and a finite displacement chain homotopy
\id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{\#}}}{{\simeq}}f_{\#}g_{\#}
. By Lemma
6.4
, we can pick
\Phi
such that each of these maps has displacement
\Phi
, and that (
9.2
) holds.

Pick
i_{0}
such that
\mathbf{D}_{n-1}=\mathbf{D}_{n-1}(i_{0})
. Set
i_{1}\coloneqq\Phi(i_{0})
and
i_{2}\coloneqq\lambda(i_{1})
. We set
\mathbf{E}_{n}\coloneqq\mathbf{D}_{n-1}\oplus\mathbf{C}_{n}(i_{2})
, which is a finite height free
R
-module over
X
by Remark
6.21
and Propositions
3.27
and
4.14
. A boundary map
\partial:\mathbf{E}_{n}\to\mathbf{D}_{n-1}
is defined by

\partial(\sigma,\omega)=\sigma+h_{\#}\partial\sigma+f_{\#}\partial\omega-f_{\#}g_{\#}\sigma.

We note that
\partial
has finite displacement and
\partial\partial(\sigma,\omega)=\partial\sigma+\partial h_{\#}\partial\sigma-f_{\#}g_{\#}\partial\sigma=-h_{\#}\partial^{2}\sigma=0
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathbf{D}_{n-1}(i_{0},N_{r}(x))
be a reduced cycle, so that
g_{\#}(\sigma)
is a reduced cycle in
\mathbf{C}_{n-1}(i_{1},N_{r+i_{1}}(x))
. Thus there is a chain
\omega\in\mathbf{C}_{n}(i_{2},N_{\mu(i_{1},r+i_{1})}(x))
with
\partial\omega=g_{\#}\sigma
. It follows that
\partial(\sigma,\omega)=\sigma+h_{\#}\partial\sigma+f_{\#}g_{\#}\sigma-f_{\#}g_{\#}\sigma=\sigma
. Thus

\mathbf{E}_{n}\to\mathbf{D}_{n-1}\to\dots\to\mathbf{D}_{0}\to 0

is a partial free resolution over
X
of dimension
n
in which
\mathbf{E}_{n}
has finite height. By Proposition
6.26
, we can extend this partial free
R
-resolution over
X
to a free
R
-resolution over
X
, proving the inductive hypothesis.

We now prove the “moreover” part of Proposition
9.3
. In the case
X
is proper, we let
Y\subseteq X
be a subspace as in Corollary
6.20
.
We carry out the preceding argument for
Y
, assuming as part of our inductive hypothesis that
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
is proper, and noting the standard resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
Y
is proper.
This allows us to construct a proper projective
R
-resolution
\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}
over
Y
such that
\mathbf{E}_{k}
has finite height for each
k\leq n
.
If
f:X\to Y
is a closest point projection, Propositions
3.27
and
6.16
ensure the pullback
f^{*}\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}
is proper projective
R
-resolution over
X
such that
f^{*}\mathbf{E}_{k}
has finite height for each
k\leq n
.

(
4
)
\implies
(
5
) is obvious.

(
5
)
\implies
(
2
):
Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a projective
R
-resolution over
X
such that
\mathbf{C}_{k}
has height
i_{0}<\infty
for each
k\leq n
. Thus
\mathbf{C}_{k}(i,Y)=\mathbf{C}_{k}(i_{0},Y)
for all
i\geq i_{0}
and
Y\subseteq X
. Since
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
a projective
R
-resolution over
X
, it is
\Omega
-weakly uniformly acyclic for some
\Omega
. Defining
\lambda:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by
\lambda(i)=\max(i,i_{0})
, it follows that for all
x\in X
and
i,r,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k<n
, the map

\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i,N_{r}(x)))\to\widetilde{H}_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(\lambda(i),N_{\Omega(i,r)}(x))),

induced by inclusion, is zero. This shows
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic (see Remark
9.2
).
∎

Using Proposition
9.3
and its proof, we deduce the following:

Corollary 9.4
.

Let
X
be a metric space and let
R
be a commutative ring. The following are equivalent:

(1)

X
is coarsely uniformly acyclic over
R
.

(2)

There exists a finite-height free
R
-resolution over
X
.

(3)

There exists a finite-height projective
R
-resolution over
X
.

We now consider the following class of metric spaces:

Definition 9.5
.

A metric space
X
is said to be of
coarse finite type over
R
if there exists a finite-dimensional finite-height projective
R
-resolution over
X
.

Proposition 9.6
.

Let
X
be a metric space that is coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic over
R
with
\ccd_{R}(X)\leq n
. Then there exists a finite height
n
-dimensional projective
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X
, which is proper when
X
is proper. In particular,
X
has coarse finite type over
R
.

Proof.

By Proposition
9.3
, there exists a projective
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
with
\mathbf{C}_{k}
of finite height for
k\leq n
, which is proper when
X
is proper. By Proposition
8.2
, there exists a projective submodule
\mathbf{P}\leq\mathbf{C}_{n}
such that
0\to\mathbf{P}\xrightarrow{\partial|_{\mathbf{P}}}\mathbf{C}_{n-1}\to\dots\to\mathbf{C}_{0}\to 0
is the required finite height projective
R
-resolution over
X
, noting that since
\mathbf{C}_{n}
has finite height, so does
\mathbf{P}
.
∎

It is not known to the author if the following is true:

Question 9.7
.

If
X
is coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic over
R
and
\ccd_{R}(X)\leq n
, does there exist a finite-height
n
-dimensional
free

R
-resolution over
X
?

This question is a coarse analogue of the following open problem regarding groups: is every group of type
FP
also of type
FL
?

The following lemma shows that certain coarse cohomology modules of coarsely uniformly acyclic metric spaces are countably generated:

Proposition 9.8
.

Let
R
be a PID.
Let
X
be a proper coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic metric space over
R
. Then
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;R)
is a countably generated
R
-module for
k\leq n
.

Proof.

By Proposition
9.3
, there exists a proper projective
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X
such that
\mathbf{C}_{k}
has finite height for each
k\leq n
. By Proposition
5.20
,
\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
is a finite height proper projective
R
-module over
X
for each
k\leq n
. Thus
\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
is countably generated for
k\leq n
. Since
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;R)\cong H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
is isomorphic to a quotient of a submodule of
\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
, the result follows by Lemma
A.1
.
∎

Definition 9.9
.

A metric space
X
is
coarsely homogeneous
if there exist
\Lambda
,
\Upsilon
such that for every
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
, there exists a
(\Lambda,\Upsilon)
-coarse equivalence
f:X\to X
with
f(x_{1})=x_{2}
.

For instance, any metric space admitting a cocompact isometric group action, or more generally a cobounded quasi-action, is coarsely homogeneous.

Proposition 9.10
.

Assume
R
is a Noetherian ring. Suppose
X
is a coarsely homogeneous proper metric space. Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a proper projective
R
-resolution over
X
such that
\mathbf{C}_{k}
is of finite height for
k\leq n
.

(1)

For
k\leq n
, the coboundary map
\delta:\mathbf{C}^{k-1}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
has uniform preimages.

(2)

Suppose that
H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
is a finitely generated
R
-module for some
k\leq n
. Then there is a constant
D
such for every
x\in X
and class
[\alpha]\in H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
, there is a cocycle
\alpha_{x}\in\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
such that
[\alpha_{x}]=[\alpha]
and
\supp(\alpha_{x})\subseteq N_{D}(x)
.

Proof.

We fix
i_{0}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{k}(i_{0})=\mathbf{C}_{k}
for all
k\leq n
, and fix
\Lambda,\Upsilon
as in Definition
9.9
. By Proposition
5.20
, this implies that
\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}(i_{0})=\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
for all
k\leq n
.
Fix a basepoint
x_{0}\in X
and for each
x\in X
, choose a
(\Lambda,\Upsilon)
-coarse equivalence
f_{x}:X\to X
with
f_{x}(x_{0})=x
.
Setting
A\coloneqq\Upsilon(0)
, we see that for each
x\in X
there exists a
(\Lambda,\Upsilon)
-coarse equivalence
g_{x}:X\to X
that is an
A
-coarse inverse to
f_{x}
. By applying Propositions
6.12
and
5.20
, there exists
\Phi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that:

(1)

For every
x\in X
, there exist augmentation-preserving chain homotopy-equivalences
(f_{x})_{\#},(g_{x})_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
of displacement
\Phi
over
f_{x}
and
g_{x}
.

(2)

For each
x\in X
, there exists a chain homotopy
\id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(h_{x})_{\#}}}{{\simeq}}(g_{x})_{\#}(f_{x})_{\#}
of displacement
\Phi
over
\id_{X}
.

(3)

The induced dual maps
(f_{x})^{\#}
,
(g_{x})^{\#}
and
(h_{x})^{\#}
have displacement
\Phi
over
g_{x}
,
f_{x}
and
\id_{X}
, when restricted to
\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
for
k\leq n
.

We define
i_{1}\coloneqq\Phi(i_{0})

(
1
): There is nothing to prove when
k=0
, so fix
1\leq k\leq n
. For each
r\geq 0
, let
B_{r}
be the submodule of
\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
generated by

\{\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}\mid\supp(\alpha)\subseteq N_{i_{1}+\Upsilon(r)+A}(x_{0})\}.

Since
R
is Noetherian and
\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
is proper and of finite height,
B_{r}
is a finitely generated
R
-module.

Since
R
is Noetherian,
B_{r}\cap\im(\mathbf{C}^{k-1}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c})
is also a finitely generated
R
-module, so we can pick
s_{r}\geq r
such that for every
\alpha\in B_{r}\cap\im(\mathbf{C}^{k-1}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c})
, there exists
\beta\in\mathbf{C}^{k-1}_{c}
with
\supp(\beta)\subseteq N_{s_{r}}(x_{0})
and
\delta\beta=\alpha
. We pick an increasing function
\Omega:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that

\Omega(r)\geq i_{1}+\Upsilon(s_{r})+r.

We claim
\delta:\mathbf{C}^{k-1}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
has
\Omega
-uniform preimages. To see this,
we fix
\alpha\in\im(\mathbf{C}^{k-1}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c})
with
\supp(\alpha)\subseteq N_{r}(x)
, and will show there exists
\beta\in\mathbf{C}^{k-1}_{c}
with
\supp(\alpha)\subseteq N_{\Omega(r)}(x)
and
\delta\beta=\alpha
.
We first observe that

\displaystyle\supp((f_{x})^{\#}(\alpha))\subseteq N_{i_{1}}(g_{x}(\supp(\alpha)))\subseteq N_{i_{1}+\Upsilon(r)+A}(x_{0}),

so that
(f_{x})^{\#}(\alpha)\in B_{r}\cap\im(\mathbf{C}^{k-1}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c})
.
Thus there exists
\beta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{C}^{k-1}_{c}
with
\supp(\beta^{\prime})\subseteq N_{s_{r}}(x_{0})
and
\delta\beta^{\prime}=(f_{x})^{\#}\alpha
. We set
\beta=(g_{x})^{\#}\beta^{\prime}-(h_{x})^{\#}\alpha
, so that

\supp(\beta)\subseteq N_{i_{1}}(f_{x}(\supp(\beta^{\prime})))\cup N_{i_{1}+r}(x)\subseteq N_{\Omega(r)}(x)

and

\delta\beta=(g_{x})^{\#}(f_{x})^{\#}\alpha-\delta(h_{x})^{\#}\alpha=\alpha+(h_{x})^{\#}\delta\alpha=\alpha.

Thus the coboundary map
\delta:\mathbf{C}^{k-1}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
has
\Omega
-uniform preimages.

(
2
): Pick a collection
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}
of cocycles in
\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
such that
[\alpha_{1}],\dots,[\alpha_{m}]
generate the finitely generated
R
-module
H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
. We can thus pick
D_{0}
large enough so that
\supp(\alpha_{i})\subseteq N_{D_{0}}(x_{0})
for all
1\leq i\leq m
. Set
D\coloneqq i_{1}+\Upsilon(D_{0})
. Then for each
x\in X
and
1\leq i\leq m
, we have

\displaystyle\supp(g^{\#}_{x}\alpha_{i})\subseteq N_{i_{1}}(f_{x}(\supp(\alpha_{i})))\subseteq N_{i_{1}}(f_{x}(N_{D_{0}}(x_{0})))\subseteq N_{D}(x).

Now for each
x\in X
, as
g_{\#}^{x}
is a chain homotopy equivalence, it follows that
g^{\#}_{x}:\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}
induces isomorphisms
g^{*}_{x}:H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\to H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
. Therefore,
[g^{\#}_{x}\alpha_{1}],\dots,[g^{\#}_{x}\alpha_{m}]
generate
H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
. It follows that each class
[\alpha]\in H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
can be represented by a cocycle
\alpha_{x}
that is an
R
-linear combination of
g^{\#}_{x}\alpha_{1},\dots,g^{\#}_{x}\alpha_{m}
. By Lemma
3.4
, we deduce that
\supp(\alpha_{x})\subseteq N_{D}(x)
as required.
∎

A basic result and very useful result in group cohomology is that if
G
is of type
FP
, then
\cd(G)=\max\{n\mid H^{n}(G,\mathbb{Z}G)\neq 0\}

[
Bro82
, Proposition VIII.6.7]
.
The following theorem is a generalisation of this result. However, our proof is quite different from the proof of the analogous result in group cohomology, which makes use of the fact that for a group
G
of type
FP_{n}
, the functor
H^{n}(G,-)
commutes with direct limits. This result has no immediate analogue for general metric spaces, so we appeal to a more hands-on proof.

Theorem 9.11
.

Assume
R
is a Noetherian ring. Suppose
X
is a coarsely homogeneous proper metric space of coarse finite type over
R
. Then
\ccd(X)=\max\{H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(X;R)\neq 0\}
.

Proof.

Let
n=\ccd_{R}(X)
and
m=\max\{H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(X;R)\neq 0\}
. It follows from Proposition
8.2
that
m\leq n
. For the converse, we assume for contradiction that
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(X;R)=0
.
Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a proper finite-height
n
-dimensional projective
R
-resolution over
X
. Since
\mathbf{C}_{n}
is
n
-dimensional, the assumption
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(X;R)=0
ensures the coboundary map
\mathbf{C}^{n-1}_{c}\xrightarrow{\delta}\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}
is surjective. By Proposition
9.10
, this coboundary map has
\Omega
-uniform preimages for some
\Omega
. We pick
\Psi
such that
\mathbf{C}_{n}
,
\mathbf{C}_{n-1}
and
\partial:\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\mathbf{C}_{n-1}
have displacement
\Psi
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{C}_{n}=(C_{n},B,\delta,p,\mathcal{F})
and
\mathbf{C}_{n-1}=(C_{n-1},C,\epsilon,q,\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
both have height
i_{0}
. By Lemma
4.13
,
\mathbf{C}_{n}
and
\mathbf{C}_{n-1}
have projective bases
\{m_{b}\}_{b\in B}
and
\{n_{c}\}_{c\in C}
respectively, of displacement
\Psi
and satisfying
m_{b}=n_{c}=0
for all
b\in B\setminus B(i_{0})
and
c\in C\setminus C(i_{0})
.
For each
b\in B
, we have
\delta_{b}\in\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}
by Remark
5.13
. Moreover, Proposition
5.20
ensures that
\supp_{\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}}(\delta_{b})\subseteq N_{\Psi(i_{0})}(p(b))
. Thus
\diam(\supp_{\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}}(\delta_{b}))\leq 2\Psi(i_{0})
. Setting
r\coloneqq\Omega(i_{0},2\Psi(i_{0}))+\Psi(i_{0})
, we see there exists
\mu_{b}\in\mathbf{C}^{n-1}_{c}
such that
\delta\mu_{b}=\delta_{b}
and
\supp_{\mathbf{C}^{n-1}_{c}}(\mu_{b})\subseteq N_{r}(p(b))
. Since
\delta_{b}=0
for
b\in B\setminus B(i_{0})
, we may assume
\mu_{b}=0
for all
b\in B\setminus B(i_{0})
.

We claim
\pi:\mathbf{C}_{n-1}\to\mathbf{C}_{n-1}
given by
\pi(\tau)=\sum_{b\in B}\mu_{b}(\tau)\partial m_{b}
for each
\tau\in\mathbf{C}_{n-1}
is a finite displacement retraction onto
\mathbf{N}\coloneqq\im(\mathbf{C}_{n}\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbf{C}_{n-1})
. By Proposition
8.2
, this claim would imply
\ccd_{R}(X)\leq n-1
, which is the desired contradiction.

First let
\tau\in\mathbf{C}_{n-1}
and suppose
\mu_{b}(\tau)\neq 0
for some
b\in B
. Then
b\in B(i_{0})
, and Proposition
5.20
implies that
d(\supp_{\mathbf{C}^{n-1}_{c}}(\mu_{b}),\supp_{\mathbf{C}_{n-1}}(\tau))\leq\Psi(i_{0})
. This implies
p(b)\in N_{\Psi(i_{0})+r}(\supp_{\mathbf{C}_{n-1}}(\tau))
. Since
\mathbf{C}_{n-1}
is proper, we see that for each
\tau\in\mathbf{C}_{n-1}
, there are only finitely many
b\in B
with
\mu_{b}(\tau)\neq 0
, and so
\pi
is well-defined. Moreover,

\displaystyle\supp_{\mathbf{C}_{n-1}}(\pi(\tau))

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in B\\
\mu_{b}(\tau)\neq 0\end{subarray}}\supp_{\mathbf{C}_{n-1}}(\partial m_{b})

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in B\\
\mu_{b}(\tau)\neq 0\end{subarray}}N_{2\Psi(i_{0})}(p(b))\subseteq N_{3\Psi(i_{0})+r}(\supp_{\mathbf{C}_{n-1}}(\tau)),

showing that as
\mathbf{C}_{n-1}
has finite height,
\pi
has finite displacement.
Finally, if
\tau=\partial\sigma
for some
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\pi(\tau)

\displaystyle=\pi(\partial\sigma)=\sum_{b\in B}\mu_{b}(\partial\sigma)\partial m_{b}=\partial\left(\sum_{b\in B}(\delta\mu_{b})(\sigma)m_{b}\right)

\displaystyle=\partial\left(\sum_{b\in B}\delta_{b}(\sigma)m_{b}\right)=\partial\sigma=\tau

showing that
\pi
is a retraction onto
\mathbf{N}
.
∎

Theorem
9.11
allows us to compare
\ccd
with asymptotic dimension
\asdim(X)
of a metric space. For background about asymptotic dimension, we refer the reader to
[
BD11
]
. It was shown by Dranishnikov that if
G
is a group of type
FP
, then
\vcd(G)\leq\asdim(G)

[
Dra09
, Proposition 5.10]
. We generalise this to metric spaces:

Proposition 9.12
.

Let
X
be a proper coarsely homogeneous metric space of coarse finite type over
\mathbb{Z}
. Then
\ccd(X)\leq\asdim(X)
.

Proof.

Let
n=\max\{k\mid HX^{k}(X;\mathbb{Z})\neq 0\}
, where
HX^{k}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is Roe’s coarse cohomology. Dranishnikov showed that
n\leq\asdim(X)

[
Dra09
, Propositions 5.1 & 5.6]
. By Theorem
9.11
and Proposition
7.5
,
n=\ccd_{\mathbb{Z}}(X)
.
∎

Specializing to the case of groups, we deduce:

Corollary 9.13
.

Let
G
be a group of type
FP_{\infty}
with
\ccd_{\mathbb{Z}}(G)<\infty
. Then
\ccd_{\mathbb{Z}}(G)\leq\asdim(G)
.

We can now strengthen Theorem
7.10
in the case that
X
is uniformly acyclic over
R
.

Theorem 9.14
.

Let
X
be a metric space, let
R
be a PID, and let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume
X
is coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic over
R
. Suppose
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a proper projective
R
-resolution over
X
.

(1)

Let
f:R\to S
be a ring homomorphism and let
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}\coloneqq\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\otimes_{R}S
, which is a proper projective
S
-resolution over
X
by Proposition
6.28
. For each
k<n
, we have a natural short exact sequence

0\to H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\otimes_{R}S\xrightarrow{\phi}H^{k}(\mathbf{D}_{c}^{\bullet})\to\Tor_{1}^{R}(H^{k+1}(\mathbf{C}_{c}^{\bullet}),S)\to 0

and an injection
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\otimes_{R}S\xrightarrow{\phi}H^{n}(\mathbf{D}_{c}^{\bullet})
.
Moreover, the injections
\phi
are given by
[\alpha]\otimes s\mapsto[\alpha\otimes s]
.

(2)

For each
k<n
, we have a natural short exact sequence

0\to\Ext^{1}_{R}(H^{k+1}(\mathbf{C}_{c}^{\bullet}),R)\to H_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf})\xrightarrow{\psi}\Hom_{R}(H^{k}(\mathbf{C}_{c}^{\bullet}),R)\to 0

and a surjection
H_{n}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf})\xrightarrow{\psi}\Hom_{R}(H^{n}(\mathbf{C}_{c}^{\bullet}),R)
. Moreover, the surjections
\psi
are given by
\psi([\phi])([\alpha])=\phi(\alpha)
.

This result should be thought of as a generalisation of the group-theoretic Universal Coefficient Theorems for groups of type
FP_{\infty}

[
Bie81
, §3]
. While there is significant overlap between Theorem
7.10
and Theorem
9.14
in light of Proposition
9.8
, neither one implies the other, and it is necessary to use both to obtain the strongest results in Section
12
.

Proof.

By Corollary
6.14
, we may assume without loss of generality that
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
satisfies the hypothesis of Lemma
7.9
.
We show that for each
k\leq n
, the inverse systems
\{H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(i))\}_{i}
are
stable
as defined in Definition
A.5
. After proving this, the result will follow from Lemma
7.9
and Theorem
A.12
.

By Proposition
9.3
, there exists a projective
R
-resolution
\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}
over
X
with
\mathbf{E}_{k}
of finite height for
k\leq n
. By Corollary
6.14
, there are chain maps
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}
and
g_{\#}:\mathbf{E}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
of displacement
\Phi
and chain homotopies
\id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{\#}}}{{\simeq}}g_{\#}f_{\#}
and
\id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\simeq}}f_{\#}g_{\#}
of displacement
\Phi
.

For
i\leq j
, we let
m_{\#}^{i,j}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i)\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(j)
be the inclusion map. Since
\mathbf{E}_{k}
has finite height for
k\leq n
, we can pick
i_{0}
such that
g_{\#}(\mathbf{E}_{k})\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{k}(i_{0})
for
k\leq n
, and set
j_{0}\coloneqq\Phi(i_{0})
. Let
f_{\#}^{i}
and
h_{\#}^{i}
be the restrictions of
f_{\#}
and
h_{\#}
to
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i)
. For arbitrary
i
, set
j\coloneqq\Phi(i)
, and consider the map

m_{\#}^{i,j},m_{\#}^{i_{0},j}g_{\#}f_{\#}^{i}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i)\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(j).

We observe that since
m_{\#}^{i_{0},j}g_{\#}f_{\#}^{i}-m_{\#}^{i,j}=\partial h_{\#}^{i}+h_{\#}^{i}\partial
, these two maps
induce identical maps

f^{*}_{i}\circ g^{*}\circ m^{*}_{i_{0},j}=m^{*}_{i,j}:H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(j))\to H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(i))

on cohomology.
We claim that
m^{*}_{i_{0},i}
restricts to an isomorphism

\im(m^{*}_{i,j})\cong\im(m^{*}_{i_{0},j_{0}}).

Since this holds for arbitrary large
i
, it follows that
\{H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(i))\}_{i}
is a stable inverse system.

To see this, first suppose
[\alpha]\in H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(j))
with
m^{*}_{i_{0},j}([\alpha])=0
. Then
m^{*}_{i,j}([\alpha])=f^{*}_{i}g^{*}m^{*}_{i_{0},j}([\alpha])=0
, showing that
m^{*}_{i_{0},i}
restricted to
{\im(m^{*}_{i,j})}
is injective.
Now suppose
[\beta]\in H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(j_{0}))
. Then

\displaystyle m^{*}_{i_{0},j_{0}}({[\beta]})=f^{*}_{i_{0}}g^{*}m^{*}_{i_{0},j_{0}}({[\beta]})=m^{*}_{i_{0},i}m^{*}_{i,j}f^{*}_{j}g^{*}m^{*}_{i_{0},j_{0}}({[\beta]}),

where last inequality follows because
f_{\#}^{j}m_{\#}^{i,j}m_{\#}^{i_{0},i}=f_{\#}^{i_{0}}
. Since
[\beta]\in H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}(j_{0}))
was arbitrary, this shows
m^{*}_{i_{0},i}
maps
{\im(m^{*}_{i,j})}
onto
\im(m^{*}_{i_{0},j_{0}})
as required.
∎

The
\mathcal{Z}
-boundary was developed by Bestvina for certain torsion-free groups
[
Bes96
]
, and later generalised by Dranishnikov
[
Dra06
]
. In this article, a
\mathcal{Z}
-boundary
will always refer to a
\mathcal{Z}
-boundary in the sense of Dranishnikov
[
Dra06
]
.
For a hyperbolic group
G
, its
\mathcal{Z}
-boundary is its Gromov boundary. For a
\CAT(0)
group
G
, its
\mathcal{Z}
-boundary is the visual boundary
\partial X
of a proper
\CAT(0)
space
X
admitting a proper cocompact
G
-action.

If
Z
is a topological space, let
\dim(X)
be the Lebesgue covering dimension of
X
, and let
\dim_{R}(X)
be the cohomological dimension of
X
with coefficients in
R
, i.e. the largest
n
such that there exists a closed subset
A\subseteq X
such that
\check{H}^{n}(X,A;R)\neq 0
, where
\check{H}
is Čech cohomology. A theorem of Alexandroff says if
X
is a compact metric space with
\dim(X)<\infty
, then
\dim(X)=\dim_{\mathbb{Z}}(X)

[
Dra09
, Theorem 1.4]
.

Proposition 9.15

(cf.
[
Mar24
, Proposition 3.9]
)
.

Suppose that
R
is a commutative ring and
G
is a group such that
\ccd_{R}(G)<\infty
. If
G
admits a
\mathcal{Z}
-boundary
Z
, then
\dim_{R}(Z)+1=\ccd_{R}(G)
. Moreover,
\dim(Z)+1=\ccd(G)
.

Proof.

Let
(\overline{X},Z)
be a
\mathcal{Z}
-structure on
G
in the sense of
[
Dra06
]
. In particular,
G
acts cocompactly and properly discontinuously on
X\coloneqq\overline{X}\setminus Z
. Since
X
is uniformly contractible
[
Dra06
, Proposition 1]
, Theorem
2.4
implies
G
is of type
FP_{\infty}
.
As
\overline{X}
is contractible, the long exact sequence in cohomology yields isomorphisms
H^{n+1}_{c}(X;R)\cong H^{n}(Z;R)
for all
n
, where
H^{*}_{c}
is cohomology with compact supports; we refer to
[
Bes96
, Proposition 1.5]
for details. Since
X
is uniformly contractible, we have
H^{n+1}_{c}(X;R)\cong HX^{n+1}(X;R)\cong H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n+1}(X;R)
by Proposition
7.5
and
[
Roe93
, Proposition 3.33]
. It follows that
H^{n+1}(G,RG)\cong H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n+1}(G;R)\cong H^{n}(Z;R)
as
R
-modules. Theorem
9.11
and the main result of
[
Dra06
]
imply that
\dim_{R}(Z)+1=\ccd_{R}(G)
for any PID
R
. Moreover, Moran showed that the topological dimension of
Z
is finite
[
Mor16
]
, so Alexandroff’s theorem implies that
\dim(Z)+1=\ccd(G)
.
∎

Corollary 9.16
.

Suppose that either:

(1)

G
is a hyperbolic group and
X
is a Cayley graph of
G
; or

(2)

G
is a
\CAT(0)
group acting geometrically on a proper
\CAT(0)
space
X
.

Then
\ccd(G)=\dim(\partial X)+1<\infty
.

Proof.

By Proposition
9.15
and the discussion preceding it, it is sufficient to show
\ccd(G)<\infty
, which we do in both cases by appealing to Proposition
8.6
. In the case
G
is hyperbolic, it acts geometrically on each Rips complex
P_{r}(G)
, which is contractible for
r
sufficiently large
[
BH99
, Theorem III.
\Gamma
.3.21]
. In the case
G
is
\CAT(0)
, we know it acts properly and cocompactly on some finite-dimensional contractible simplicial complex
[
BH99
, Remark III.
\Gamma
.3.27]
.
∎

10.
Spaces of coarse cohomological dimension one

In this section we prove Theorem
10.2
, characterising quasi-geodesic metric spaces
X
of coarse cohomological dimension one as unbounded quasi-trees.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring with 1. If
X
is a quasi-geodesic metric space, then
X
is coarsely uniformly
0
-acyclic over
R
.

Proof.

Suppose
X
is quasi-isometric to a geodesic metric space
Y
. Then for each
y\in Y
and
r\geq 0
,
N_{r}(y)
is path-connected, and so the Rips complex
P_{i}(N_{r}(y))
is connected for all
i
. This ensures the map
\widetilde{H}_{0}(P_{i}(N_{r}(x));R)\to\widetilde{H}_{0}(P_{i}(N_{r}(x));R)
induced by inclusion is zero, and so
Y
is coarsely uniformly
0
-acyclic over
R
. Since being coarsely uniformly
0
-acyclic over
R
is invariant under coarse equivalence,
X
is also coarsely uniformly
0
-acyclic over
R
.
∎

Theorem 10.2
.

Let
X
be a quasi-geodesic metric space. Then
\ccd_{R}(X)=1
if and only if
X
is quasi-isometric to an unbounded simplicial tree.

We think of a simplicial tree as a metric space by taking the induced path metric in which each edge has length 1.
We prove Theorem
10.2
by appealing to Manning’s bottleneck criterion:

Theorem 10.3

(
[
Man05
, Theorem 4.6]
)
.

Let
X
be a geodesic metric space. Then
X
is quasi-isometric to a simplicial tree if and only if there exists a constant
\Delta>0
such that the following condition holds. For all
x,y\in X
, there exists some
m\in X
with
d(x,m)=d(y,m)=\frac{1}{2}d(x,y)
such that every path from
x
to
y
intersects
B_{\Delta}(m)
.

In order to prove Theorem
10.2
, we require a few lemmas.
The following lemma relates 1-chains in the standard resolution to paths:

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
X
be a metric space and let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be the ordered standard
R
-resolution over
X
. If
x,y\in X
and
\partial\sigma=y-x
for some
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{1}(i)
, then there exists a sequence
\{x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subseteq\supp(\sigma)\cup\{x,y\}
with
x_{0}=x
,
x_{n}=y
and
d(x_{j},x_{j+1})\leq i
for all
j
.

Proof.

We note that
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{1}(i)
can be expressed as a finite sum
\sigma=\sum_{j=0}^{k}r_{j}[z_{j},y_{j}]
, where for each
j
, we have
r_{j}\in R\setminus\{0\}
,
z_{j},y_{j}\in X
and
d(z_{j},y_{j})\leq i
.
By Lemma
6.25
, the chain complex
\mathbf{C}_{1}(i)\to\mathbf{C}_{0}(i)\to 0
can be identified with the simplicial chain complex of the 1-skeleton of
P_{i}(X)
, the Rips complex of
X
with parameter
i
. A standard argument implies that
\sigma
must be a sum of a path in
P_{i}(X)
from
x
to
y
, and a linear combination of closed loops in
P_{i}(X)
. Since
\supp(\sigma)=\{z_{0},\dots,z_{k}\}
, this completes the proof.
∎

We also show that a path can be approximated by a 1-chain in the following sense:

Lemma 10.5
.

Let
X
be a metric space and
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
the ordered standard
R
-resolution over
X
. Suppose
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
(\lambda,\mu)
-uniformly
0
-acyclic over
R
and
p:[0,L]\to X
is a path from
x
to
y
. Then there exists a 1-chain
\omega\in\mathbf{C}_{1}(\lambda(0))
with
\partial\omega=y-x
and
\supp(\omega)\subseteq N_{\mu(0,1)}(\im(p))
.

Proof.

Since
p
is uniformly continuous, there is a constant
\delta>0
such that
d(p(s),p(t))\leq 1
whenever
|s-t|\leq\delta
. We thus pick a sequence
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{N}=L
with
t_{j}-t_{j-1}\leq\delta
for each
j
.
Setting
x_{j}=p(t_{j})
for each
j
, we have a sequence
x=x_{0},\dots,x_{N}=y
with
d(x_{j-1},x_{j})\leq 1
for all
j
.
Note that by Lemma
6.4
, each
x_{j}-x_{j-1}
is a reduced 0-cycle in
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(0,N_{1}(x_{j}))
. Thus there exists some
\omega_{j}\in\mathbf{C}_{1}(\lambda(0),N_{\mu(0,1)}(x_{j}))
with
\partial\omega_{j}=x_{j}-x_{j-1}
. Therefore
\supp(\omega_{j})\subseteq N_{\mu(0,1)}(x_{j})\subseteq N_{\mu(0,1)}(\im(p))
. Setting
\omega\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{N}\omega_{j}
, we see
\supp(\omega)\subseteq N_{\mu(0,1)}(\im(p))
and
\partial\omega=y-x
as required.
∎

Proof of Theorem
10.2
.

First suppose
X
is quasi-isometric to an unbounded simplicial tree
T
. It follows from Example
6.11
that the simplicial chain complex of
T
with coefficients in
R
can be endowed with the structure of projective
R
-resolution over
T
, hence
\ccd_{R}(T)\leq 1
. Since
T
is unbounded, Proposition
8.10
implies
\ccd_{R}(T)=1
. As
\ccd_{R}(-)
is invariant under quasi-isometries, it follows
\ccd_{R}(X)=1
.

Conversely, suppose
\ccd_{R}(X)=1
. Replacing
X
by geodesic metric space quasi-isometric to
X
if necessary, we can assume
X
is itself a geodesic metric space. Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be the standard
R
-resolution over
X
. By Proposition
9.3
and Lemma
10.1
, we can choose
(\lambda,\mu)
such that
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
(\lambda,\mu)
-uniformly
0
-acyclic over
R
. By Lemma
4.17
and Proposition
8.2
,
\im(\mathbf{C}_{2}\to\mathbf{C}_{1})\leq\mathbf{C}_{1}
has a complement
\mathbf{P}\leq\mathbf{C}_{1}
, and there is a finite displacement retraction
r_{\#}
from
\mathbf{C}_{1}
to
\mathbf{P}
. Let
\Phi
be the displacement of
r_{\#}
. Since
\mathbf{P}
is a complement of
\im(\mathbf{C}_{2}\to\mathbf{C}_{1})=\ker(\mathbf{C}_{1}\to\mathbf{C}_{0})
,
\partial|_{\mathbf{P}}:\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{C}_{0}
is injective and
\partial m=\partial r_{\#}m
for each
m\in\mathbf{C}_{1}
. Set
i_{0}\coloneqq\lambda(0)
,
i_{1}=\Phi(i_{0})
,
D\coloneqq\mu(0,1)
,
D_{1}\coloneqq\Phi(i_{0})+D
,
D_{2}\coloneqq 3D_{1}+i_{1}
and
\Delta\coloneqq D_{1}+D_{2}
.

Let
x,y\in X
and let
\gamma
be a geodesic from
x
to
y
with midpoint
m
. By Lemma
10.5
, there exists
\omega\in\mathbf{C}_{1}(i_{0})
with
\supp(\omega)\subseteq N_{D}(\im(\gamma))
and
\partial\omega=y-x
.
Therefore, we deduce that
r_{\#}(\omega)\in\mathbf{C}_{1}(i_{1})
, that
\partial r_{\#}\omega=\partial\omega=y-x
and that

\supp(r_{\#}\omega)\subseteq N_{\Phi(i_{0})}(\supp(\omega))\subseteq N_{D_{1}}(\im(\gamma))
. It follows from Lemma
10.4
that there exists a sequence
x=x_{0},\dots,x_{n}=y
in
\supp(r_{\#}\omega)\cup\{x,y\}\subseteq N_{D_{1}}(\im(\gamma))
with
d(x_{j-1},x_{j})\leq i_{1}
for all
j
.
Let
\pi:X\to\im(\gamma)
be a closest point projection, and set
y_{j}\coloneqq\pi(x_{j})
for each
j
. Then
d(y_{j-1},y_{j})\leq 2D_{1}+i_{1}
. Let
\gamma_{j}
be the geodesic segment
[y_{j-1},y_{j}]_{\gamma}
along
\gamma
. Since
\gamma
is a geodesic and the concatenation
\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{n}
is path from
x
to
y
contained in
