\im(\gamma)
, it follows
m\in\gamma_{j}
for some
j
. Thus

d(x_{j},m)\leq d(x_{j},y_{j})+d(y_{j},m)\leq d(x_{j},y_{j})+d(y_{j},y_{j-1})\leq 3D_{1}+i_{1}=D_{2},

and so
m\in N_{D_{2}}(\supp(r_{\#}\omega)\cup\{x,y\})
.

Now let
p
be any path from
x
to
y
. By Lemma
10.5
, there exists
\tau\in\mathbf{C}_{1}(i_{0})
with
\supp(\tau)\subseteq N_{D}(\im(p))
and
\partial\tau=y-x
. Thus
\supp(r_{\#}\tau)\cup\{x,y\}\subseteq N_{D_{1}}(\im(p))
. However, since
\partial r_{\#}\tau=\partial r_{\#}\omega=y-x
and
\partial|_{\mathbf{P}}
is injective, we deduce
r_{\#}\tau=r_{\#}\omega
. Therefore,

m\in N_{D_{2}}(\supp(r_{\#}\omega)\cup\{x,y\})\subseteq N_{D_{2}}(\supp(r_{\#}\tau)\cup\{x,y\})\subseteq N_{\Delta}(\im(p)).

In other words, every path
p
from
x
to
y
must intersect the ball
B_{\Delta}(m)
. By Theorem
10.3
, this condition guarantees that
X
is quasi-isometric to a simplicial tree.
∎

11.
Cap and cup products

In this section, we develop a notion of cup and cap product for proper metric spaces, whose properties are described in Lemma
11.5
and Proposition
11.8
. In applications, both in Section
12
and in upcoming work, the utility of the theory lies not in simply looking at the induced maps on the level of homology/cohomology, but on the precise quantitative bounds on supports on the chain/cochain level.

Throughout this section, we fix a metric space
X
and a proper projective
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X
. We equip
X\times X
with the
\ell_{1}
-metric. We define the
diagonal embedding

\gamma:X\to X\times X
by
\gamma(x)=(x,x)
, so that
d_{X\times X}(\gamma(x),\gamma(x^{\prime}))=2d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})
.

Definition 11.1
.

A
diagonal approximation
of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a chain map
\Delta:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
that is augmentation-preserving and of finite displacement over the diagonal embedding
\gamma
.

Proposition
6.12
implies that:

Proposition 11.2
.

The diagonal approximation exists and is unique up to a finite displacement chain homotopy equivalence.

We now fix a diagonal approximation
\Delta
and pick
\Phi
large enough such that
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
have displacement
\Phi
, and
\Delta
has displacement
\Phi
over
\gamma
.
We suppose
\mathbf{C}_{k}=(C_{k},B_{k},\delta^{k},p_{k},\{B_{k}(i)\})
and each
\mathbf{C}_{k}
is equipped with a projective basis
\{m_{b}^{k}\}_{b\in B_{k}}
satisfying the conclusion of Lemma
4.13
when
\mathbf{C}_{k}
has finite height.

Definition 11.3
.

Given
\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{j}
and
\beta\in\mathbf{C}^{k}
, we define the
cup product

\alpha\smile\beta\in\mathbf{C}^{j+k}
by

(\alpha\smile\beta)(\sigma)=(\alpha\otimes\beta)\Delta(\sigma)

for each
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{j+k}
.
If
\tau\in\mathbf{C}_{n}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{j}
with
j\leq n
, we define the
cap product

\tau\frown\alpha\in\mathbf{C}_{n-j}
by
(\alpha\otimes 1)\Delta\tau
.

Remark 11.4
.

For explicitness, we note that if
\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{j}
,
\beta\in\mathbf{C}^{k}
and
\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2}\in\mathbf{C}_{l}\otimes\mathbf{C}_{j+k-l}
, then
(\alpha\otimes\beta)(\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})
is equal to
\alpha(\sigma_{1})\beta(\sigma_{2})
if
l=j
and is equal to zero otherwise. Similarly,
(\alpha\otimes 1)(\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})
is equal to
\alpha(\sigma_{1})\sigma_{2}
if
l=j
and is equal to zero otherwise.

The following properties of cup and cap products will be used extensively:

Lemma 11.5
.

There exists an increasing function
\Psi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
, depending only on
\Phi
, such that for all
\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{j}
,
\beta\in\mathbf{C}^{k}
and
\tau\in\mathbf{C}_{n}
with
n\geq j
, the following hold:

(1)

\delta(\alpha\smile\beta)=(-1)^{k}\delta\alpha\smile\beta+\alpha\smile\delta\beta
.

(2)

\partial(\tau\frown\alpha)=\tau\frown\delta\alpha+(-1)^{j}\partial\tau\frown\alpha
.

(3)

For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\supp(\alpha\smile\beta,i)\subseteq N_{\Psi(i)}(\supp(\alpha,\Psi(i)))\cap N_{\Psi(i)}(\supp(\beta,\Psi(i))).

(4)

If
\tau\in\mathbf{C}_{n}(i)
, then
\tau\frown\alpha\in\mathbf{C}_{n-j}(\Psi(i))
and

\supp(\tau\frown\alpha)\subseteq N_{\Psi(i)}(\supp(\tau))\cap N_{\Psi(i)}(\supp(\alpha,\Psi(i))).

(5)

If
j=n
,
\partial\tau=0
and
\delta\alpha=0
, then
\epsilon(\tau\frown\alpha)=\alpha(\tau)
.

Remark 11.6
.

The sign conventions adopted mean the formulae (
1
) and (
2
) are somewhat non-standard. They were chosen specifically so that in the case where
\partial\tau=0
, the map
g_{\#}:\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
defined by
\alpha\mapsto\tau\frown\alpha
is a chain map. This fact will be used extensively in Section
12
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}=\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
.
For
\sigma\in\mathbf{D}_{n}
, let
\sigma_{j}
denote the component of
\sigma
contained in
\mathbf{C}_{j}\otimes\mathbf{C}_{n-j}
. For each
i,s\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
i_{s}\coloneqq\Phi^{s}(i)
.

(
1
): Let
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{j+k+1}
. Then

\displaystyle\delta(\alpha\smile\beta)(\sigma)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{j+k+1}(\alpha\smile\beta)(\partial\sigma)=(-1)^{j+k+1}(\alpha\otimes\beta)\Delta(\partial\sigma)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{j+k+1}(\alpha\otimes\beta)((\partial\otimes 1)(\Delta\sigma)_{j+1}+(-1)^{j}(1\otimes\partial)(\Delta\sigma)_{j})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}(\delta\alpha\otimes\beta)(\Delta\sigma)_{j+1}+(\alpha\otimes\delta\beta)(\Delta\sigma)_{j}

\displaystyle=\left((-1)^{k}\delta\alpha\otimes\beta+\alpha\otimes\delta\beta\right)(\Delta\sigma)

\displaystyle=\left((-1)^{k}\delta\alpha\smile\beta+\alpha\smile\delta\beta\right)(\sigma)

as required.

(
2
):

\displaystyle\partial\tau\frown\alpha

\displaystyle=(\alpha\otimes 1)\Delta\partial\tau=(\alpha\otimes 1)\left((\partial\otimes 1)(\Delta\tau)_{j+1}+(-1)^{j}(1\otimes\partial)(\Delta\tau)_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{j+1}(\delta\alpha\otimes 1)(\Delta\tau)+(-1)^{j}\partial(\alpha\otimes 1)(\Delta\tau)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{j}\left(-\tau\frown\delta\alpha+\partial(\tau\frown\alpha)\right)

so that
\partial(\tau\frown\alpha)=\tau\frown\delta\alpha+(-1)^{j}\partial\tau\frown\alpha
.

(
3
):
Suppose
x\in\supp(\alpha\smile\beta,i)
. Then there is some
b\in p_{j+k}^{-1}(x)\cap B_{j+k}(i)
with
(\alpha\smile\beta)(m^{j+k}_{b})=(\alpha\otimes\beta)(\Delta(m^{j+k}_{b}))\neq 0
. Thus
(\alpha\otimes\beta)(\Delta(m^{j+k}_{b})_{j})\neq 0
.
Since
m^{j+k}_{b}\in\mathbf{C}(i_{1})
, we have
\Delta(m^{j+k}_{b})\in\mathbf{D}_{j+k}(i_{2})
and so Proposition
4.15
implies

\displaystyle\Delta(m^{j+k}_{b})_{j}=\sum_{(b_{1},b_{2})\in B_{j}(i_{2})\times B_{k}(i_{2})}(\delta^{j}_{b_{1}}\otimes\delta^{k}_{b_{2}})(\Delta(m^{j+k}_{b}))m^{j}_{b_{1}}\otimes m^{k}_{b_{2}}.

As
(\alpha\otimes\beta)(\Delta(m^{j+k}_{b})_{j})\neq 0
, there exist
b_{1}\in B_{j}(i_{2})
and
b_{2}\in B_{k}(i_{2})
such that:

•

(\delta_{b_{1}}\otimes\delta_{b_{2}})(\Delta(m^{j+k}_{b})_{j})\neq 0
;

•

(\alpha\otimes\beta)(m^{j}_{b_{1}}\otimes m^{k}_{b_{2}})=\alpha(m^{j}_{b_{1}})\beta(m^{k}_{b_{2}})\neq 0
.

Setting
x_{1}\coloneqq p_{j}(b_{1})
and
x_{2}\coloneqq p_{k}(b_{2})
, this implies
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\supp(\Delta(m^{j+k}_{b}))
,
x_{1}\in\supp(\alpha,i_{2})
and
x_{2}\in\supp(\beta,i_{2})
.
As
\Delta
has finite displacement over
\gamma
,

\displaystyle\supp(\Delta(m^{j+k}_{b}))\subseteq N_{i_{2}}(\gamma(\supp(m^{j+k}_{b})))\subseteq N_{i_{2}}(\gamma(N_{i_{1}}(x)))\subseteq N_{i^{\prime}}(x,x),

where
i^{\prime}\coloneq i_{2}+2i_{1}
. This implies
x_{1},x_{2}\in N_{i^{\prime}}(x)
, and so

\supp(\alpha\smile\beta,i)\subseteq N_{i^{\prime}}(\supp(\alpha,i_{2}))\cap N_{i^{\prime}}(\supp(\beta,i_{2})).

(
4
):
Suppose
\tau\in\mathbf{C}_{n}(i)
. Since
\Delta
has displacement
\Phi
, we can apply Proposition
4.15
to see that
\Delta(\tau)\in\mathbf{D}_{n}(i_{1})\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i_{2})\otimes\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i_{2})
. This implies

\tau\frown\alpha=(\alpha\otimes 1)(\Delta(\tau))\in(\alpha\otimes 1)\left(\mathbf{C}_{j}(i_{2})\otimes\mathbf{C}_{n-j}(i_{2})\right)\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{n-j}(i_{2})

as required.

Now suppose
x\in\supp(\tau\frown\alpha)=\supp((\alpha\otimes 1)\Delta\tau)
.
Since
\tau\in\mathbf{C}_{n}(i)
, we deduce
\tau=\sum_{b\in B_{n}(i)}\delta^{n}_{b}(\tau)m^{n}_{b}
. Thus
x\in\supp((\alpha\otimes 1)\Delta m^{n}_{b})
for some
b\in B_{n}(i)
with
\delta^{n}_{b}(\tau)\neq 0
.
Since
m^{n}_{b}\in\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}(i_{1})
, we see
\Delta(m^{n}_{b})\in\mathbf{D}_{n}(i_{2})
and so

\displaystyle\Delta(m^{n}_{b})_{j}=\sum_{(b_{1},b_{2})\in B_{j}(i_{2})\times B_{n-j}(i_{2})}(\delta^{j}_{b_{1}}\otimes\delta^{n-j}_{b_{2}})(\Delta(m^{n}_{b}))m^{j}_{b_{1}}\otimes m^{n-j}_{b_{2}}.

Since
x\in\supp((\alpha\otimes 1)\Delta(m^{n}_{b}))
, there exist
b_{1}\in B_{j}(i_{2})
and
b_{2}\in B_{n-j}(i_{2})
such that:

•

(\delta^{j}_{b_{1}}\otimes\delta^{n-j}_{b_{2}})(\Delta(m^{n}_{b}))\neq 0
,

•

x\in\supp((\alpha\otimes 1)(m^{j}_{b_{1}}\otimes m^{n-j}_{b_{2}}))=\supp(\alpha(m^{j}_{b_{1}})m^{n-j}_{b_{2}})
. In particular,
\alpha(m^{j}_{b_{1}})\neq 0
and
x\in\supp(m^{n-j}_{b_{2}})
.

We now set
x_{0}=p_{n}(b)
,
x_{1}=p_{j}(b_{1})
and
x_{2}=p_{n-j}(b_{2})
, observing that
x_{0}\in\supp(\tau)
, that
x_{1}\in\supp(\alpha,i_{2})
, and that
x\in\supp(m_{b_{2}}^{n-j})\subseteq N_{i_{3}}(x_{2})
. Moreover,

\displaystyle(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\in\supp(\Delta(m_{b}^{n}))\subseteq N_{i_{2}}(\gamma(\supp(m_{b}^{n})))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{i_{2}}(\gamma(N_{i_{1}}(x_{0})))\subseteq N_{i^{\prime}}(x_{0},x_{0}),

where
i^{\prime}\coloneqq i_{2}+2i_{1}
. Thus
x_{1},x_{2}\in N_{i^{\prime}}(x_{0})
. We conclude that

\displaystyle x\in N_{i_{3}+i^{\prime}}(x_{0})

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{i_{3}+i^{\prime}}(x_{0})\cap N_{i_{3}+2i^{\prime}}(x_{1})

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{i_{3}+i^{\prime}}(\supp(\tau))\cap N_{i_{3}+2i^{\prime}}(\supp(\alpha,i_{2}))

as required.

(
5
): Since
\Delta
is augmentation-preserving,
\epsilon=(\epsilon\otimes\epsilon)\circ\Delta=\epsilon\circ(1\otimes\epsilon)\circ\Delta
on
\mathbf{C}_{0}
. Thus
f_{\#}\coloneqq(1\otimes\epsilon)\circ\Delta:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is an augmentation-preserving chain map of finite displacement over the identity, hence there is a finite displacement chain homotopy
\id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{\#}}}{{\simeq}}f_{\#}
.
Thus

\displaystyle\epsilon(\tau\frown\alpha)

\displaystyle=\epsilon((\alpha\otimes 1)\Delta\tau)=\alpha((1\otimes\epsilon)\Delta\tau)=\alpha(f_{\#}\tau)

\displaystyle=\alpha(\tau+\partial h_{\#}\tau+h_{\#}\partial\tau)=\alpha(\tau),

where the last equality holds because
\delta\alpha=0
and
\partial\tau=0
.∎

Proposition
11.8
implies:

Corollary 11.7
.

The cup and cap product in Proposition
11.8
descend to a cup product

\smile:H^{j}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet})\times H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{[\alpha]\times[\beta]\to[\alpha\smile\beta]}H^{j+k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet})

and a cap product

\frown:H_{n}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet})\times H^{j}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{[\tau]\times[\alpha]\to[\tau\frown\alpha]}H_{n-j}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}).

We recall from Proposition
5.17
that we identify
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
as a submodule of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf}
via the double dual map. We thus show that the cap products extends as follows:

Proposition 11.8
.

The cap product
\mathbf{C}_{n}\times\mathbf{C}^{j}\to\mathbf{C}_{n-j}
extends linearly to a cap product

\mathbf{C}_{n}^{\lf}\times\mathbf{C}^{j}\to\mathbf{C}_{n-j}^{\lf}

such the following holds. There exists a function
\Psi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
, depending only on
\Phi
, such that for all
\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{j}
and
\tau\in\mathbf{C}_{n}^{\lf}
with
n\geq j
, the following hold:

(1)

\partial(\tau\frown\alpha)=\tau\frown\delta\alpha+(-1)^{j}\partial\tau\frown\alpha
.

(2)

If
\tau\in\mathbf{C}_{n}^{\lf}(i)
, then
\tau\frown\alpha\in\mathbf{C}_{n-j}^{\lf}(\Psi(i))
and

\supp(\tau\frown\alpha)\subseteq N_{\Psi(i)}(\supp(\tau))\cap N_{\Psi(i)}(\supp(\alpha,\Psi(i))).

(3)

If
\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{j}_{c}
, then
\tau\frown\alpha\in\mathbf{C}_{n-j}
.

(4)

Suppose that
j=n
, that
\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}
, that
\partial\tau=0
and that
\delta\alpha=0
. Then
\epsilon(\tau\frown\alpha)=\alpha(\tau)
.

Proof.

Pick
\Psi
such that Lemma
11.5
hold. If
\tau\in\mathbf{C}_{n}^{\lf}
, then
\tau(\Ann(\mathbf{C}_{n}(i)))=0
for some
i
. Then Proposition
5.17
ensures
\tau
is given by the formal sum
\tau=\sum_{b\in B_{n}(i)}\delta^{n}_{b}(\tau)m^{n}_{b}
, interpreted as in Notation
5.14
.
We define

\tau\frown\alpha\coloneqq\sum_{b\in B_{n}(i)}\delta^{n}_{b}(\tau)\left(m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha\right).

In order to show this is a well-defined element of
\mathbf{D}_{n-j}^{\lf}
, we need to show the following.

(i)

For each
\beta\in\mathbf{C}^{n-j}_{c}
, there are only finitely
b\in B_{n}(i)
such that
\delta^{n}_{b}(\tau)\beta(m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha)\neq 0
. Therefore,
\tau\frown\alpha
defines an element of
\Hom_{R}(\mathbf{C}^{n-j}_{c},R)
given by

\beta\mapsto\sum_{b\in B_{n}(i)}\delta^{n}_{b}(\tau)\beta(m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha).

(ii)

There is some
i_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such
(\tau\frown\alpha)(\Ann(\mathbf{C}_{n-j}(i_{1})))=0
.

For
(i)
, we note that if
b\in B_{n}(i)
, then
m^{n}_{b}\in\mathbf{C}_{n}(\Phi(i))
, so that by Lemma
11.5
we know that
m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha\in\mathbf{C}_{n-j}(i_{1})
and that
\supp(m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha)\subseteq N_{i_{1}}(p_{n}(b))
, where
i_{1}\coloneqq\Psi(\Phi(i))+\Phi(i)
. We now fix
\beta\in\mathbf{C}^{n-j}_{c}
. We see that if
\beta(m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha)\neq 0
, then by Lemma
5.10
,
\emptyset\neq\supp(\beta,i_{1})\cap\supp(m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha)
, and so
p_{n}(b)\in N_{i_{1}}(\supp(\beta,i_{1}))
. Since
\mathbf{C}_{n}
is proper and
\supp(\beta,i_{1})
is finite by Proposition
5.9
, we conclude that there are only finitely many
b\in B_{n}(i)
with
\beta(m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha)\neq 0
, proving
(i)
.

For
(ii)
, we note that if
\beta\in\Ann(\mathbf{C}_{n-j}(i_{1}))
with
i_{1}
as above, then for each
b\in B_{n}(i)
, we have
m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha\in\mathbf{C}_{n-j}(i_{1})
, and so
\beta(m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha)=0
. This implies
(\tau\frown\alpha)(\Ann(\mathbf{C}_{n-j}(i_{1})))=0
as required.

Property (
1
) now follows by linearity and Lemma
11.5
. For (
2
), we first note that since
(\tau\frown\alpha)(\Ann(\mathbf{C}_{n-j}(i_{1})))=0
, we have
\tau\frown\alpha\in\mathbf{C}_{n-j}^{\lf}(\Phi(i_{1}))
by Proposition
5.17
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\supp(\tau\frown\alpha)

\displaystyle=\left\{p_{n-j}(b^{\prime})\,\middle|\,b^{\prime}\in B_{n-j},\sum_{b\in B_{n}(i)}\delta^{n}_{b}(\tau)\delta_{b^{\prime}}^{n-j}\left(m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha\right)\neq 0\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in B_{n}(i)\\
p_{n}(b)\in\supp(\tau)\end{subarray}}\supp(m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha)

Since
\supp(m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha)\subseteq N_{i_{1}}(p_{n}(b))\cap N_{i_{1}}(\supp(\alpha,i_{1}))
by Lemma
11.5
and the choice of
i_{1}
, it follows that

\supp(\alpha\frown\tau)\subseteq N_{i_{1}}(\supp(\tau))\cap N_{i_{1}}(\supp(\alpha,i_{1}))

as required for (
2
).

If
\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{j}_{c}
, then Proposition
5.9
implies
\supp(\alpha,i_{1})
is finite, and s Lemma
11.5
implies that all but finitely many summands of
\tau\frown\alpha=\sum_{b\in B_{n}(i)}\delta^{n}_{b}(\tau)\left(m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha\right)
are zero. This ensures that
\tau\frown\alpha\in\mathbf{C}_{n-j}
as required for (
3
).

It remains to prove (
4
). Let
f_{\#}
and
h_{\#}
be as in the proof of (
5
) of Lemma
11.5
. In particular, we have
\alpha(f_{\#}(\sigma))=\epsilon(\sigma\frown\alpha)
for all
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{n}
, and
\partial h_{\#}+h_{\#}\partial=f_{\#}-\id
. Applying Lemma
5.18
, we see that
f_{\#}
can be extended to a chain map
f_{\#}^{\lf}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf}
, and
h_{\#}
extends to a chain homotopy
h_{\#}^{\lf}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet+1}^{\lf}
such that
\partial h_{\#}^{\lf}+h_{\#}^{\lf}\partial=f_{\#}^{\lf}-\id
.
Since
\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}
and
\mathbf{C}_{n}
is proper, it follows that there are only finitely many
b\in B_{n}(i)
with
m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha\neq 0
. Thus

\displaystyle\epsilon(\tau\frown\alpha)

\displaystyle=\epsilon\left(\sum_{b\in B_{n}(i)}\delta^{n}_{b}(\tau)\left(m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha\right)\right)=\sum_{b\in B_{n}(i)}\delta^{n}_{b}(\tau)\epsilon\left(m^{n}_{b}\frown\alpha\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in B_{n}(i)}\delta^{n}_{b}(\tau)\alpha(f_{\#}(m^{n}_{b}))=\alpha\left(f_{\#}^{\lf}\left(\sum_{b\in B}\delta^{n}_{b}(\tau)m^{n}_{b}\right)\right)=\alpha(f_{\#}^{\lf}(\tau))

\displaystyle=\alpha(\tau+\partial h_{\#}^{\lf}\tau+h_{\#}^{\lf}\partial\tau)=\alpha(\tau)

since
\partial\tau=0
and
\delta\alpha=0
. This proves (
4
).
∎

12.
Coarse Poincaré duality spaces

In this section, we recall Kapovich–Kleiner’s notion of coarse Poincaré duality spaces
[
KK05
]
. We give several characterisations of these spaces, and prove Theorems
A
and
B
from the introduction.

We recall from Proposition
5.20
that if
\mathbf{M}
is a proper projective
R
-module over
X
of finite height, then so is
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{c}
. Thus if
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a finite height
R
-chain complex over
X
, the notion of a chain map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
being of finite displacement makes sense. We thus define a coarse Poincaré duality group as follows:

Definition 12.1
.

Given a natural number
n\geq 1
and a commutative ring
R
, a proper metric space
X
is a
coarse
R
-Poincaré duality space of dimension
n
, or coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
space for short, if there exists a proper finite height projective
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X
such that the following holds. There are finite displacement chain maps
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
and
\overline{f}_{\#}:\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and finite displacement chain homotopies
\overline{f}_{\#}\circ f_{\#}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{\#}}}{{\simeq}}\id
and
f_{\#}\circ\overline{f}_{\#}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{h}_{\#}}}{{\simeq}}\id
. We call any such
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
a
PD_{n}^{R}
-complex over
X
.

Lemma 12.2
.

Let
X
be a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
space and let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
-complex over
X
. Suppose
X
is coarsely equivalent to
Y
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
is any proper finite height projective
R
-resolution over
Y
. Then
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
-complex over
Y
, and so
Y
is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
space.
In particular, setting
X=Y
, we observe that any proper finite height projective
R
-resolution over
X
is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
complex.

Proof.

This follows easily from Proposition
6.12
, using an argument similar to
[
Mar18
, Proposition 4.3]
.
∎

Example 12.3
.

We compare the notion of Poincaré duality spaces in Definition
12.1
with the definitions considered by Kapovich–Kleiner
[
KK05
]
:

•

Recall that a metric simplicial complex is a pair
(X^{(1)},X)
, where
X
is a simplicial complex and
X^{(1)}
is its 1-skeleton endowed with the induced path metric
[
KK05
]
. In Definition 6.1 of
[
KK05
]
, Kapovich–Kleiner define a Poincaré duality space to be a uniformly acyclic metric simplicial complex
(X^{(1)},X)
whose simplicial chain complex
C_{\bullet}(X)
satisfies properties analogous to those defined in Definition
12.1
. By Example
6.11
, the simplicial chain complex
C_{\bullet}(X)
can be given the structure of a projective
\mathbb{Z}
-resolution over
X^{(1)}
, which is hence a coarse
PD_{n}^{\mathbb{Z}}
complex. Thus, for any coarse Poincaré duality space
(X^{(1)},X)
of dimension
n
in the sense of
[
KK05
, Definition 6.1]
, the underlying metric space
X^{(1)}
is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
space in the sense of Definition
12.1
.

•

More generally, in
\lx@sectionsign 11
of
[
KK05
]
, Kapovich–Kleiner define
metric complexes

(X,\mathbf{C}_{\bullet})
, where
X
is a metric space and
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a chain complex. Up to some freedom in the choice of filtrations, it is straightforward to check that there is a correspondence between proper finite-height free
\mathbb{Z}
-resolutions over
X
, and the chain complex
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
of a finite-type uniformly acyclic metric complex in the sense of
\lx@sectionsign 11
of
[
KK05
]
.
In
\lx@sectionsign 11
of
[
KK05
]
, Kapovich–Kleiner define a
Poincaré duality space

to be a metric complex
(X,\mathbf{C}_{\bullet})
,
where
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
satisfies properties analogous to those defined in Definition
12.1
. In particular, it is easy to deduce
X
is a coarse
PD_{n}^{\mathbb{Z}}
space in the sense of Definition
12.1
if and only if there is a metric complex
(X,\mathbf{C}_{\bullet})
that is a coarse Poincaré duality space of dimension
n
in the sense of
[
KK05
,
\lx@sectionsign 11
]
.

We describe some immediate properties of coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
spaces:

Proposition 12.4
.

If
X
is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
space, the following hold:

(1)

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;R)\cong\begin{cases}R&\textrm{if}\;k=n,\\
0&\textrm{if}\;k\neq n.\end{cases}

(2)

X
has coarse finite type over
R
and
\ccd_{R}(X)=n
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
complex and pick
f_{\#}
,
\overline{f}_{\#}
,
h_{\#}
and
\overline{h}_{\#}
as in Definition
12.1
.

(
1
): Since
\overline{f}_{\#}:\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a chain homotopy equivalence and

H_{0}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet})\cong\begin{cases}R&\textrm{if}\;k=0,\\
0&\textrm{if}\;k\neq 0,\end{cases}

the result follows.

(
2
): The fact that
X
is coarsely uniformly acyclic over
R
follows from Proposition
9.3
and the fact that
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a finite-height projective
R
-resolution over
X
. Applying Lemma
12.9
below to the maps
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
,
g_{\#}=\overline{f}_{\#}:\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{\#}}}{{\simeq}}g_{\#}f_{\#}
, we conclude that
\ccd_{R}(X)\leq n
. Since
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(X;R)=R\neq 0
, Proposition
8.2
implies
\ccd_{R}(X)=n
. By Proposition
9.6
,
X
has coarse finite type over
R
.
∎

Definition 12.5
.

A countable group
G
is a
coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
group
if, when equipped with a proper left-invariant metric, it is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
space.

We recall the following class of groups introduced by Bieri–Eckmann:

Definition 12.6

(
[
BE73
,
BE74
]
)
.

A group
G
is a
Poincaré duality group of dimension
n
over the ring
R
, or a
PD_{n}^{R}
group for short, if it is of type
FP_{\infty}^{R}
,
\cd_{R}(G)<\infty
and

H^{k}(G;RG)\cong\begin{cases}R&\textrm{if}\;k=n,\\
0&\textrm{if}\;k\neq n.\end{cases}

We now state some useful properties of coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
groups and their relation to
PD_{n}^{R}
groups.

Proposition 12.7
.

Let
G
be coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
group. Then the following hold:

(1)

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

H^{k}(G;RG)\cong\begin{cases}R&\textrm{if}\;k=n,\\
0&\textrm{if}\;k\neq n.\end{cases}

(2)

G
is of type
FP_{\infty}^{R}
and
\ccd_{R}(G)=n
.

(3)

Every
PD_{n}^{R}
group is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
group.

(4)

G
is a Poincaré duality group of dimension
n
over the ring
R
if and only if
\cd_{R}(G)<\infty
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a proper projective
R
-resolution over
G
. We deduce (
1
) and (
2
) from Proposition
12.4
, Corollary
8.9
and Theorem
2.4
. Kapovich–Kleiner showed (
3
)
[
KK05
, Example 11.6]
, whilst (
4
) follows readily from (
1
), (
2
) and Definition
12.6
.
∎

Remark 12.8
.

In
[
KK05
, Example 11.6]
, Kapovich–Kleiner further clarify the relationship between coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
groups and
PD_{n}^{R}
groups. They observe that if
G
is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
group, then
G
is a
PD_{n}^{R}
group precisely when there exists a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
complex
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
as in Definition
12.1
, admitting a free
G
-action, such that the maps
f_{\#}
,
g_{\#}
,
h_{\#}
and
\overline{h}_{\#}
are all
G
-equivariant.

Lemma 12.9
.

Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a projective
R
-resolution over a metric space
X
. Suppose that for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a non-negative cochain complex
D^{\bullet}
and chain maps
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to D^{n-\bullet}
and
g_{\#}:D^{n-\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
such that the composition
g_{\#}f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is augmentation-preserving and of finite displacement over
\id_{X}
. Then
\ccd_{R}(X)\leq n
.

In Lemma
12.9
,
D^{\bullet}
is only assumed to be a complex of
R
-modules, not
R
-modules over
X
. Thus
f_{\#}
and
g_{\#}
are only required to be chain maps, not finite displacement chain maps.

Proof.

We first assume
n>0
. Since
g_{\#}f_{\#}
has finite displacement and is augmentation-preserving, Proposition
6.12
implies there is a finite displacement chain homotopy
\id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{\#}}}{{\simeq}}g_{\#}f_{\#}
.
Let
r\coloneqq g_{n}f_{n}+\partial h_{n}:\mathbf{C}_{n}\to\mathbf{C}_{n}
, which has finite displacement. For each
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{n}
, we have

\partial r(\sigma)=\partial g_{n}f_{n}\sigma+\partial^{2}h_{n}\sigma=g_{n}\delta f_{n}\sigma=0,

where the last equality follows because
f_{n}\sigma\in D^{0}
and
\delta:D^{0}\to D^{-1}=0
is the zero map. Thus
\im(r)\subseteq\ker(\mathbf{C}_{n}\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbf{C}_{n-1})=\im(\mathbf{C}_{n+1}\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbf{C}_{n})
.
Moreover, if
\sigma\in\im(\mathbf{C}_{n+1}\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbf{C}_{n})
, then

\sigma=g_{\#}f_{\#}\sigma+h_{\#}\partial\sigma+\partial h_{\#}\sigma=r(\sigma).

We thus see that
r
is a finite displacement retraction onto
\im(\mathbf{C}_{n+1}\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbf{C}_{n})
. Proposition
8.2
now implies
\ccd_{R}(X)\leq n
.

In the case
n=0
, we suppose
g_{\#}f_{\#}
has displacement
\Phi
. We know from weak uniform acyclicity of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
that there is a constant
\Omega_{0}
such that for each
x\in X
, there is some
m_{x}\in\mathbf{C}_{0}(\Omega_{0})
with
\supp(m_{x})\subseteq N_{\Omega_{0}}(x)
and
\epsilon(m_{x})=1
. Therefore
\supp(g_{\#}f_{\#}m_{x})\subseteq N_{\Phi(\Omega_{0})+\Omega_{0}}(x)
for all
x\in X
. Moreover,
\supp(g_{\#}f_{\#}m_{x})
is non-empty since
\epsilon(g_{\#}f_{\#}m_{x})=\epsilon(m_{x})=1
and so
g_{\#}f_{\#}m_{x}\neq 0
. For each
x,y\in X
, we have
m_{x}-m_{y}=\partial\omega
for some
\omega\in\mathbf{C}_{1}
, and so
f_{\#}(\omega)\in D^{-1}=0
. Therefore
f_{\#}(m_{x}-m_{y})=\delta f_{\#}(\omega)=0
, and so
f_{\#}(m_{x})=f_{\#}(m_{y})
for all
x,y\in X
. This implies

\emptyset\neq\supp(g_{\#}f_{\#}m_{x})=\supp(g_{\#}f_{\#}m_{y})\subseteq N_{\Phi(\Omega_{0})+\Omega_{0}}(x)\cap N_{\Phi(\Omega_{0})+\Omega_{0}}(y).

Thus
d(x,y)\leq 2\Phi(\Omega_{0})+2\Omega_{0}
for all
x,y\in X
, and so
X
is bounded. Therefore,
\ccd_{R}(X)=0
by Proposition
8.10
.
∎

In the remainder of this section, we give several conditions that imply a space is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
space. These all rely on the following criterion. It is important to note that in the following lemma, we do not assume
X
is uniformly acyclic.

Lemma 12.10
.

Let
R
be a Noetherian ring, let
X
be a coarsely homogeneous proper metric space and let
(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet},\epsilon)
be a proper projective
R
-resolution over
X
. Let
n\geq 1
and suppose
H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})=0
for
k<n
and there exists
[\alpha]\in H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
and a chain map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
satisfying the following:

(1)

For each
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{0}
,
[f_{\#}(\sigma)]=\epsilon(\sigma)[\alpha]
in
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
.

(2)

There is a function
\Phi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that if
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
, then

\supp(f_{\#}(\sigma),i)\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(\supp(\sigma)).

Suppose also there exists some
[\tau]\in H_{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\lf}_{\bullet})
such that
\tau(\alpha)=1
. Then
X
is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
space.

Proof.

Since the proof is fairly long, we split it into three steps.

Step 1:
X
is coarsely uniformly acyclic over
R
.

We pick
\Phi_{0}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
has displacement
\Phi_{0}
. Fix a diagonal approximation
\Delta:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and choose
\Psi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
large enough so that Lemma
11.5
and Proposition
11.8
hold. Pick
\Omega:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
\Omega
-weakly uniformly acyclic.
Pick
j_{0}
large enough so that
\tau\in\mathbf{C}^{\lf}_{n}(j_{0})
, and set
j_{1}\coloneqq\Psi(j_{0})
. We define
g_{\#}:\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
by
g_{\#}(\alpha)=\tau\frown\alpha
. Since
\partial\tau=0
, Proposition
11.8
ensures that
g_{\#}
is a chain map.

We claim that the composition
g_{\#}f_{\#}
is an augmentation-preserving chain map of finite displacement over
\id_{X}
. Firstly, suppose
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{0}
.
Then using Proposition
11.8
and the hypotheses on
\tau
and on
f_{\#}
, we see

\displaystyle\epsilon(g_{\#}f_{\#}(\sigma))=\epsilon(\tau\frown f_{\#}(\sigma))=\tau(f_{\#}(\sigma))=\epsilon(\sigma)\tau(\alpha)=\epsilon(\sigma).

For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
, Proposition
11.8
implies:

•

g_{\#}f_{\#}(\sigma)\in\mathbf{C}_{k}(j_{1})
;

•

\supp(g_{\#}f_{\#}(\sigma))\subseteq N_{j_{1}}(\supp(f_{\#}\sigma,j_{1}))\subseteq N_{j_{2}}(\supp(\sigma))
,

where
j_{2}\coloneqq j_{1}+\Phi(j_{1})
.
This shows that
g_{\#}f_{\#}
has finite displacement. Proposition
6.12
now implies that there is finite-displacement chain homotopy
\id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{\#}}}{{\simeq}}g_{\#}f_{\#}
, which we may assume is of displacement
\Phi_{1}
.

We define

(1)

\lambda(i)\coloneqq\max(j_{1},\Phi_{1}(i))
and

(2)

\mu(i,r)\coloneqq\max(\Phi_{1}(i)+r,j_{2}+\Omega(i,r)+r)
.

We show that for each
x\in X
,
i,r\in\mathbb{N}
, and reduced cycle
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}(i,N_{r}(x))
, there is some
\omega\in\mathbf{C}_{k+1}(\lambda(i))
with
\supp(\omega)\subseteq N_{\mu(i,r)}(x)
such that
\partial\omega=\sigma
. By Lemma
6.4
and Proposition
9.3
, this will imply that
X
is uniformly acyclic.
We thus fix some reduced cycle
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}(i,N_{r}(x))
.
As
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
\Omega
-weakly uniformly acyclic, there exists some
\omega^{\prime}\in\mathbf{C}_{k+1}(\Omega(i,r),N_{\Omega(i,r)}(x))
with
\partial\omega^{\prime}=\sigma
.
We set
\omega\coloneqq g_{\#}f_{\#}\omega^{\prime}-h_{\#}\sigma
and first note that

\displaystyle\partial\omega=\partial g_{\#}f_{\#}\omega^{\prime}-\partial h_{\#}\sigma=g_{\#}f_{\#}\sigma-\partial h_{\#}\sigma=\sigma+h_{\#}\partial\sigma=\sigma.

By using the properties of
g_{\#}f_{\#}
described above, we have
g_{\#}f_{\#}(\omega^{\prime})\in\mathbf{C}_{k+1}(j_{1})\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{k+1}(\lambda(i))
and

\supp(g_{\#}f_{\#}(\omega^{\prime}))\subseteq N_{j_{2}}(\supp(\omega^{\prime}))\subseteq N_{j_{2}+\Omega(i,r)}(\supp(\sigma))\subseteq N_{\mu(i,r)}(x)

Moreover, as
h_{\#}
has displacement
\Phi_{1}
, we see
h_{\#}(\sigma)\in\mathbf{C}_{k+1}(\Phi_{1}(i))\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{k+1}(\lambda(i))
and

\supp(h_{\#}\sigma)\subseteq N_{\Phi_{1}(i)}(\supp(\sigma))\subseteq N_{\Phi_{1}(i)+r}(x)\subseteq N_{\mu(i,r)}(x).

We have thus shown
\partial\omega=\sigma
with
\omega\in\mathbf{C}_{k+1}(\lambda(i))
and
\supp(\omega)\subseteq N_{\mu(i,r)}(x)
as required. This implies
X
is coarsely uniformly acyclic over
R
.

Step 2: Reduction to the case
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
n
-dimensional and of finite height.

Applying Lemma
12.9
to the maps
f_{\#}
and
g_{\#}
, we conclude that
\ccd_{R}(X)\leq n
. By Proposition
9.6
, there exists an
n
-dimensional finite-height proper
R
-resolution
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
of
X
. We want to show all the hypotheses of the lemma we are trying to prove, which we assume hold for a projective
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
, also hold for
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
.

By Proposition
6.12
, there are finite displacement augmentation-preserving chain maps
l_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
and
m_{\#}:\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and chain homotopy equivalences
\id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{\#}}}{{\simeq}}m_{\#}l_{\#}
and
\id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{\#}}}{{\simeq}}l_{\#}m_{\#}
, and we assume all these maps have displacement
\Phi_{2}
. By increasing
\Phi_{2}
, we may assume
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
also have displacement
\Phi_{2}
.

We define
\hat{f}_{\#}:\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{D}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
to be the composition

\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}\xrightarrow{m_{\#}}\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\xrightarrow{f_{\#}}\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}\xrightarrow{m^{\#}_{c}}\mathbf{D}^{n-\bullet}_{c},

and define
[\hat{\alpha}]\coloneqq[m^{\#}_{c}\alpha]\in H^{n}(\mathbf{D}^{n}_{c})
.
For each
\sigma\in\mathbf{D}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle[\hat{f}_{\#}(\sigma)]=[m^{\#}_{c}f_{\#}m_{\#}\sigma]=m^{*}_{c}([f_{\#}m_{\#}\sigma])=m^{*}_{c}(\epsilon(m_{\#}\sigma)[\alpha])=\epsilon(\sigma)[\hat{\alpha}]

as required.
For each
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{D}(i)
, we apply Lemma
5.11
to deduce

\displaystyle\supp(\hat{f}_{\#}(\sigma),i)

\displaystyle=\supp(m^{\#}_{c}f_{\#}m_{\#}(\sigma),i)

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{\Phi_{2}^{2}(i)+\Phi_{2}(i)}(\supp(f_{\#}m_{\#}(\sigma),\Phi_{2}^{2}(i)))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{\Phi_{2}^{2}(i)+\Phi_{2}(i)+\Phi(\Phi_{2}^{2}(i))}(\supp(m_{\#}(\sigma)))

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{\hat{\Phi}(i)}(\supp(\sigma)),

where
\hat{\Phi}(i)\coloneqq\Phi_{2}^{2}(i)+2\Phi_{2}(i)+\Phi(\Phi_{2}^{2}(i))
. Moreover, setting
\hat{\tau}\coloneqq l^{\lf}_{\#}(\tau)
, the proof of Proposition
7.8
implies
\hat{\tau}(\hat{\alpha})=\tau(\alpha)=1
. Finally, since
m_{\#}
is a finite displacement chain homotopy equivalence, it induces isomorphism
H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\cong H^{k}(\mathbf{D}^{\bullet}_{c})
for each
k
, so that
H^{k}(\mathbf{D}^{\bullet}_{c})=0
for
k<n
. We have thus shown that all the hypotheses of the lemma hold after replacing
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
,
f_{\#}
,
[\alpha]
,
[\tau]
and
\Phi
with
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}
,
\hat{f}_{\#}
,
[\hat{\alpha}]
,
[\hat{\tau}]
and
\hat{\Phi}
.

Step 3: Proof in the case
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
n
-dimensional and of finite height.

By Step 2, it is sufficient to complete the proof when
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is
n
-dimensional and of finite height. We assume
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
satisfies these properties, and consider the chain map
g_{\#}:\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and the finite displacement chain homotopy
\id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{\#}}}{{\simeq}}g_{\#}f_{\#}
as in Step 1.
The maps
f_{\#}
and
g_{\#}
are shown in the commutative diagram in Figure
5
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}^{0}_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}^{n-1}_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

Figure 5.
A commutative diagram showing the chain maps
f_{\#}
and
g_{\#}
.

Since
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is a finite-height resolution, Proposition
5.20
implies
\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
is a finite-height
R
-chain complex over
X
. Thus Remark
5.22
, combined with the hypothesis on
f_{\#}
and Proposition
11.8
, ensures that
f_{\#}
and
g_{\#}
have finite displacement over
\id_{X}
. We thus have finite-displacement chain maps
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
and
g_{\#}:\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
, and a finite displacement chain homotopy
\id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\simeq}}g_{\#}f_{\#}
.
By Definition
12.1
, it remains to show there exists a finite displacement chain homotopy
\id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\simeq}}f_{\#}g_{\#}
. To do this, we will construct an augmentation
\kappa:\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}\to R
such that
f_{\#}
and
g_{\#}
are augmentation-preserving, and
(\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c},\kappa)
is a projective
R
-resolution over
X
. The result will then follow from Proposition
6.12
.

We first claim
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\cong R
and is generated by
[\alpha]
.
Proposition
5.20
implies
\mathbf{C}_{k}\cong(\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c})^{*}_{c}
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and that we can dualise
f_{\#}
,
g_{\#}
and
h_{\#}
to obtain finite displacement cochain maps

\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}\xrightarrow{g^{\#}_{c}}\mathbf{C}_{n-\bullet}\xrightarrow{f^{\#}_{c}}\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}

and a cochain homotopy
\id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h^{\#}_{c}}}{{\simeq}}{f^{\#}_{c}}{g^{\#}_{c}}
.
We now observe that:

•

The composition
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\xrightarrow{g^{*}_{c}}H_{0}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet})\xrightarrow{f^{*}_{c}}H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
is the identity map.

•

H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\neq 0
, since there exists
[\alpha]\in H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
and
[\tau]\in H_{n}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf})
with
\tau(\alpha)=1
.

•

H_{0}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet})\cong R
, since the projective
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is weakly uniformly acyclic.

These facts imply that
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\cong R
. Let
\beta\in\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}
be a cocycle such that
[\beta]
generates
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\cong R
. Thus there is some
r\in R
with
[\alpha]=r[\beta]
. Since
1=\tau(\alpha)=\tau(r\beta)=r\tau(\beta)
, we see that
r
is a unit, proving the claim.

We define
\kappa:\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}\to R
by
\kappa\coloneqq\epsilon\circ g_{\#}
, where
\epsilon:\mathbf{C}_{0}\to R
is the augmentation of
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
. We first observe that

\displaystyle(\kappa\circ\delta)(\mathbf{C}^{n-1}_{c})=(\epsilon\circ g_{\#}\circ\delta)(\mathbf{C}^{n-1}_{c})=(\epsilon\circ\partial\circ g_{\#})(\mathbf{C}^{n-1}_{c})=0

since
\epsilon\circ\partial=0
; thus
\kappa
is an augmentation of
\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
.
Pick now pick some
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{0}
with
\epsilon(\sigma)=1
, so that
[f_{\#}\sigma]=\epsilon(\sigma)[\alpha]=[\alpha]
. Thus

\kappa(\alpha)=\kappa(f_{\#}\sigma)=\epsilon(g_{\#}f_{\#}\sigma)=\epsilon(\sigma)=1

since
g_{\#}f_{\#}
is augmentation-preserving. Thus for each
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathbf{C}_{0}
, we have
\kappa(f_{\#}\sigma^{\prime})=\kappa(\epsilon(\sigma^{\prime})\alpha)=\epsilon(\sigma^{\prime})
, so that
f_{\#}
is augmentation-preserving. By the definition of
\kappa
, it is clear that
g_{\#}
is augmentation-preserving.

Since
(\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c},\kappa)
is an
R
-chain complex of projective
R
-modules over
X
by Proposition
5.20
, all that remains is to show
(\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c},\kappa)
is weakly uniformly acyclic.
Suppose
\beta\in\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}
satisfies
\kappa(\beta)=0
. As
\mathbf{C}^{n+1}_{c}=0
, we note that
\delta\beta=0
. Since
\kappa(\beta)=0
,
\kappa(\alpha)=1
and
[\alpha]
generates
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
, it follows that
[\beta]=0
, showing that
\im(\mathbf{C}^{n-1}_{c}\xrightarrow{\delta}\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c})=\ker(\kappa)
. Since
H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})=0
for
k<n
, this implies that:

0\to\mathbf{C}^{0}_{c}\to\cdots\to\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}\xrightarrow{\kappa}R\to 0

is exact. We know from Proposition
9.10
that the coboundary maps
\delta:\mathbf{C}^{k-1}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
have uniform preimages.
As
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
is weakly uniformly acyclic, there exists a constant
D\in\mathbb{N}
such that for each
x\in X
, there exists some
\sigma_{x}\in\mathbf{C}_{0}(D)
with
\supp(\sigma_{x})\subseteq N_{D}(x)
and
\epsilon(\sigma_{x})=1
. For each
x\in X
, set
\alpha_{x}\coloneqq f_{\#}(\sigma_{x})
.
Since
f_{\#}
is augmentation-preserving and of finite displacement,
there exists a constant
D^{\prime}
such that for all
x\in X
,
\kappa(\alpha_{x})=1
,
\supp(\alpha_{x})\subseteq N_{D^{\prime}}(x)
and
\alpha_{x}\in\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}(D^{\prime})
. By Proposition
6.7
,
(\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c},\kappa)
is weakly uniformly acyclic, and we are done.
∎

We now give conditions under which the hypothesis of Lemma
12.10
can be satisfied. In the case that we assume
X
satisfies appropriate finiteness conditions, we obtain the following:

Theorem 12.11
.

Let
R
be a PID and let
n\geq 1
. Suppose
X
is a coarsely homogeneous proper metric space that is coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic over
R
. Assume that:

(1)

H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;R)=0
for
k<n
.

(2)

H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(X;R)
is a non-zero finitely generated
R
-module.

Then
X
is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
space.

Proof.

By Proposition
9.3
, there exists a proper projective
R
-resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
over
X
such that
\mathbf{C}_{k}
has finite height for
k\leq n
. We pick
i_{0}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{k}(i_{0})=\mathbf{C}_{k}
for
k\leq n
. By Proposition
5.20
, for each
k\leq n
,
\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
is a projective
R
-module over
X
of height at most
i_{0}
. We first complete the proof under the assumption that
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\cong H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(X;R)
is a torsion-free
R
-module.

We proceed by showing that the hypotheses of Lemma
12.10
are satisfied. Since
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
is a finitely generated torsion-free module over a PID, it is free. Let
[\alpha]\in H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
be a non-zero primitive element of
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
, so that there is a homomorphism
\overline{\tau}:H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\to R
with
\overline{\tau}([\alpha])=1
. It thus follows from Theorem
9.14
that there exists some
[\tau]\in H_{n}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf})
with
\tau(\alpha)=1
. It remains to construct a map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
satisfying the hypotheses of Lemma
12.10
.

We consider
\mathbf{Z}^{n}_{c}\coloneqq\ker(\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}^{n+1}_{c})
and
\mathbf{B}^{n}_{c}=\im(\mathbf{C}^{n-1}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c})
as geometric submodules of
\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}
. Let
\mathbf{L}\coloneqq\mathbf{B}^{n}_{c}+R\alpha
be the geometric submodule of
\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}
generated by
\mathbf{B}^{n}_{c}
and the cyclic submodule
R\alpha
. Since
[\alpha]
is primitive in
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
, the quotient map
\mathbf{Z}^{n}_{c}\to\mathbf{Z}^{n}_{c}/\mathbf{B}^{n}_{c}=H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
restricts to a surjection
j:\mathbf{L}\to R
with
j(\alpha)=1
and
j(\mathbf{B}^{n}_{c})=0
. By construction, we have that
[\beta]=j(\beta)[\alpha]
for all
\beta\in\mathbf{L}
, and that

(12.1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}^{n-1}_{c}\xrightarrow{\delta}\mathbf{L}\xrightarrow{j}R\to 0

is exact.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}_{n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}^{0}_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}^{1}_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}^{n-1}_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

Figure 6.
A commutative diagram showing the map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
.

By Proposition
9.10
, there is a constant
D
such that for each
x\in X
, there is a cocycle
\alpha_{x}\in\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}
with
[\alpha_{x}]=[\alpha]
and
\supp(\alpha_{x})\subseteq N_{D}(x)
. In particular, we note that
\alpha_{x}\in\mathbf{L}
and
j(\alpha_{x})=1
. Since
\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}
has height at most
i_{0}
, we have
\alpha_{x}\in\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}(i_{0})
for all
x\in X
.
Suppose that
\mathbf{C}_{0}=(C_{0},B,\delta,p,\mathcal{F})
with projective basis
\{m_{b}\}
. We can therefore use Lemma
4.5
to construct a finite displacement map
f_{0}:\mathbf{C}_{0}\to\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}
such that
f_{0}(m_{b})=\epsilon(m_{b})\alpha_{p(b)}
for all
b\in B
. Since
\im(f)\subseteq\mathbf{L}
and
j(f_{0}(m_{b}))=\epsilon(m_{b})j(\alpha_{p(b)})=\epsilon(m_{b})
for all
b\in B
, it follows that
j\circ f_{0}
and
\epsilon
agree on
\mathbf{C}_{0}
.
Thus for each
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{0}
,

{[f_{0}(\sigma)]}=j(f_{0}(\sigma))[\alpha]=\epsilon(\sigma)[\alpha].

Therefore, the first condition in the statement of Lemma
12.10
is satisfied.

We now extend
f_{0}
to a chain map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
as shown in Figure
6
. Indeed, it follows from Proposition
9.10
that all the maps in the bottom row of Figure
6
have uniform preimages.
Since
j\circ f_{0}=\epsilon
and (
12.1
) is exact, it follows that
\im(\mathbf{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{0}\circ\partial}\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c})\subseteq\im(\mathbf{C}^{n-1}_{c}\xrightarrow{\delta}\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c})
. We can thus apply Proposition
4.6
to define a finite displacement map
f_{1}:\mathbf{C}_{1}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-1}_{c}
with
\delta f_{1}=f_{0}\partial
. Using the hypothesis
H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})=0
for
k<n
, we repeatedly apply Proposition
4.6
to define a finite displacement chain map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
as in Figure
6
.
Since
f_{\#}
is of finite displacement and each
\mathbf{C}_{k}
and
\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
has finite height for
k\leq n
, it follows from Remark
5.22
that
f_{\#}
satisfies the second condition in the statement of Lemma
12.10
. Thus all the hypotheses of Lemma
12.10
are satisfied, so we conclude that
X
is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
space. This completes the proof under the assumption
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
is assumed to be torsion-free. In particular, this completes the proof in the case
R
is a field.

We now assume for contradiction that
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
is not torsion-free. The structure theory for finitely generated modules over a PID implies
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
contains a direct summand isomorphic to
R/p^{j}R
for some
j\geq 1
and prime
p\in R
. We thus consider the field
\mathbb{F}\coloneqq R/pR
. Our choice of
p
ensures that
\Tor_{1}^{R}(H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}),\mathbb{F})
and
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}
are both non-zero, being the kernel and cokernel respectively of the map
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\xrightarrow{\times p}H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
.
Let
\mathbf{D}_{\bullet}=\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\otimes_{R}\mathbb{F}
, which is a proper projective
\mathbb{F}
-resolution over
X
by Proposition
6.28
. As
\mathbf{D}_{k}
has finite height for
k\leq n
by Proposition
4.16
, it follows that
X
is coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic over
\mathbb{F}
by Proposition
9.3
.

Since
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;\mathbb{F})\cong H^{k}(\mathbf{D}^{\bullet}_{c})
and
H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})=0
for
k<n
, Theorem
9.14
implies that
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;\mathbb{F})=0
for
k<n-1
, and
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n-1}(X;\mathbb{F})\cong\Tor_{1}^{R}(H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}),\mathbb{F})
is non-zero and finite-dimensional. As we have already completed the proof in the case where the coefficients are a field, we apply the result to conclude
X
is a coarse
PD_{n-1}^{\mathbb{F}}
space. In particular, this implies that
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(X;\mathbb{F})=0
by Proposition
12.4
. However, we know from Theorem
9.14
that
0\neq H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\otimes_{R}\mathbb{F}
is isomorphic to a submodule of
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(X;\mathbb{F})\cong H^{n}(\mathbf{D}^{\bullet}_{c})
, which yields the desired contradiction.
∎

Combining Theorem
12.11
with Proposition
12.4
, we conclude:

Corollary 12.12
.

Let
R
be a PID and let
n\geq 1
. Suppose
X
is a coarsely homogeneous proper metric space that is coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic over
R
. Assume that
H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{k}(X;R)=0
for
k<n
. Then either:

(1)

H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(X;R)=0
.

(2)

H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(X;R)\cong R
, in which case
X
is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
space.

(3)

H_{\textrm{coarse}}^{n}(X;R)
is not finitely generated as an
R
-module.

We would like to strengthen Theorem
12.11
by removing the hypothesis that
X
is coarsely uniformly
(n-1)
-acyclic. Unfortunately, one of the key points in the proof of Theorem
12.11
relies on the fact the coboundary maps
\delta:\mathbf{C}^{k-1}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
in the bottom row of Figure
6
have the uniform preimage property, and so Proposition
4.6
can be applied. In the case where the
\mathbf{C}_{k}
do not have finite height, it is not clear what the analogue of the uniform preimage property should be.

However, we can make progress if we restrict ourselves to the case where
X
is a countable group
G
equipped, as always, with a proper left-invariant metric. The key point is that we can choose
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
so the coboundary maps
\delta:\mathbf{C}^{k-1}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
are
G
-equivariant. We can then define a chain map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
as in the statement of Lemma
12.10
, by requiring that each
f_{\#}(\mathbf{C}_{k})
is contained in a finitely generated
G
-submodule of
\mathbf{C}^{n-k}_{c}
; see Proposition
12.14
for a precise statement. The condition that
\im(f_{\#})
be contained in a finitely generated
G
maps is a surrogate for the uniform preimage condition, and allows us to construct the desired map
f_{\#}
. This idea allows us to prove the following far-reaching generalisation of a result of Farrell, who proved a similar theorem in the case that
G
satisfies additional finiteness properties
[
Far75
, Theorem 1]
.

Theorem 12.13
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbb{F}
be a field and let
G
be a countable group. If
H^{k}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=0
for
k<n
, then exactly one of the following holds:

(1)

\dim_{\mathbb{F}}H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=0
.

(2)

\dim_{\mathbb{F}}H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=1
, in which case
G
is a coarse Poincaré duality group of dimension
n
over
\mathbb{F}
.

(3)

\dim_{\mathbb{F}}H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=\infty
.

Moreover, if
H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)
has countable dimension and a finite-dimensional
G
-invariant subspace, then
\dim_{\mathbb{F}}H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=1
.

Before proving Theorem
12.13
, we briefly recall some details regarding the standard resolution
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
of a countable group
G
. As noted in Corollary
6.22
, each
\mathbf{C}_{k}=(C_{k},B_{k},\delta^{k},p_{k},\{B_{k}(i)\})
is a proper
G
-induced
R
-module over
G
, and all boundary maps are
G
-invariant. We recall that
\mathbf{C}_{k}
is a free
R
-module over
G
with basis
B_{k}=\{[g_{0},\dots,g_{k}]\mid g_{0},\dots,g_{k}\in G\}
and
p_{k}([g_{0},\dots,g_{k}])=g_{0}
. Since the
G
-action on
B_{k}
is given by
g[g_{0},\dots,g_{k}]=[gg_{0},\dots,gg_{k}]
, we observe that

\Sigma_{k}=\{[g_{0},g_{1},\dots,g_{n}]\in B_{k}\mid g_{0}=1\}

is a basis of
\mathbf{C}_{k}
as an
RG
-module. Moreover, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Sigma_{k}(i)\coloneqq\Sigma_{k}\cap\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
is a finite basis of
\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
as an
RG
-module.

The left
G
-action on
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
satisfies
g\supp(\sigma)=\supp(g\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
and
g\in G
.
There is a right
G
-action on
\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c}
given by
(\alpha\cdot g)(\sigma)=\alpha(g\sigma)
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
,
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}
and
g\in G
. It is straightforward to verify that
\supp(\alpha\cdot g,i)=g^{-1}\supp(\alpha,i)
for all
i,k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}
and
g\in G
. Moreover, since the coboundary maps
\delta:\mathbf{C}^{k}_{c}\to\mathbf{C}^{k+1}_{c}
are
G
-equivariant, each cohomology module
H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
admits a right
G
-action given by
[\alpha]\cdot g=[\alpha\cdot g]
.

We can now formulate the new ingredient needed to prove Theorem
12.13
:

Proposition 12.14
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring and let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
G
be a countable group and let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be the standard
R
-resolution of
G
. Suppose
H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})=0
for
k<n
, and there exists a non-zero class
[\alpha]\in H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
contained in a
G
-invariant submodule
N\leq H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
that is finitely generated as an
R
-module. Then there exists a chain map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
such that:

(1)

For each
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{0}
,
[f_{\#}(\sigma)]=\epsilon(\sigma)[\alpha]
in
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
.

(2)

There is a function
\Phi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that if
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
, then

\supp(f_{\#}(\sigma),i)\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(\supp(\sigma)).

To prove Proposition
12.14
, we will make repeated use of the following lemma:

Lemma 12.15
.

Let
G
be a countable group and let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be the standard
R
-resolution over
G
.
Assume there is a homomorphism
f:\mathbf{C}_{k}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-k}_{c}
such that the following holds.
For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a finitely generated submodule
M_{i}\leq\mathbf{C}^{n-k}_{c}
such that for all
g\in G
,
f(g\Sigma_{k}(i))\subseteq M_{i}g^{-1}
.
Then:

(1)

f(\sigma)\subseteq\langle M_{i}\supp(\sigma)^{-1}\rangle
for all
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
, where
\langle M_{i}\supp(\sigma)^{-1}\rangle
is the
R
-submodule generated by
M_{i}\supp(\sigma)^{-1}
.

(2)

There exists a function
\Phi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that if
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
, then

\supp(f(\sigma),i)\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(\supp(\sigma)).

Proof.

(
1
): For each
b\in B_{k}(i)
we have
b\in p_{k}(b)\Sigma_{k}(i)
, and so
f(b)\in M_{i}p_{k}(b)^{-1}
. For
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
, we have
\sigma=\sum_{b\in B_{k}(i)}\delta_{b}(\sigma)b
. Since
\delta_{b}(\sigma)\neq 0
if and only if
p_{k}(b)\in\supp(\sigma)
, we have

f(\sigma)\in\sum_{p_{k}(b)\in\supp(\sigma)}M_{i}p_{k}(b)^{-1}=\langle M_{i}\supp(\sigma)^{-1}\rangle

as required.

(
2
):
Since each
M_{i}
is finitely generated, we can pick
\Phi
such that for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in M_{i}
,
\supp(\alpha,i)\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(1)
.
Thus for each
\alpha\in M_{i}g^{-1}
, we have
\supp(\alpha,i)\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(g)
. Consequently, for each
\alpha\in\langle M_{i}\supp(\sigma)^{-1}\rangle
, we have
\supp(\alpha,i)\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(\supp(\sigma))
. Combining this with (
1
) yields the desired conclusion.
∎

Proof of Propsotion
12.14
.

Pick
\Phi
such that
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
has displacement
\Phi
. We recall
\Sigma_{k}(i)
is a free
RG
-basis of each
\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
, and that
B_{k}(i)=G\Sigma_{k}(i)
is a free
R
-basis of each
\mathbf{C}_{k}(i)
. Moreover,
\epsilon(b)=1
for all
g\in G
and
b\in B_{0}(i)
.
We will define a chain map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
and a collection
\{M_{k,i}\}_{k,i\in\mathbb{N}}
, where each
M_{k,i}
is a finitely generated
R
-submodule of
\mathbf{C}^{n-k}_{c}
satisfying
f_{\#}(g\Sigma_{k}(i))\subseteq M_{k,i}g^{-1}
for all
k,i\in\mathbb{N}
and
g\in G
. Furthermore, we will also show that
[f_{\#}(\sigma)]=\epsilon(\sigma)[\alpha]
for all
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{0}
, and so applying Lemma
12.15
will prove the result.

We will define
f_{k}:\mathbf{C}_{k}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-k}_{c}
and
\{M_{k,i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
by induction on
k
.
First, we prove the base case
k=0
.
Let
[\alpha_{1}],\dots,[\alpha_{m}]
be a finite generating set of
N
.
For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let
M_{0,i}=\langle\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}\rangle
be the
R
-submodule of
\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}
generated by
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}
.
Since
N
is
G
-invariant, for each
g\in G
, the image of
M_{0,i}g^{-1}
in
H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
is equal to
N
. As
[\alpha]\in N
, for each
g\in G
, there is some
\alpha_{g}\in M_{0,i}g^{-1}
with
[\alpha_{g}]=[\alpha]
.

We note
\Sigma_{0}=\Sigma_{0}(i)=\{[1]\}
for all
i
, and
B_{0}=B_{0}(i)=G\Sigma_{0}(i)=\{[g]\mid g\in G\}
for all
i
.
We can thus define an
R
-module homomorphism
f_{0}:\mathbf{C}_{0}\to\mathbf{C}^{n}_{c}
by
f_{0}([g])=\alpha_{g}
for all
g\in G
. By construction, this map satisfies the property that

f_{\#}(g\Sigma_{0}(i))\subseteq M_{0,i}g^{-1}

for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
g\in G
.
Moreover, for each
\sigma\in\mathbf{C}_{0}
, we have
\sigma=\sum_{g\in G}\delta_{[g]}(\sigma)[g]
with
\epsilon(\sigma)=\sum_{g\in G}\delta_{[g]}(\sigma)
. Thus

\displaystyle[f_{\#}(\sigma)]=\sum_{g\in G}\delta_{[g]}(\sigma)[f_{\#}({[g]})]=\sum_{g\in G}\delta_{[g]}(\sigma)[\alpha_{g}]=\sum_{g\in G}\delta_{[g]}(\sigma)[\alpha]=\epsilon(\sigma)[\alpha]

as required. This proves the base case.

For inductive hypotheses, assume we have a chain map
f_{\#}:[\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}]_{k}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
and
\{M_{k,i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, where each
M_{k,i}
is finitely generated
R
-submodule of
\mathbf{C}^{n-k}_{c}
such that
f_{\#}(g\Sigma_{k}(i))\subseteq M_{k,i}g^{-1}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
g\in G
.
For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let
F_{i}\coloneqq N_{\Phi(i)}(1)\subseteq G
.
Since
F_{i}
is finite and
M_{k,i}
is finitely generated, so is
\langle M_{k,i}F_{i}^{-1}\rangle
. Since
R
is Noetherian,
T_{k,i}\coloneqq\im(\mathbf{C}^{n-k-1}_{c}\xrightarrow{\delta}\mathbf{C}^{n-k}_{c})\cap\langle M_{k,i}F_{i}^{-1}\rangle
is also finitely generated. Hence, we can pick a finitely generated submodule
M_{k+1,i}\subseteq\mathbf{C}^{n-k-1}_{c}
such that
\delta(M_{k+1,i})=T_{k,i}
.

We now fix some
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k+1}(i)
with
g\in\supp(\sigma)
. Since
\supp(g\sigma)=\{g\}
and
\partial
has displacement
\Phi
, we see
\supp(\partial g\sigma)\subseteq N_{\Phi(i)}(g)=gF_{i}
. By the inductive hypothesis and Lemma
12.15
, we deduce
f_{\#}(\partial g\sigma)\subseteq\langle M_{k,i}F_{i}^{-1}g^{-1}\rangle=\langle M_{k,i}F_{i}^{-1}\rangle g^{-1}
. We now show
f_{\#}(\partial g\sigma)
is a coboundary. Indeed, we first note that
\delta f_{\#}(\partial g\sigma)=f_{\#}(\partial^{2}g\sigma)=0
so that
f_{\#}(\partial g\sigma)
is a cocycle. Since
H^{j}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})=0
for
j<n
, we have
f_{\#}(\partial g\sigma)\in\im(\delta)
when
k>0
. In the case
k=0
, we have
[f_{\#}(\partial g\sigma)]=\epsilon(\partial g\sigma)[\alpha]=0
, again showing
f_{\#}(\partial g\sigma)
is a coboundary.

We have thus shown that for all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k+1}(i)
and
g\in G
,
f_{\#}(\partial g\sigma)\in\langle M_{k,i}F_{i}^{-1}\rangle g^{-1}
and
f_{\#}(\partial g\sigma)
is a coboundary, so that
f_{\#}(\partial b)\in T_{k,i}g^{-1}
. By the definition of
M_{k+1,i}
and
G
-equivariance of
\delta
, we can choose some
n_{g\sigma}\in M_{k+1,i}g^{-1}
with
\delta n_{g\sigma}=f_{\#}(\partial g\sigma)
for each
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k+1}(i)
and
g\in G
. Defining
f_{\#}(g\sigma)=n_{g\sigma}
allows us to extend
f_{\#}
to a map
f_{k+1}:\mathbf{C}_{k+1}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-k-1}_{c}
with

f_{\#}(g\Sigma_{k+1}(i))\subseteq M_{k+1,i}g^{-1}

for all
g\in G
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
. This proves the inductive step.
∎

Proof of Theorem
12.13
.

Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be the standard projective
\mathbb{F}
-resolution over
G
. By Corollary
8.9
, there is an isomorphism
H^{k}(G,\mathbb{F}G)\cong H^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
as right
RG
-modules. The hypotheses ensure there exists a non-zero class
[\alpha]\in H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})
contained in a non-zero finite-dimensional
G
-invariant subspace. By Theorem
7.10
, there exists an element
\tau\in H_{n}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf})
such that
\tau(\alpha)=1
. Moreover, we can apply Proposition
12.14
to construct a map
f_{\#}:\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{C}^{n-\bullet}_{c}
satisfying the hypotheses of Lemma
12.10
. We conclude by applying Lemma
12.10
to deduce
X
is a coarse
PD_{n}^{\mathbb{F}}
space as required.
∎

We now focus on the case the coefficient ring is not a field:

Theorem 12.16
.

Let
R
be a countable PID, let
G
be a countable group and let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose:

(1)

H^{k}(G,RG)=0
for
k<n
.

(2)

H^{n}(G,RG)
is a non-zero finitely generated
R
-module.

(3)

H^{n+1}(G,RG)
is a countably generated
R
-module.

Then
G
is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
group.

Remark 12.17
.

Theorem
12.11
shows that if
G
of type
FP_{n}^{R}
, we can remove the hypothesis that
H^{n+1}(G,RG)
is countably generated from the preceding theorem.

Proof.

We will use Corollary
8.9
implicitly throughout this proof.
Suppose for contradiction that
M\coloneqq H^{n}(G,RG)
contains torsion. As in the proof of Theorem
12.11
, we see that for some prime
p\in R
, both
\Tor_{1}^{R}(M,\mathbb{F})
and
M\otimes_{R}\mathbb{F}
are non-zero, where
\mathbb{F}
is the field
R/pR
. It follows from Theorem
7.10
and Corollary
8.9
that
H^{k}(G,\mathbb{F}G)=0
for
k<n-1
, that
H^{n-1}(G,\mathbb{F}G)\cong\Tor_{1}^{R}(M,\mathbb{F})
, and that
M\otimes_{R}\mathbb{F}
is a submodule of
H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)
. Since
H^{n-1}(G,\mathbb{F}G)\cong\Tor_{1}^{R}(M,\mathbb{F})
is finite dimensional as
M
is finitely generated, Theorem
12.13
implies
G
is a coarse
PD_{n-1}^{\mathbb{F}}
group. This contradicts Proposition
12.4
and the fact that
0\neq M\otimes_{R}\mathbb{F}
is a submodule of
H^{n}(G,\mathbb{F}G)
, so we conclude that
M
is torsion-free.

Since
M
is torsion-free and finitely generated, it is free. Let
\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}
be a proper projective
R
-resolution over
G
, and let
[\alpha]\in H^{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}_{c})\cong M
be a non-zero primitive element. By Theorem
7.10
, there is some class
[\tau]\in H_{n}(\mathbf{C}_{\bullet}^{\lf})
with
\tau(\alpha)=1
. We can thus apply Proposition
12.14
and Lemma
12.10
to conclude that
X
is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
space as required.
∎

This implies the following characterisation of coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
groups.

Corollary 12.18
.

Suppose
G
is a countable group,
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
R
is either a field or a countable PID. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

G
is a coarse
PD_{n}^{R}
group.

(2)

H^{k}(G;RG)\cong\begin{cases}R&\textrm{if}\;k=n,\\
0&\textrm{if}\;k\neq n.\end{cases}

Crucially, Corollary
12.18
does not assume
G
satisfies any finiteness conditions.

Appendix A
Some results about derived limits

In this appendix, we deduce some useful facts about inverse and derived limits. The key result is Corollary
A.9
, which states that under mild hypotheses, if
C^{\bullet}=\varprojlim C^{\bullet}_{i}
is an inverse limit and
H^{k}(C^{\bullet})
is countably generated for all
k
, then the inverse systems
\{H^{k}(C^{\bullet}_{i})\}_{i}
are stable.

In this section, we assume
R
is a PID.
We make use of the following facts about modules over
R
:

Lemma A.1

(
[
Rot15
, Theorem B-2.28]
)
.

Let
R
be a PID.

(1)

If
F
is a free
R
-module and
F^{\prime}\leq F
, then
F^{\prime}
is a free
R
-module with
\rank(F^{\prime})\leq\rank(F)
, where these ranks may be infinite cardinals.

(2)

Any submodule of a countably generated
R
-module is countably generated.

Proof.

(1) is
[
Rot15
, Theorem B-2.28]
. For (2), let
M
be a countable generated
R
-module, and let
\pi:F\to M
be a surjection, with
F
a countably generated free
R
-module. Then
F^{\prime}\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(N)
is a submodule of
F
with
\rank(F^{\prime})\leq\rank(F)
by (1). Thus
F^{\prime}
is countably generated, and since
\pi|_{F^{\prime}}:F^{\prime}\to N
is surjective, it follows that
N
is countably generated.
∎

We recall an
inverse system
consists of a sequence
\{M_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of
R
-modules equipped with
bonding maps

f_{i}^{j}:M_{j}\to M_{i}
for each
i\leq j
, satisfying the following:

•

f_{i}^{i}=\id

•

f_{i}^{j}\circ f_{j}^{k}=f_{i}^{k}
for all
i\leq j\leq k
.

The
Eilenberg map
of the inverse system
\{M_{i}\}
is the map
\Delta:\prod_{i}M_{i}\to\prod_{i}M_{i}
given by
(m_{i})\mapsto(m_{i}-f_{i}^{i+1}(m_{i+1}))
. We define the
inverse limit
of
\{M_{i}\}
to be
\varprojlim M_{i}\coloneqq\ker(\Delta)
and the
derived limit
to be
\varprojlim^{1}M_{i}\coloneqq\coker(\Delta)
. We note that there are
projections

f_{i}:\varprojlim M_{i}\to M_{i}
that commute with the bonding maps. It can be shown that
\varprojlim M_{i}
is characterised by the following
universal property
: If there exists an
R
-module
N
and a family of maps
\phi_{i}:N\to M_{i}
such that
f^{j}_{i}\circ\phi_{j}=\phi_{i}
for all
j\geq i
, then there is a unique map
\phi:N\to\varprojlim M_{i}
such that
f_{i}\circ\phi=\phi_{i}
for all
i
.

Suppose
0\to\{L_{i}\}\to\{M_{i}\}\to\{N_{i}\}\to 0
is a short exact sequence of inverse systems, i.e. for each
i
there is a short exact sequence
0\to L_{i}\to M_{i}\to N_{i}\to 0
, and these short exact sequence commute with the bonding maps. Applying the snake lemma yields the following
six-term short exact sequence
:

\displaystyle 0\to\varprojlim L_{i}\to\varprojlim M_{i}\to\varprojlim N_{i}\to\varprojlim{}^{1}L_{i}\to\varprojlim{}^{1}M_{i}\to\varprojlim{}^{1}N_{i}\to 0

An inverse system is said to satisfy the
Mittag-Leffler condition
if for each
i
, there exists
j\geq i
such that
\im(M_{j}\xrightarrow{f_{i}^{j}}M_{i})=\im(M_{k}\xrightarrow{f_{i}^{k}}M_{i})
for all
k\geq j
.

Proposition A.2
.

Assume
R
is either a field or a countable PID.
Let
\{M_{i}\}
be an inverse system of countably generated
R
-modules.

(1)

\varprojlim{}^{1}M_{i}=0
if and only if
\{M_{i}\}
satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition.

(2)

\varprojlim{}^{1}M_{i}
is either zero or an uncountably generated
R
-module.

(3)

If
\varprojlim{}M_{i}
is countably generated, then there exists an
i_{0}
such that the projection
f_{k}:\varprojlim{}M_{i}\to M_{k}
is injective for all
k\geq i_{0}
.

In order to prove Proposition
A.2
, we require two lemmas.
The following is a variant of a result of Gray
[
Gra66
]
:

Lemma A.3
.

Let
R
be as in Proposition
A.2
.
Let
M
be an
R
-module and let
M=M_{0}\supseteq M_{1}\supseteq M_{2}\supseteq\dots
be a descending sequence of submodules that does not stabilise, i.e. for each
j
,
\cap_{i}M_{i}\neq M_{j}
. Then
\varprojlim M/M_{i}
is an uncountably generated
R
-module.

Proof.

For each
i
, we consider the short exact sequence

1\to M_{i}/M_{i+1}\to M/M_{i+1}\to M/M_{i}\to 1.

In the case
R
is a field, the above short exact sequence splits as
M/M_{i+1}\cong M/M_{i}\oplus M_{i}/M_{i+1}
. This ensures that
\varprojlim M/M_{i}\cong\prod_{i}M_{i}/M_{i+1}
, which is uncountably generated as required.

In the case
R
is countable, we note that an
R
-module is countably generated if and only if it is countable. We can pick set-theoretic sections of the maps
M/M_{i+1}\to M/M_{i}
for each
i
, and hence identify
M/M_{i+1}
with
M/M_{i}\times M_{i}/M_{i+1}
as sets. Thus
\varprojlim M/M_{i}
can be identified with
\prod_{i}M_{i}/M_{i+1}
as sets, showing that
\varprojlim M/M_{i}
is an uncountable set, hence uncountably generated as an
R
-module.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

Let
R
be as in Proposition
A.2
.
If
\{M_{i}\}
is an inverse sequence of countably generated
R
-modules that does not satisfy the Mittag-Leffler condition, then
\varprojlim^{1}M_{i}
is an uncountably generated
R
-module, and in particular, is non-trivial.

Proof.

We assume for contradiction that
\{M_{i}\}
does not satisfy the Mittag-Leffler condition and
\varprojlim^{1}M_{i}
is countably generated. We can pick
i_{0}
such that
\im(M_{j}\to M_{i_{0}})\neq\im(M_{j+1}\to M_{i_{0}})
for infinitely many
j
. Set
W_{i}=\im(M_{i}\to M_{i_{0}})
. For each
i\geq i_{0}
, we have a short exact sequence
1\to K_{i}\to M_{i}\to W_{i}\to 1
, where
K_{i}=\ker(M_{i}\to M_{i_{0}})
.
By the six-term exact sequence, we have a surjection
\varprojlim^{1}M_{i}\to\varprojlim^{1}W_{i}
, hence
\varprojlim^{1}W_{i}
is also countably generated.

Let
W=W_{i_{0}}
and note that the sequence
W=W_{i_{0}}\supseteq W_{i_{0}+1}\supseteq\dots
does not stabilise by the choice of
i_{0}
. We now consider the short exact sequence
1\to\{W_{i}\}\to\{W\}\to\{W/W_{i}\}\to 1
. The six-term exact sequence yields an exact sequence
W\to\varprojlim W/W_{i}\to\varprojlim^{1}W_{i}\to 0
, noting that
\varprojlim^{1}W=0
as
\{W\}
is the constant inverse system. Since
W
and
\varprojlim^{1}W_{i}
are countably generated, so is
\varprojlim W/W_{i}
. This contradicts Lemma
A.3
and the choice of
i_{0}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
A.2
.

The “if” direction of (
1
) is well-known, see
[
Wei94
, Proposition 3.5.7]
, while the “only if” direction follows from Lemma
A.4
. We deduce (
2
) from (
1
) and Lemma
A.4
.
It remains to show (
3
).
Let
N=\varprojlim{}M_{i}
and let
f_{i}:N\to M_{i}
be the projection. Let
N_{i}\coloneq\ker(f_{i})
, so that
N_{0}\supseteq N_{1}\supseteq\cdots
is a descending chain of submodules of
N
.

The universal property of inverse limits implies there is an isomorphism
\pi:N\to\varprojlim N/N_{i}
such that postcomposing
\pi
with the projection to
N/N_{i}
is the quotient map.
Since we are assuming that
\varprojlim N/N_{i}\cong\varprojlim_{i}M_{i}
is countably generated, it follows from Lemma
A.3
that the sequence
(N_{i})
stabilises, i.e. there exists an
i_{0}
such that
N_{k}=N_{i_{0}}
for all
k\geq i_{0}
. Since
N=\varprojlim{}M_{i}=\varprojlim N/N_{i}=N/N_{i_{0}}
, it follows that
N_{i_{0}}=0
and thus
f_{k}:\varprojlim{}M_{i}\to M_{k}
is injective for all
k\geq i_{0}
.
∎

Definition A.5
.

An inverse system is said to be
stable
if it satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition and the projection
\varprojlim M_{i}\to M_{i}
is injective for
i
sufficiently large.

Stable inverse systems have the following characterisation, which makes them easy to work with:

Proposition A.6

(
[
Sta24
, Lemma 12.31.5.]
)
.

Let
\{M_{i}\}_{i}
be a stable inverse system of
R
-modules, and let
M=\varprojlim M_{i}
. Then there exists an
i_{0}
such that for all
i\geq i_{0}
, there is a direct sum decomposition
M_{i}\cong M\oplus Z_{i}
such that the following hold:

(1)

For all
j\geq i\geq i_{0}
, the bonding maps
f^{j}_{i}:M\oplus Z_{j}\to M\oplus Z_{i}
preserve the direct sum decomposition, and are of the form
f^{j}_{i}(m,z_{j})=(m,f^{j}_{i}(z_{j}))
for each
m\in M
and
z_{j}\in Z_{j}
.

(2)

For all
i\geq i_{0}
, there is some
j\geq i
such that
f^{j}_{i}(Z_{j})=0
.

(3)

Each projection
f_{i}:\varprojlim M_{i}\to M_{i}=M\oplus Z_{i}
is of the form
f_{j}(m)=(m,0)
.

Proof.

As
\{M_{i}\}
is stable, we can pick
k
large enough such that
f_{k}:M\to M_{k}
is injective. We pick
i_{0}\geq k
such that
\im(M_{i_{0}}\xrightarrow{f_{k}^{i_{0}}}M_{k})=\im(M_{i}\xrightarrow{f_{k}^{i}}M_{k})
for all
i\geq i_{0}
. Since
\im(f_{k})=\im(f_{k}^{i_{0}})
, there is homomorphism
\beta:M_{i_{0}}\to M
such that
f_{k}^{i_{0}}=f_{k}\circ\beta
.
For all
i\geq i_{0}
, we see that
f_{k}\beta f^{i}_{i_{0}}f_{i}=f_{k}
. Since
f_{k}=f_{k}^{i}f_{i}
is injective, we deduce
f_{i}\beta f^{i}_{i_{0}}:M_{i}\to M_{i}
is a projection onto
\im(f_{i})
. Therefore,
M_{i}=\im(f_{i})\oplus Z_{i}
, where
Z_{i}\coloneqq\ker(f_{i}\beta f^{i}_{i_{0}})
. Identifying
M
with
\im(f_{i})
via
f_{i}
, we have
M_{i}\cong M\oplus Z_{i}
and the bonding map respect these direct product decompositions. It is straightforward to verify each
f^{j}_{i}:M\oplus Z_{j}\to M\oplus Z_{i}
is of the form
(m,z_{j})=(m,f^{j}_{i}(z_{j}))
, and the projection
f_{i}:M\to M\oplus Z_{i}
is of the form
m\mapsto(m,0)
. Moreover, since
\{M_{i}\}
satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition, for each
i\geq i_{0}
there is some
j\geq i
with
\im(f_{i}^{j})=\im(f_{i})
. Thus
f_{i}^{j}(Z_{j})=0
as required.
∎

Corollary A.7
.

Let
\{M_{i}\}
be a stable inverse system of
R
-modules with inverse limit
M
. For each
R
-module
N
, the induced inverse systems
\{M_{i}\otimes_{R}N\}
and
\{\Tor_{1}^{R}(M_{i},N)\}
are stable with
\varprojlim{}(M_{i}\otimes_{R}N)=M\otimes_{R}N
and
\varprojlim{}(\Tor_{1}^{R}(M_{i},N))=\Tor_{1}^{R}(M,N)
.

Proof.

This follows from the isomorphisms
M_{i}\cong(\varprojlim M_{i})\oplus Z_{i}
from Proposition
A.6
and the fact that
-\otimes_{R}N
and
\Tor_{1}^{R}(-,N)
commute with direct sums.
∎

Under mild assumptions, the cohomology of an inverse limit of chain complexes can be computed using the Milnor exact sequence:

Proposition A.8
.

Let
\{C^{\bullet}_{i}\}_{i}
be an inverse system of chain complexes such that each
C^{\bullet}_{i}
is a chain complex of countably generated projective
R
-modules, and
\{C^{\bullet}_{i}\}
satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition. Let
C^{\bullet}=\varprojlim C^{\bullet}_{i}
.
For each
k
, there is a short exact sequence

0\to\varprojlim_{i}{}^{1}H^{k-1}(C_{i}^{\bullet})\to H^{k}(C^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\psi}\varprojlim_{i}{}H^{k}(C_{i}^{\bullet})\to 0.

Moreover, the projection
H^{k}(C^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\psi}\varprojlim_{i}{}H^{k}(C_{i}^{\bullet})\to H^{k}(C_{i}^{\bullet})
is induced by the projection
C^{\bullet}\to C^{\bullet}_{i}
.

Proof.

Since
\{C^{\bullet}_{i}\}_{i}
satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition, Proposition
A.2
implies the Eilenberg map
\Delta:\prod_{i}C^{\bullet}_{i}\to\prod_{i}C^{\bullet}_{i}
is surjective. As
\ker(\Delta)=C^{\bullet}
, this yields the short exact sequence

0\to C^{\bullet}\to\prod_{i}C^{\bullet}_{i}\xrightarrow{\Delta}\prod_{i}C^{\bullet}_{i}\to 0.

The corresponding long exact sequence in cohomology is

\cdots\prod_{i}H^{k-1}(C^{\bullet}_{i})\xrightarrow{\Delta^{k-1}}\prod_{i}H^{k-1}(C^{\bullet}_{i})\to H^{k}(C^{\bullet})\to\prod_{i}H^{k}(C^{\bullet}_{i})\xrightarrow{\Delta^{k}}\cdots,

where
\Delta^{*}
are the appropriate Eilenberg maps. The required short exact sequence follows from the observation that
\varprojlim_{i}{}^{1}H^{k-1}(C_{i}^{\bullet})\cong\coker(\Delta^{k-1})
and
\varprojlim_{i}H^{k}(C_{i}^{\bullet})\cong\ker(\Delta^{k})
.
∎

Combining Lemma
A.1
, Propositions
A.2
and
A.8
, we conclude the following:

Corollary A.9
.

Suppose that
\{C^{\bullet}_{i}\}_{i}
and
C^{\bullet}
are as in Proposition
A.8
and
R
is either a countable PID or a field. Suppose
H^{k}(C^{\bullet})
is countably generated for
k\leq n
. Then the inverse system
\{H^{k}(C^{\bullet}_{i})\}
is stable for each
k<n
.

Recall there is a dual notion of a direct system. A
direct system
consists of a sequence
\{M_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of
R
-modules equipped with
bonding maps

f_{i}^{j}:M_{i}\to M_{j}
for each
i\leq j
, satisfying the following:

•

f_{i}^{i}=\id
; and

•

f_{j}^{k}\circ f_{i}^{j}=f_{i}^{k}
for all
i\leq j\leq k
.

The
direct limit

\varinjlim M_{i}
is an
R
-module
M
equipped with
canonical maps

f_{i}:M_{i}\to M
such that
f_{i}=f_{j}\circ f_{i}^{j}
for all
i\leq j
, and satisfying the following universal property: If
N
is any
R
-module such that there are maps
g_{i}:M_{i}\to N
satisfying
g_{i}=g_{j}\circ f_{i}^{j}
for all
i\leq j
, there is a unique map
g:M\to N
such that
g_{i}=g\circ f_{i}
for all
i
.

If
\{M_{i}\}
is an
inverse
system of
R
-modules and
N
is an
R
-module, then applying the contravariant functors
\Hom_{R}(-,N)
and
\Ext_{R}^{1}(-,N)
to
\{M_{i}\}
yields the induced direct systems
\{\Hom_{R}(M_{i},N)\}
and
\{\Ext_{R}^{1}(M_{i},N)\}
. We have the following analogue of Corollary
A.7
:

Corollary A.10
.

Let
\{M_{i}\}
be a stable inverse system of
R
-modules with inverse limit
M
. Then the induced inverse systems
\{\Hom_{R}(M_{i},N)\}
and
\{\Ext_{R}^{1}(M_{i},N)\}
have direct limits
\varinjlim{}\Hom_{R}(M_{i},N)=\Hom_{R}(M,N)
and
\varinjlim{}\Ext_{R}^{1}(M_{i},N)=\Ext_{R}^{1}(M,N)
.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
A.6
and the fact that
\Hom_{R}(-,N)
and
\Ext_{R}^{1}(-,N)
commute with finite direct sums.
∎

Unlike inverse limits, direct limits of
R
-modules are exact: if
0\to\{L_{i}\}\to\{M_{i}\}\to\{N_{i}\}\to 0
is a short exact sequence of direct systems, then there is a short exact sequence
0\to\varinjlim L_{i}\to\varinjlim M_{i}\to\varinjlim N_{i}\to 0
. Moreover, homology commutes with direct limits: if
C_{\bullet}=\varinjlim_{i}C_{\bullet}^{i}
is a direct limit of chain complexes, then
H_{n}(C_{\bullet})=\varinjlim_{i}(H_{n}(C_{\bullet}^{i}))
.

Lemma A.11
.

Let
\{M_{i}\}
be an inverse sequence of countably generated
R
-modules and let
N
be an
R
-module.

(1)

There is a pairing

\Phi:\varinjlim\Hom_{R}(M_{i},N)\to\Hom(\varprojlim M_{i},N)

such that
\Phi(g_{i}(\alpha))(\sigma)=\alpha(f_{i}(\sigma))
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in\Hom_{R}(M_{i},N)
and
\sigma\in\varprojlim M_{i}
, where
f_{i}:\varprojlim M_{i}\to M_{i}
and
g_{i}:\Hom_{R}(M_{i},N)\to\varinjlim\Hom_{R}(M_{i},N)
are the canonical maps.

(2)

In the case where
R
is a field and
\varprojlim M_{i}
has countable dimension, then
\Phi
is surjective.

Proof.

(
1
): Let
f_{i}^{j}:M_{j}\to M_{i}
be the bonding maps of
\{M_{i}\}
. Since the maps
f_{i}^{*}:\Hom_{R}(M_{i},N)\to\Hom(\varprojlim M_{i},N)
dual to
f_{i}:\varprojlim M_{i}\to M_{i}
satisfy
f_{j}^{*}(f_{i}^{j})^{*}=f_{i}^{*}
for all
i\leq j
, the universal property of direct limits yields the required map
\Phi
.

(
2
): Since
\varprojlim M_{i}
has countable dimension, Proposition
A.2
implies there is some
j
such that
f_{j}:\varprojlim M_{i}\to M_{j}
is injective. Let
\phi\in\Hom(\varprojlim M_{i},N)
. Picking a complement of
\im(f_{j})
in
M_{j}
, we can define a map
\widetilde{\phi}:M_{j}\to N
such that
\widetilde{\phi}\circ f_{j}=\phi
. Thus we have
\Phi(g_{j}(\widetilde{\phi}))=\widetilde{\phi}\circ f_{j}=\phi
, showing
\Phi
is indeed surjective.
∎

We now prove versions of the Künneth and Universal Coefficient Theorems for inverse limits:

Theorem A.12
.

Let
R
be a PID and let
N
be an
R
-module.
Suppose that
\{C^{\bullet}_{i}\}_{i}
and
C^{\bullet}
are as in Proposition
A.8
. Assume also that each
C^{k}_{i}
is a free
R
-module.
Assume that for
k\leq n
,
\{H^{k}(C^{\bullet}_{i})\}
is a stable inverse sequence.

(1)

Let
D^{\bullet}_{i}\coloneqq C^{\bullet}_{i}\otimes_{R}N
for each
i
, and let
D^{\bullet}\coloneqq\varprojlim_{i}D^{\bullet}_{i}
.
For each
k<n
, we have a natural short exact sequence

0\to H^{k}(C^{\bullet})\otimes_{R}N\xrightarrow{\phi}H^{k}(D^{\bullet})\to\Tor_{1}^{R}(H^{k+1}(C^{\bullet}),S)\to 0,

and an injection
H^{n}(C^{\bullet})\otimes_{R}N\xrightarrow{\phi}H^{n}(D^{\bullet})
.
Moreover, the injections
\phi
are given by
[\alpha]\otimes s\mapsto[\alpha\otimes s]
.

(2)

Let
E_{\bullet}\coloneqq\varinjlim_{i}\Hom_{R}(C^{\bullet}_{i},N)
. For each
k<n
, we have a natural short exact sequence

0\to\Ext^{1}_{R}(H^{k+1}(C^{\bullet}),N)\to H_{k}(E_{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\psi}\Hom_{R}(H^{k}(C^{\bullet}),N)\to 0,

and a surjection
H_{n}(E_{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\psi}\Hom_{R}(H^{n}(C^{\bullet}),N)
. Moreover, these surjections
\psi
are given by the pairing in Lemma
A.11
.

(3)

In the case where
R
is a field and
H^{n+1}(C^{\bullet})
is countable dimensional, there is a surjection
H_{n+1}(E_{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\psi}\Hom_{R}(H^{n+1}(C^{\bullet}),N)
given by Lemma
A.11
.

Remark A.13
.

Corollary
A.7
yields many situations in which Theorem
A.12
can be applied.

Proof.

Propositions
A.2
and
A.8
imply that for
k\leq n+1
, we have
\varprojlim H^{k}(C^{\bullet}_{i})=H^{k}(C^{\bullet})
.

(
1
):
Since each
C^{\bullet}_{i}
is chain complex of free
R
-modules and
R
is a PID, we have short exact sequences

(A.1)

\displaystyle 0\to H^{k}(C^{\bullet}_{i})\otimes_{R}N\to H^{k}(D^{\bullet}_{i})\to\Tor_{1}^{R}(H^{k+1}(C^{\bullet}_{i}),N)\to 0

for each
k
, and these are natural in
C^{\bullet}_{i}
. If
k\leq n
, then Corollary
A.7
implies both
H^{k}(C^{\bullet}_{i})\otimes_{R}N
and
\Tor_{1}^{R}(H^{k}(C^{\bullet}_{i}),N)
are stable, with inverse limits
H^{k}(C^{\bullet})\otimes_{R}N
and
\Tor_{1}^{R}(H^{k}(C^{\bullet}),N)
respectively. Applying the six-term exact sequence to (
A.1
) for each
k<n
, we deduce that
\varprojlim^{1}H^{k}(D^{\bullet}_{i})=0
and there is a short exact sequence

(A.2)

\displaystyle 0\to H^{k}(C^{\bullet})\otimes_{R}N\to\varprojlim_{i}H^{k}(D^{\bullet}_{i})\to\Tor_{1}^{R}(H^{k+1}(C^{\bullet}),N)\to 0

for each
k<n
.
Propositions
A.2
and
A.8
then imply

\varprojlim_{i}H^{k}(D^{\bullet}_{i})\cong H^{k}(D^{\bullet})

for
k\leq n
. Combining this with (
A.2
) yields the desired short exact sequence.
Applying the six-term exact sequence to (
A.1
) with
k=n
yields an injection
H^{n}(C^{\bullet})\otimes_{R}N\to\varprojlim_{i}H^{n}(D^{\bullet}_{i})\cong H^{n}(D^{\bullet})
.

(
2
):
Since each
C^{\bullet}_{i}
is chain complex of free
R
-modules and
R
is a PID, we have short exact sequences

(A.3)

\displaystyle 0\to\Ext_{R}^{1}(H^{k+1}(C^{\bullet}_{i}),N)\to H_{k}(\Hom_{R}(C^{\bullet}_{i},N))\to\Hom_{R}(H^{k}(C^{\bullet}_{i}),N)\to 0

for each
k
.
Corollary
A.10
implies that for each
k\leq n
, the direct systems
\{\Ext_{R}^{1}(H^{k}(C^{\bullet}_{i}),N)\}
and
\{\Hom_{R}(H^{k}(C^{\bullet}_{i}),N)\}
are stable with direct limits
\Ext_{R}^{1}(H^{k}(C^{\bullet}),N)
and
\Hom_{R}(H^{k}(C^{\bullet}),S)
respectively.
Thus applying the exact functor
\varinjlim_{i}
to (
A.3
) with
k\leq n
, and using the fact the homology commutes with
\varinjlim_{i}
, we deduce that there is a short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to\varinjlim_{i}\Ext_{R}^{1}(H^{k+1}(C^{\bullet}_{i}),N)\to H_{k}(E_{\bullet})\to\Hom_{R}(H^{k}(C^{\bullet}),N)\to 0

for each
k\leq n
.
In the case
k<n
, we have

\varinjlim_{i}\Ext_{R}^{1}(H^{k+1}(C^{\bullet}_{i}),N)\cong\Ext_{R}^{1}(H^{k+1}(C^{\bullet}),N)

as required.

(
3
) is just a restatement of Lemma
A.11
in the present context.
∎
